2.5  Two-player zero-sum games

Although this document studies multiplayer games, we will sometimes use methods that bring us back to the more classical framework of two-player zero-sum games.
We will therefore need the following notions and results.

Definition

7

(Zero-sum game)
.

A game
\mathcal{G}
, with
\Pi=\{i,j\}
, is
zero-sum
if
\mu_{j}=-\mu_{i}
.

Most of the games we will consider, be they zero-sum or not, are
Borel games
.

Definition

8

(Borel game)
.

A game
\mathcal{G}
is
Borel
if the function
\mu
, from the set
V^{\omega}
equipped with the product topology to the Euclidian space
\mathbb{R}^{\Pi}
, is Borel, i.e., if for every Borel set
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{\Pi}
, the set
\mu^{-1}(B)
is Borel.

Two-players zero-sum Borel games have the following important property, called
determinacy
.

Lemma

1

(
[
BorelDeterminacy
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
be a zero-sum Borel game, with
\Pi=\{i,j\}
. Then, we have the following equality:

\supop_{\sigma_{i}}~\infop_{\sigma_{j}}~\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle=\infop_{\sigma_{j}}~\supop_{\sigma_{i}}~\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle.

That quantity is called
adversarial value
, or simply
value
, of the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, denoted by
\mathsf{val}_{i}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
.

Solving
the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
means computing its value.
The concept of value can also be extended to multiplayer games: when
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
is a multiplayer game and
i
is a player, the quantity
\mathsf{val}_{i}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
is the value of the game obtained by merging all the other players into one fictional
adversarial
player, i.e. a player whose payoff function would be the opposite of player
i
’s one.
In other words, the quantity
\mathsf{val}_{i}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
is the best payoff that player
i
can ensure in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, whatever the other players do.
When
\mathcal{G}
is clear from the context and when
v_{0}\in V_{i}
, we write
\mathsf{val}(v_{0})
for
\mathsf{val}_{i}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, and call it simply
value of the vertex

v_{0}
.
When it exists, a strategy that ensures the payoff
\mathsf{val}_{i}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
for player
i
is called
optimal
.
If
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
is a Boolean game and if
\mathsf{val}_{i}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}=1
, it will also be called a
winning
strategy.

Solving two-player zero-sum games will often be much simpler in games where stationary optimal strategies exist.
Let us give a sufficient condition to identify such games.

Definition

9

(Shuffling)
.

Let
\pi,\chi
and
\xi
be three plays in a game
\mathcal{G}
.
The play
\xi
is a
shuffling
of
\pi
and
\chi
if there exist two infinite sequences of indices
k_{0}<k_{1}<\dots
and
\ell_{0}<\ell_{1}<\dots
such that
\chi_{0}=\pi_{k_{0}}=\chi_{\ell_{0}}=\pi_{k_{1}}=\dots
, and:

\xi=\pi_{0}\dots\pi_{k_{0}-1}\chi_{0}\dots\chi_{\ell_{0}-1}\pi_{k_{0}}\dots\pi_{k_{1}-1}\chi_{\ell_{0}}\dots\chi_{\ell_{1}-1}\dots.

Definition

10

(Convexity, concavity)
.

A payoff function
\mu_{i}:\mathsf{Plays}\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
convex
if for every shuffling
\xi
of two plays
\pi
and
\chi
, we have
\mu_{i}(\xi)\geq\minop\{\mu_{i}(\pi),\mu_{i}(\chi)\}
.
It is
concave
if the function
-\mu_{i}
is convex.

Lemma

2
.

In a two-player zero-sum game played on a finite graph, every player whose payoff function is concave has an optimal strategy that is stationary.

Proof.

According to
[
DBLP:conf/icalp/Kopczynski06
]
, this result is true for Boolean objectives.
It follows that for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
, if a player
i
, whose payoff function is concave, has a strategy that ensures
\mu_{i}(\pi)\geq x
(understood as a Boolean objective), then they have a stationary one.
Hence the equality:

\mathsf{val}_{i}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}=\supop_{\sigma_{i}\in\mathsf{Sta}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}}~\infop_{\sigma_{j}}~\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle.

Since the underlying graph
(V,E)
is assumed to be finite, there exists a finite number of stationary strategies, hence there exists a stationary strategy
\sigma_{i}
that realizes the infimum above.
∎

2.6  Equilibria and constrained existence problem

In a game, among the strategy profiles that are available for all players, we will be interested in specific classes of strategy profiles that can be considered as more stable than others, because of guarantees they maintain when players deviate from their strategies.
Such classes of strategy profiles are usually called
equilibria
.
Our contribution focuses mostly on
Nash equilibria
and
subgame-perfect equilibria
, and later on
strong secure equilibria
and
risk-sensitive equilibria
.
We will also study those notions in different classes of games.

However, the question that we ask will almost always be the same: does there exist an equilibrium, in a given game, that generates a payoff in a given interval for each player?
We give here a formal definition of that problem for some class
\mathcal{C}
of games, and for some general class
\mathcal{E}
of strategy profiles, that must be understood as some notion of equilibrium.

Problem

1

(Constrained existence problem)
.

Given a game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\in\mathcal{C}
and two vectors
\bar{x},\bar{y}\in(\mathbb{Q}\cup\{\pm\infty\})^{\Pi}
, does there exist a strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}\in\mathcal{E}
such that the inequality
\bar{x}\leq\mu\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle\leq\bar{y}
holds?

The quantities
x_{i}
are called
lower thresholds
.
They are said to be
effective
when they are different from
-\infty
.
Similarly, the quantities
y_{i}
are called
upper thresholds
, and are considered
effective
when they are different from
+\infty
.
Note that thresholds are assumed to be rational, even in games where payoffs may not be, so that they can be represented using a finite number of bits.
Most of this document is dedicated to studying the complexity of this problem, for various equilibrium notions
\mathcal{E}
and game classes
\mathcal{C}
.

PART II:

NASH EQUILIBRIA

Chapter 3:  Nash equilibria

The concept of
Nash equilibria
, though under a different name, appears to have first been introduced by the French mathematician Antoine-Augustin Cournot
[
cournot1838recherches
]
.
Later, John Nash provided a more general definition
[
nash1951noncooperative
]
in a setting quite different from ours, as he was not considering graph games but matrix games, in the sense of
section

1.4
.
In such games, he proved that what is now known as a
Nash equilibrium
always exists, provided that players are allowed to
randomize
over multiple actions—a behavior that is implicitly prohibited in our formalism.
This result is now widely recognized as
Nash’s theorem
.

Both Cournot’s and Nash’s work were motivated by economics, where their insights remain extensively used today.
For instance, if a strategy profile represents the behavior of competing firms, a Nash equilibrium corresponds to a stable situation: if those companies want to coordinate but do not trust each other, following an agreement that constitutes a Nash equilibrium ensures that no company will have an incentive to deviate from that agreement.

As multiplayer games became a topic of interest in computer science, researchers naturally focused on this notion.
The complexity of the constrained existence problem of Nash equilibria was therefore already known for several of the most classical classes of games that we study here.
We use however this chapter to present some of those results (
1
and
2
), prove new ones (
3
,
4
and
6
), and introduce the classes of games that we will also study in
part

III
.

3.1  Definition

Nash equilibria are strategy profiles from which no player can increase their payoff by deviating unilaterally.

Definition

11

(Nash equilibrium)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
be a game.
A strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is a
Nash equilibrium
, or
NE
for short, in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
if and only if for each player
i
and for every deviation
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
for player
i
, we have the inequality
\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle\leq\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
.

When the strategy
{\bar{\sigma}}
is
not
an NE, we call the deviations
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
that do not observe the inequality above
profitable
deviations.

3.2  Parity games

Let us consider a system required to ensure a given condition, typically called a
specification
, over the long run.
A concrete example would be a robot tasked with regularly delivering parcels arriving at a warehouse to an apartment block.

As a first abstraction, we assume that the robot has infinite carrying capacity.
At any given moment, it therefore has two available actions: either do nothing, or take all the parcels in the warehouse and deliver them to the apartment block.
A second abstraction consists in disregarding the delivery time, as long as every parcel that appears in the warehouse is eventually delivered.
A third abstraction, which is central to all our formalisms, is to assume that the robot operates indefinitely and can thus plan its strategy over an infinite horizon.

The company managing the robot and the warehouse may then choose to care about their customers only as long as they continue placing orders: if they stop ordering, delivering the remaining parcels is no longer necessary.
In this framework, the robot’s objective can be expressed in a simple way: if parcels appear in the warehouse infinitely often, then the robot must infinitely often carry them to the apartment block.

Now, let us extend this setting by introducing a castle, which may also receive deliveries.
Since the castle owner has subscribed to a premium service, deliveries to the castle take precedence over those to the apartment block.
The robot’s objective then becomes the following: if parcels for the castle appear in the warehouse infinitely often, the robot must infinitely often deliver them to the castle.
Otherwise, and only in that case, it must fulfill the previously defined objective.

We observe that the robot’s objective is defined using
priorities
among different possible situations.
A natural and elegant way to formalize such an objective is through a
parity
condition, a concept widely used in automata theory.
Indeed, it is well known that
\omega
-regular languages are precisely those recognized by a non-deterministic automaton whose acceptance condition is a parity condition.

3.2.1 Definition

Given a graph
(V,E)
, a
color mapping
on
(V,E)
is a mapping
\kappa:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
.

Definition

12

(Parity)
.

The game
\mathcal{G}
is a
parity game
if for each player
i
, there exists a color mapping
\kappa_{i}
on the underlying graph of
\mathcal{G}
such that for every play
\pi
, we have
\mu_{i}(\pi)=1
if the color
\minop_{v\in\mathsf{Inf}(\pi)}\kappa_{i}(v)
is even, and
\mu_{i}(\pi)=0
if it is odd.

A
Büchi game
is a parity game in which all colors are
0
or
1
: then, each player’s objective consists simply in visiting infinitely often vertices of color
0
.
Dually, a
co-Büchi game
is a parity game in which all colors are
1
and
2
: then, each player’s objective consists in, eventually, avoiding vertices of color
1
.

3.2.2 Results

The constrained existence problem of Nash equilibria in parity games has already been studied by Michael Ummels.

Theorem

1

(
[
DBLP:conf/fossacs/Ummels08
]
)
.

The constrained existence problem of Nash equilibria in parity games is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete.
Hardness still holds in co-Büchi games, and when there is no effective lower threshold, and only one effective upper threshold.
In Büchi games, the problem is in
\mathsf{P}
.

Actually, in the cited article, Michael Ummels shows hardness with one effective lower (and not upper) threshold, but the result given here can be quickly showed by adding one player to his reduction—and we will need that result under this form in
chapter

8
.

3.3  Mean-payoff games

Imagine a system designed to generate a specific resource—an automated solar plant, for example, that produces green hydrogen. The plant’s objective would not be to maintain continuous hydrogen production. At times, such as when there is no sunlight, production may halt, but that downtime could be used for maintenance, like cleaning the equipment.
If the plant is located outside Belgium, where long periods of sunlight are possible, maintenance would still be necessary, even if it temporarily reduces production. What matters, however, is the
average
production over time—periods of peak output can compensate for necessary pauses.
Similarly, an initially long phase of low or no production may not be an issue if it ultimately leads to higher efficiency in the long run. The key concept here is the
asymptotic average
production, which is precisely what
mean-payoff objectives
capture.

3.3.1 Definition

Contrary to parity games, mean-payoff games are typically
quantitative games
.
The players receive a reward at each turn, and they aim at maximizing their limit average reward.

Given a graph
(V,E)
, a
reward function
on
(V,E)
is a mapping
r:E\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}
.

Definition

13

(Mean-payoff)
.

In a graph
(V,E)
, we define for each reward mapping
r
the
mean-payoff function

\mathsf{mp}_{r}:h_{0}\dots h_{n}\mapstochar\rightarrow\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k}r\left(h_{k}h_{k+1}\right)
.
Then, we write

\text{\lx@text@underline{\smash{$\mathsf{mp}$}}}_{r}(\pi)=\liminfop_{n}\mathsf{mp}_{r}(\pi_{\leq n}).

The game
\mathcal{G}
is a
mean-payoff game
if there exists a tuple
(r_{i})_{i\in\Pi}
of reward mappings such that for each player
i
, we have
\mu_{i}=\text{\lx@text@underline{\smash{$\mathsf{mp}$}}}_{r_{i}}
.

When the context is clear, we write
\mathsf{mp}_{i}
for
\mathsf{mp}_{r_{i}}
, and
\text{\lx@text@underline{\smash{$\mathsf{mp}$}}}_{i}
for
\text{\lx@text@underline{\smash{$\mathsf{mp}$}}}_{r_{i}}
.
We then also write
\mathsf{mp}(\pi)
for the tuple
(\mathsf{mp}_{i}(\pi))_{i\in\Pi}
.

3.3.2 Results

Again, the constrained existence problem of Nash equilibria in mean-payoff games has already been studied by Michael Ummels.

Theorem

2

(
[
Ummels2011
]
)
.

The constrained existence problem of Nash equilibria in mean-payoff games is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete.
Hardness still holds when all rewards are
0
and
1
, and when there is no effective lower threshold and only one effective upper threshold.

Again, Michael Ummel’s proved hardness with one lower threshold, but a quick modification of his proof gives us the desired result.

3.4  Discounted-sum games

Let us return to our example of an automated solar plant producing hydrogen, which we used to illustrate mean-payoff games.
The assumption that only the
asymptotic
average reward matters may seem bold: in practice, as time passes, uncertainties grow, and one might prefer to secure reasonable short-term rewards rather than rely on promises of greater gains in the distant future:
a bird in the hand is worth two in the bush.

This idea is captured by
discounted-sum
objectives.

3.4.1 Definition

Definition

14

(Discounted-sum)
.

In a graph
(V,E)
, we define for each reward mapping
r
and each
discount factor

\beta\in(0,1)
the
discounted-sum function

\mathsf{ds}_{r}^{\beta}:h\mapstochar\rightarrow\sum_{k}\beta^{k}r(h_{k}h_{k+1})
.
Then, we write

\mathsf{ds}^{\beta}_{r}(\pi)=\limop_{n}\mathsf{ds}^{\beta}_{r}(\pi_{\leq n}).

The game
\mathcal{G}
is a
discounted-sum game
if there exists a discount factor
\beta\in(0,1)\cap\mathbb{Q}
and a tuple
(r_{i})_{i\in\Pi}
of reward mappings such that for each
i
and every
\pi
, we have
\mu_{i}(\pi)=\mathsf{ds}^{\beta}_{r_{i}}(\pi)
.
When the context is clear, we write
\mathsf{ds}_{i}
for
\mathsf{ds}_{r_{i}}^{\beta}
.

3.4.2 The target discounted-sum problem

Discounted-sum objectives are closely related to the following problem, which is as simple to state as it is difficult to analyze.

Problem

2

(Target Discounted-Sum Problem)
.

Given four quantities
\beta,a,b,t\in\mathbb{Q}
with
0<\beta<1
, is there a sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\{a,b\}^{\omega}
such that
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}u_{n}\beta^{n}=t
?

Although this problem naturally arises in various fields, the Target Discounted-Sum (
TDS
) problem is surprisingly difficult to solve, and its decidability remains an open question.
For further details, the interested reader may refer to
[
DBLP:conf/lics/BokerHO15
]
.
We will now show that the problem we wish to study is at least as challenging.

3.4.3 Hardness result

Theorem

3
.

The TDS problem reduces to the constrained existence problem of NEs in discounted-sum games.
This hardness result still holds when there is no effective lower threshold, and only one effective upper threshold.

Proof.

Let
a,b,t\in\mathbb{Q}
and let
\beta\in(0,1)\cap\mathbb{Q}
form an instance of the TDS problem.
We construct from those inputs a discounted-sum game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright a}
, depicted by Figure
7
, with discount factor
\beta
.
Player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
’s rewards are zero on all edges.
In that game, we claim that there exists an NE
{\bar{\sigma}}
with
t\leq\mu_{\Box}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle\leq t
, if and only if
a,b,t
, and
\beta
form a positive instance of the TDS problem.

–

Indeed, if there exists an NE
{\bar{\sigma}}
satisfying
t\leq\mu_{\Box}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle\leq t
, i.e.
\mu_{\Box}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle=t
, let
\pi=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
.
Then, the sequence
(u_{n})_{n}=(\pi_{n})_{n}
is such that
\sum_{n}u_{n}\beta^{n}=\mu_{\Box}(\pi)=t
.

–

Conversely, let us note that player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
is the only player who actually makes choices, and that her payoff is
0
in every play.
Consequently, every strategy profile in that game is an NE.
Thus, if there exists a sequence
(u_{n})_{n}\in\{a,b\}^{\mathbb{N}}
with
\sum_{n}u_{n}\beta^{n}=t
, then that sequence also defines a play
\pi
with
\mu_{\Box}(\pi)=t
, and every strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
with
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle=\pi
is an NE.

To establish hardness even with only one effective upper threshold, we can extend this reasoning to the slightly more involved construction depicted by
Figure

8
.
∎

a
b
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{b}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{a}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{b}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{a}}

Figure 7
:
A game constructed from an instance of the TDS problem

v_{0}
v_{1}
v_{2}
v_{3}

b
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{-1}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{b}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\diamond}}{{-b}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{-1}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{a}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\diamond}}{{-a}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{-1}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{b}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\diamond}}{{-b}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{-1}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{a}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\diamond}}{{-a}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{t\lambda(1-\lambda)}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\diamond}}{{-t(1-\lambda)}}

Figure 8
:
Another game constructed from the same instance of
TDS

3.4.4 Co-recursive enumerability

The previous theorem suggests that designing algorithms to solve these problems lies beyond the scope of this thesis.
However, we will now show that, like the TDS problem, our problem is co-recursively enumerable.
The key idea is as follows: a fundamental property of discounted-sum objectives is that when a play yields a payoff outside a given interval for some player, there exists a prefix of that play after which the gap becomes irrecoverable. Beyond this point, we can stop reading the play and confidently conclude that the payoff will fall outside the interval.
Therefore, although strategy profiles are generally infinite objects and exist in uncountably many variations, profitable deviations can be identified by analyzing their behavior over a finite (though unbounded) number of histories.

Theorem

4
.

The constrained existence problem of NEs in discounted-sum games is co-recursively enumerable.

Proof.

We present here a semi-algorithm that recognizes negative instances of our problem.

Given a discounted-sum game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
with discount factor
\beta
, a player
i
and two threshold vectors
\bar{x}
and
\bar{y}
, we give an algorithm that stops if and only if there exists no NE
{\bar{\sigma}}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
with
\bar{x}\leq\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle\leq\bar{y}
.
But first, let us give a preliminary result that will justify the correctness of our result.

\blacktriangleright
Preliminary result

We show here the following lemma.

Lemma

3
.

For every play
\pi
, each player
j
and every index
n
, we have:

\mu_{j}(\pi)\in\left[\mathsf{ds}_{j}\left(\pi_{\leq n}\right)-M\beta^{n},\mathsf{ds}_{j}\left(\pi_{\leq n}\right)+M\beta^{n}\right],

where:

M=\frac{1}{1-\beta}\maxop_{uv\in E}~\maxop_{j\in\Pi}~|r_{j}(uv)|

is a bound on the payoff (in absolute value) of every player.

Proof.

Let us proceed by induction on
n
.

Base case.

When
n=0
, for every play
\pi
starting from
v_{0}
and each player
j
, we have:

\displaystyle\mu_{j}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}r_{j}(\pi_{k}\pi_{k+1})\beta^{k}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k}\beta^{k}\maxop_{uv\in E}\maxop_{j\in\Pi}|r_{j}(uv)|=M

and symetrically
\mu_{j}(\pi)\geq-M
, which constitutes the desired interval since
\mathsf{ds}_{i}(\pi_{0})=0
.

Inductive case.

Now, if the desired property is true for
n\geq 0
, let us prove that it is true for
n+1
.
Let
v\pi
be a play.
By induction hypothesis, we have
\mu_{j}(\pi)\in\left[\mathsf{ds}_{j}(\pi_{\leq n})-M\beta^{n},\mathsf{ds}_{j}(\pi_{\leq n})+M\beta^{n}\right].

Hence:

\displaystyle\mu_{j}(v\pi)

\displaystyle=r_{j}(v\pi_{0})+\sum_{k}r_{j}(\pi_{k}\pi_{k+1})\beta^{k+1}

\displaystyle=r_{j}(v\pi_{0})+\beta\mu_{j}(\pi)

\displaystyle\geq r_{j}(v\pi_{0})+\beta\mathsf{ds}_{j}(\pi_{\leq n})-M\beta^{n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ds}_{j}(v_{0}\pi_{\leq n})-M\beta^{n+1},

and analogously for the upper bound.
∎

\blacktriangleright
Algorithm

Let
\left(h^{(n)}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a recursive enumeration of the nonempty histories in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
by increasing order of lengths (we can, for example, order histories of the same length with the lexicographic order induced by some arbitrary order on vertices).

Now, let
T
be the infinite tree whose nodes of depth
n+1
are all possible
n
-uples
\left({\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(0)}),\dots,{\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(n)})\right)
, and where the children of the node
\left({\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(0)}),\dots,{\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(n)})\right)
are the nodes of the form:

\left({\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(0)}),\dots,{\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(n)}),{\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(n+1)})\right).

Thus, every node partially defines a complete strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
, and every infinite branch entirely defines it—and conversely, every complete strategy profile is defined by an infinite branch.

A node
\left({\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(0)}),\dots,{\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(n)})\right)
is called
Nash-irrational
if there exist two indices
\ell,m\leq n
, with
|h^{(\ell)}|=|h^{(m)}|=p
, an index
k\leq p
and a player
i
such that:

–

we have
h^{(\ell)}_{\leq k}=h^{(m)}_{\leq k}
;

–

the history
h^{(\ell)}
is compatible with the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
that is partially defined by the node;

–

the history
h^{(m)}
is compatible with the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
;

–

and finally, we have:

\mathsf{ds}_{i}\left(h^{(m)}\right)-\mathsf{ds}_{i}\left(h^{(\ell)}\right)>2M\beta^{p-1}.

The same node is called
off-topic
if for some player
i
we have
\mathsf{ds}_{i}\left(h\right)>y_{i}+M\beta^{|h|-1}
, or
\mathsf{ds}_{i}\left(h\right)<x_{i}-M\beta^{|h|-1}

where
h
is the longest history that is compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}
as partially defined by the node.

Our algorithm consists in constructing the tree
T^{\prime}
, obtained from the tree
T
by cutting every branch after the first Nash-irrational or off-topic node, and declaring the instance to be negative once that construction is finished.

\blacktriangleright
Correctness

To show that our algorithm is correct, we must prove that the tree
T^{\prime}
will be finite if and only if we have a negative instance of the constrained existence problem.
By Kőnig’s lemma, that will be the case if and only if every branch is finite, i.e., if and and only if every branch of the tree
T
contains either an off-topic or a Nash-irrational node.
Therefore, we will be done if we prove that, given a branch of
T
and the corresponding strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
, the branch contains no such node if and only if the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is an NE and satisfies
\bar{x}\leq\mu\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle\leq\bar{y}
.

Off-topic nodes.

Using
3
, we know that we have
\bar{x}\leq\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle\leq\bar{y}
if and only if the corresponding branch contains no off-topic node.

If the branch contains a Nash-irrational node, then the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is not an NE.

If the branch contains a Nash-irrational node
\left({\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(0)}),\dots,{\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(n)})\right)
.
Let us use the notations
k,\ell,m,
and
i
from the definition of Nash-irrationality.
Let us define
\pi=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
: note that the history
h^{(\ell)}
is the prefix of length
p
of the play
\pi
.
Similarly, let us extend the history
h^{(m)}
into some play
\pi^{\prime}
, compatible with the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
.
By
3
, the inequality:

\mathsf{ds}_{i}\left(h^{(m)}\right)-\mathsf{ds}_{i}\left(h^{(\ell)}\right)>2M\beta^{p-1}

implies
\mu_{j}(\pi^{\prime})>\mu_{j}(\pi)
, and therefore the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is not an NE.

If the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is not an NE, then the branch contains a Nash-irrational node.

If
{\bar{\sigma}}
is not an NE, then there exists a player
i
and a strategy
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
such that we have:

\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle<\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle.

Then, let
\pi=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
, and let
\pi^{\prime}=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle
: since
\mu_{i}(\pi)<\mu_{i}(\pi^{\prime})
, by
3
, there exists an index
k
such that:

\mathsf{ds}_{i}\left(\pi_{<k}\right)+M\beta^{k-1}<\mathsf{ds}_{i}\left(\pi^{\prime}_{<k}\right)-M\beta^{k-1}.

Let now
\ell
and
m
be the indices such that
h^{(\ell)}=\pi_{<k}
, and
h^{(m)}=\pi^{\prime}_{<k}
.
Then, we have
\mathsf{ds}_{j}\left(h^{(m)}\right)-\mathsf{ds}_{j}\left(h^{(\ell)}\right)>2M\beta^{k-1}
: along the branch corresponding to
{\bar{\sigma}}
, the node of depth
\maxop\{\ell,m\}
is Nash-irrational.
Which ends the proof.
∎

3.5  Energy games

Let us revisit the example of our green hydrogen plant, but now assume that it is connected to another system that consumes the hydrogen. For instance, the delivery robot from
section

3.2
, which arrives whenever necessary to collect fuel from a tank where the station stores the hydrogen it produces.
The plant’s goal is no longer to maximize hydrogen production, which would after all be a rather productivist objective.
Instead, its aim is to ensure that the robot will always find the required amount of fuel available—in other words, to avoid a situation in which the robot would attempt to take more fuel than what is stored in the tank.
Such scenarios are modeled with
energy objectives
.

3.5.1 Definition

Like mean-payoff and discounted-sum objectives, energy objectives are based on reward functions.
However, energy objectives are Boolean.

Definition

15

(Energy)
.

In a graph
(V,E)
, we associate to each reward mapping
r
the
energy level function

\mathsf{el}_{r}
that maps a finite path
h=h_{0}\dots h_{n}
to the element of
\mathbb{N}\cup\{\bot\}
defined as follows.

•

If the path has length
1
, we define
\mathsf{el}_{r}(h_{0})=0
.

•

Otherwise, if we have
\mathsf{el}_{r}(h_{\leq n})\neq\bot
and
\mathsf{el}_{r}(h_{\leq n})+r(h_{n}h_{n+1})\geq 0
, we define
\mathsf{el}_{r}(h_{\leq n+1})=\mathsf{el}_{r}(h_{\leq n})+r(h_{n}h_{n+1})
.

•

In every other case, we define
\mathsf{el}_{r}(h_{\leq n+1})=\bot
.

The game
\mathcal{G}
is an
energy game
if there exists a tuple
(r_{i})_{i\in\Pi}
of reward mappings such that for each player
i
and every play
\pi
, we have
\mu_{i}(\pi)=0
if we have
\mathsf{el}_{r_{i}}(\pi_{\leq n})=\bot
for some
n
, and
\mu_{i}(\pi)=1
otherwise.
When the context is clear, we write
\mathsf{el}_{i}
for
\mathsf{el}_{r_{i}}
.

3.5.2 Two-counter machines

We will see in
section

3.5.3
that energy games can be used to encode
counter machines
.
Let us first define that model.

Definition

16

(Two-counter machine)
.

A
two-counter machine
is a tuple

\mathcal{K}=\left(Q,q_{0},q_{\mathsf{f}},\Delta_{C_{1}^{+}},\Delta_{C_{2}^{+}},\Delta_{C_{1}^{-}},\Delta_{C_{2}^{-}}\right)
, with:

•

a finite set
Q
of
states
;

•

an
initial state

q_{0}\in Q
;

•

a
final state

q_{\mathsf{f}}
;

•

two sets
\Delta_{C_{1}^{+}},\Delta_{C_{2}^{+}}\in Q\times Q
of
incremental transitions
;

•

and two sets
\Delta_{C_{1}^{-}},\Delta_{C_{2}^{-}}\subseteq Q\times Q\times Q
of
test transitions
;

such that every state
q\in Q\setminus\{q_{\mathsf{f}}\}
admits exactly one outgoing transition (either incremental or test), and such that the state
q_{\mathsf{f}}
admits none.
We call
sequence of transitions
a finite or infinite sequence
\eta_{0}\eta_{1}\eta_{2}\dots
such that we have
\eta_{0}=q_{0}
and that for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a
counter

C\in\{C_{1},C_{2}\}
such that we have either
(\eta_{k},\eta_{k+1})\in\Delta_{C^{+}}
,
(\eta_{k},\eta_{k+1},\cdot)\in\Delta_{C^{-}}
, or
(\eta_{k},\cdot,\eta_{k+1})\in\Delta_{C^{-}}
.

We define the
interpretation

\hat{C}
of each counter
C
on sequences of transitions as follows:

•

on the one-state sequence of transitions, we define
\hat{C}(q_{0})=0
;

•

if we have
(q_{n-1},q_{n})\in\Delta_{C^{+}}
, we define
\hat{C}(q_{0}\dots q_{n})=\hat{C}(q_{0}\dots q_{n-1})+1
;

•

if we have
(q_{n-1},q_{n},\cdot)\in\Delta_{C^{-}}
, we define
\hat{C}(q_{0}\dots q_{n})=\hat{C}(q_{0}\dots q_{n-1})-1
;

•

if we have
(q_{n-1},\cdot,q_{n})\in\Delta_{C^{-}}
, we define
\hat{C}(q_{0}\dots q_{n})=\hat{C}(q_{0}\dots q_{n-1})
.

A
run
of the machine
\mathcal{K}
is a sequence of transition, either infinite or ending with
q_{\mathsf{f}}
, such that for every index
k
:

•

if we have
(\eta_{k},\eta_{k+1},\cdot)\in\Delta_{C^{-}}
, then we have
\hat{C}(\eta_{0}\dots\eta_{k})>0
;

•

and if we have
(\eta_{k},\cdot,\eta_{k+1})\in\Delta_{C^{-}}
, then we have
\hat{C}(\eta_{0}\dots\eta_{k})=0
.

Note that each two-counter machine admits exactly one run.
A machine
halts
if that unique run is finite.

Figures

9
and
10
depict two examples of two-counter machines.
The arrow with label
C_{1}^{+}
from
q_{0}
to
q_{1}
indicates a transition
(q_{0},q_{1})\in\Delta_{C_{1}^{+}}
.
The arrows with the label
C_{1}^{-}
from
q_{1}
to itself and with the label
C_{1}=0
from
q_{1}
to
q_{\mathsf{f}}
indicate a transition
(q_{1},q_{1},q_{\mathsf{f}})\in\Delta_{C_{1}^{-}}
.

Figure 9
:
A machine that halts on
(0,0)
q_{0}
q_{1}
q_{\mathsf{f}}
C_{1}^{+}
C_{1}^{-}
C_{1}=0

Figure 10
:
A machine that does not halt on
(0,0)
q_{0}
q_{1}
q_{\mathsf{f}}
C_{1}^{+}
C_{1}^{-}
C_{1}=0

We will use the following well-known result:

Theorem

5

(
[
minsky1961recursive
]
)
.

The problem of deciding whether a given two-counter machine halts is undecidable, and in particular not co-recursively enumerable.

3.5.3 Results

In energy games, our problem is undecidable, since a counter machine can be encoded in an energy game.
However, while we showed co-recursive enumerability in discounted-sum games, we will show that in energy games, it is recursively enumerable.

Theorem

6
.

The constrained existence problem of Nash equilibria in energy games is undecidable, even with no effective lower threshold and only one effective upper threshold, and recursively enumerable.

Proof.

\blacktriangleright
Undecidability.

We show undecidability by reduction from the halting problem of a two-counter machine.

q_{0}^{1}
C_{1}^{\top}
q_{0}^{2}
C_{2}^{\top}

(a)

Initial state

q_{\mathsf{f}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C_{1}^{\bot}}}{{-1}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C_{2}^{\bot}}}{{-1}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{W}}}}{{-1}}

(b)

Final state

q
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C^{\top}}}{{1}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C^{\bot}}}{{1}}

(c)

Incrementations

q
C^{\top}
(if
C>0
)
q^{\prime}
C^{\bot}
(if
C=0
)
\triangle
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C^{\top}}}{{-1}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C^{\bot}}}{{-1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C^{\bot}}}{{-1}}

(d)

Tests

Figure 11
:
Gadgets

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a two-counter machine.
We define an energy game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright q_{0}^{1}}
with five players (players
C_{1}^{\top}
,
C_{1}^{\bot}
,
C_{2}^{\top}
,
C_{2}^{\bot}
, and
{\mathfrak{W}}
, called
Witness
) by assembling the gadgets presented in Figure
11
.
The rewards that are not presented are equal to
0
, and the players controlling relevant vertices are written in black boxes.
For each state of
\mathcal{K}
, we define from one to two vertices, plus the additional vertex
\triangle
.
Then, a play in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright q_{0}^{1}}
that does not reach the vertex
\triangle
simulates a sequence of transitions of
\mathcal{K}
, that is a valid run if and only if each time the test gadget is visited, the player
C^{\top}
(with
C\in\{C_{1},C_{2}\}
) controlling
q
goes to
q^{\prime}
if and only if her energy level is
0
.
Then, at each step, the counter
C_{i}
is captured by the energy level of player
C_{i}^{\top}
, always equal to the energy level of player
C_{i}^{\bot}
.
Let us now prove that the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright q_{0}^{1}}
admits an NE where Witness loses if and only if the machine
\mathcal{K}
terminates.

If there is an NE where Witness loses, then the machine
\mathcal{K}
terminates.

Let
{\bar{\sigma}}
be an NE such that
\mu_{\mathfrak{W}}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle=0
.
Let
\pi=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
: that play is then lost by Witness.
Since the only edge that makes Witness lose energy is the edge
q_{\mathsf{f}}q_{\mathsf{f}}
, we deduce that the play
\pi
reaches the vertex
q_{\mathsf{f}}
, and therefore simulates a terminating sequence of transitions of
\mathcal{K}
(note that one transition may be represented by several edges).
We must now prove that this run is valid, i.e. that tests are simulated correctly.

–

Let us assume that at some point along the play
\pi
, from a vertex
q
, player
C_{i}^{\top}
, with
i\in\{1,2\}
, does not take the edge to the vertex
q^{\prime}
while her energy level is zero.
Then, her energy level drops to
\bot
and she loses.
Therefore, she has a profitable deviation at the beginning of the play, by looping on the vertex
q_{0}^{i}
.

–

Let us now assume that she goes to the vertex
q^{\prime}
, while her energy is positive.
Then, player
C_{i}^{\bot}
’s energy is also positive: he can go to the vertex
\triangle
and win.
That would be a profitable deviation, since, as the play
\pi
reaches the vertex
q_{\mathsf{f}}
, it is lost by player
C_{i}^{\bot}
.

Therefore, the play
\pi
does not fake any test, and simulates correctly the machine
\mathcal{K}
, which terminates.

If the machine
\mathcal{K}
terminates, then there is an NE where Witness loses.

Then, let us define a strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright q_{0}^{1}}
as follows: in tests of counter
C
, player
C^{\top}
goes to
q^{\prime}
if and only if her energy level is positive; from
q^{\prime}
, player
C^{\bot}
never goes to the vertex
\triangle
.
Let
\pi=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
: since tests are simulated correctly, the play
\pi
simulates the run of
\mathcal{K}
, and therefore reaches the vertex
q_{\mathsf{f}}
.
It is therefore lost by Witness, as well as by the players
C_{1}^{\bot}
and
C_{2}^{\bot}
.

Witness has no profitable deviation, since he does not control any vertex.
The only vertices controlled by each player
C^{\bot}
are the vertices of the form
q^{\prime}
.
Those vertices are reached only when player
C^{\top}
’s, and therefore player
C^{\bot}
’s energy level is zero.
Then, deviating and going to the vertex
\triangle
is not profitable for player
C^{\bot}
, since it makes him immediately lose.
The strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is therefore an NE, lost by Witness.

Conclusion.

Every NE outcome in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright q_{0}^{1}}
is won by Witness if and only if the machine
\mathcal{K}
does not terminate.
Therefore, the halting problem of two-counter machines reduces to the constrained existence problem of NEs in energy games, which is therefore undecidable.

\blacktriangleright
Recursive enumerability

Recursive enumerability will be a consequence of the following result.

Lemma

4
.

For every NE
{\bar{\sigma}}
in the energy game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, there exists a finite-memory NE
{\bar{\sigma}}^{\star}
with
\mu({\bar{\sigma}})=\mu({\bar{\sigma}}^{\star})
.

Proof.

Let
{\bar{\sigma}}
be an NE in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
.
Let
\pi=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
.
By Dickson’s lemma, there exist two indices
m
and
n
, with
m<n
, such that
\pi_{m}=\pi_{n}
and such that for every player
i
that does not lose the play
\pi
, we have
\mathsf{el}_{i}(\pi_{\leq m})\leq\mathsf{el}_{i}(\pi_{\leq n})
.
Moreover, we can chose
m
great enough to have
\mathsf{el}_{j}(\pi_{\leq m})=\bot
for every player
j
that loses the play
\pi
.
Thus, the players winning the play
\pi
are exactly the players winning the play
\chi=\pi_{<m}\left(\pi_{m}\dots\pi_{n-1}\right)^{\omega}
.

Now, let
k\leq n
, and let
i
be the player controlling the vertex
\pi_{k}
.
If
i
is a player who loses the play
\pi
, then since
{\bar{\sigma}}
is an NE, any play of the form
\pi_{\leq k}\chi
compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
is lost by player
i
; in other words, the strategy profile:

{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright\pi_{\leq k}}:\begin{cases}\mathsf{Hist}_{-i}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright\pi_{k}}&\rightarrow V\\
h&\mapstochar\rightarrow{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}(\pi_{<k}h)\end{cases}

is a strategy profile against which player
i
cannot win.
It is known (see for example
[
DBLP:conf/formats/BouyerFLMS08
, Lemma 10]
) that stationary strategies are sufficient to falsify an energy objective.
Therefore, let
{\bar{\tau}}^{k}_{-i}
be a stationary strategy profile, from the vertex
\pi_{k}
, against which player
i
cannot win.
Let
\tau^{k}_{i}
be an arbitrary stationary strategy.
If
i
is not a player who loses the play
\pi
, then we define
{\bar{\tau}}^{k}
as an arbitrary stationary strategy profile.

q_{0}
…
q_{m}
…
q_{n-1}
q^{\prime}_{m}
q^{\prime}_{n-1}
\pi_{0}|\pi_{1}
\pi_{m-1}|\pi_{m}
\pi_{m}|\pi_{m+1}
\pi_{n-2}|\pi_{n-1}
\pi_{n-1}|\pi_{m}
v\in V\setminus\{\pi_{m}\}|{\bar{\tau}}^{m}(v)
v\in V\setminus\{\pi_{n-1}\}|{\bar{\tau}}^{n-1}(v)
v|{\bar{\tau}}^{m}(v)
v|{\bar{\tau}}^{n}(v)

Figure 12
:
The memory structure
\mathcal{M}

Let
\mathcal{M}
be the memory structure depicted by
Figure

12
, and defined as follows: it has
2n
states, namely
q_{0},\dots,q_{n-1}
and
q^{\prime}_{0},\dots,q^{\prime}_{n-1}
.
From each state
q_{k}
, the transition reading the vertex
\pi_{k}
leads to the state
q_{k+1}
(or
q_{m}
if
k=n-1
), and outputs the vertex
\pi_{k+1}
(or
\pi_{m}
if
k=n-1
).
The transition reading any other vertex
v
(if
k\geq 1
) leads to the state
q^{\prime}_{k}
and outputs the vertex
{\bar{\tau}}^{k-1}(v)
.
From the state
q^{\prime}_{k}
, the transition reading each vertex
v
leads to
q^{\prime}_{k}
, and outputs the vertex
{\bar{\tau}}^{k-1}(v)
(since
{\bar{\tau}}^{k-1}
is stationary).
Thus, the strategy profile induced by the memory structure
\mathcal{M}
is the strategy profile that follows the play
\chi
, and that punishes any player who deviates by following the stationary strategy profile
{\bar{\tau}}^{k}
.
That strategy profile is finite-memory, and generates the same payoff vector as
{\bar{\sigma}}
, as desired.
∎

Moreover, once we have a memory structure, there is an algorithm that says whether it induces an NE or not.

Lemma

5
.

Given an energy game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
and a multiplayer memory structure
\mathcal{M}
, deciding whether the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
induced by
\mathcal{M}
is an NE can be done in deterministic polynomial time.

Proof.

Given a player
i
, that player has a profitable deviation from
{\bar{\sigma}}
if and only if these two conditions are satisfied:

*

the play
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
is lost by player
i
;

*

there exists a play
\pi
compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
such that
\mu_{i}(\pi)=1
.

The first condition can be checked in polynomial time, since the play
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
is a lasso whose size is bounded by a polynomial function of
\lVert\mathcal{M}\rVert
.
The second condition is satisfied if and only if there exists a play giving player
i
the payoff
1
in the one-player game:

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\left(\{i\},V\times Q,(V\times Q),E^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}\right),

where
\mu^{\prime}:(\pi_{0},q_{0})(\pi_{1},q_{1})\dots\mapstochar\rightarrow\mu_{i}(\pi)
, and
E^{\prime}
contains the transitions
(u,p)(v,q)
such that
\delta(p,u)=q
and either
\nu(p,u)=v
, or
u\in V_{i}
.
Checking the existence of such a play can be done in polynomial time according to
[
DBLP:conf/formats/BouyerFLMS08
, Theorem 7]
.
∎

Thus, a semi-algorithm that recognizes the positive instances of the constrained existence problem consists in enumerating the deterministic multiplayer memory structures on
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, and for each of them, to check (by diagonalization):

–

whether the only strategy profile compatible with it is an NE: that problem is decidable (in polynomial time) by
5
;

–

whether that strategy profile generates a payoff vector between
\bar{x}
and
\bar{y}
: that is recursively enumerable, by constructing step by step its outcome and computing the energy levels on the fly.

By
4
, we have a positive instance of the constrained problem if and only if at least one memory structure satisfies those two conditions.
The constrained existence problem is therefore recursively enumerable.
∎

PART III:

SUBGAME-PERFECT EQUILIBRIA

Chapter 4:  Subgame-perfect equilibria and negotiation

Let us imagine the following situation: in a parking lot, cars—which can be automated, semi-automated, or driven solely by a human—must enter and exit through the same entrance, which is too narrow to allow two cars to pass simultaneously.
When one car arrives and another one leaves, they (and their possibly existing drivers) must agree on who should pass first to avoid getting stuck.
Since endless negotiations are undesirable, they need a protocol to make this decision. In game-theoretic terms, such a protocol would be represented by a strategy profile.
For example, the protocol could be as follows: when two cars arrive at the same time, one direction (typically exiting) has priority over the other. When there are queues on both sides, cars alternate.

A more challenging issue arises when a car does not follow the protocol.
Ideally, such deviations should not allow the car to gain time in any way; otherwise, impatient drivers would always be tempted to deviate, making the protocol ineffective in practice. This is why it is desirable for the strategy profile representing the protocol to be a Nash equilibrium.

On the other hand, let us consider the following protocol, which does satisfy the conditions of a Nash equilibrium: first, cars adhere to the priority rules defined above. Then, if a car goes through the entrance when it is not supposed to, all other cars stop driving, resulting in a complete and permanent standstill.
The flaw in this approach is that the threat meant to prevent deviations is enforced by agents who have their own objectives (reaching their destinations within a reasonable time), which they will not simply disregard when a deviation occurs.

This situation highlights a fundamental limitation of Nash equilibria in sequential settings: a problem known as
non-credible threats
.
In doing so, it also advocates for a more robust equilibrium concept, one that leads to interesting algorithmic challenges:
subgame-perfect equilibria
.
That concept was first introduced by Reinhard Selten
[
selten
]
, and earned him the Nobel prize in economic sciences in 1994, shared with John Nash and John Harsanyi.

4.1  Subgame-perfect equilibria

Formally, subgame-perfect equilibria will be defined by the fact that players play rationally not only in the outcome of the strategy profile, but also in
subgames
.

Definition

17

(Subgame, substrategy)
.

Let
hv
be a history in the game
\mathcal{G}
. The
subgame
of the game
\mathcal{G}
after the history
hv
is the initialized game
\left(\Pi,V,(V_{i})_{i},E,\mu_{\upharpoonright hv}\right)_{\upharpoonright v}
, where
\mu_{\upharpoonright hv}
maps each play to its payoff in the game
\mathcal{G}
, assuming that the history
hv
has already been played: formally, for every
\pi\in\mathsf{Plays}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright hv}
, we have
\mu_{\upharpoonright hv}(\pi)=\mu(h\pi)
.

If
\sigma_{i}
is a strategy in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, its
substrategy
after
hv
is the strategy
\sigma_{i\upharpoonright hv}
in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright hv}
, defined by
\sigma_{i\upharpoonright hv}(h^{\prime})=\sigma_{i}(hh^{\prime})
for every
h^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Hist}_{i}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright hv}
.

Remark

2
.

The initialized game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
is also the subgame of
\mathcal{G}
after the one-vertex history
v_{0}
.

We can now define subgame-perfect equilibria.

Definition

18

(Subgame-perfect equilibrium)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
be a game.
The strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is a
subgame-perfect equilibrium
, or
SPE
for short, in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, if and only if for every history
h
in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h}
is a Nash equilibrium in the subgame
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright h}
.

Example

3
.

We have recalled in Figure
13
the game that was already given in
1
.
In this game, there are actually two NEs:

•

one, depicted in blue, where
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
goes to the vertex
b
and then player
\square
goes to
c
, and both get the payoff
1
;

•

and one, depicted in red, where player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
stays in
a
, and has no incentive to deviate to
b
because player
\square
plans to stay in
b
.

However, only the first one is an SPE, because in the subgame after the history
ab
, player
\square
has a profitable deviation by going to
c
.

\begin{matrix}~\\
~\\
~\\
~\\
\mu_{\circ}=\mu_{\square}=0\end{matrix}
a
\begin{matrix}~\\
~\\
~\\
~\\
\mu_{\circ}=\mu_{\square}=0\end{matrix}
b
\begin{matrix}~\\
~\\
~\\
~\\
\mu_{\circ}=\mu_{\square}=1\end{matrix}
c

Figure 13
:
Two Nash equilibria

While Nash equilibria are known to exist in all the game classes we study in this thesis, it is not the case of SPEs
[
SOLAN2003911
,
DBLP:conf/csl/BrihayeBMR15
]
.
We will therefore, sometimes, need a quantitative relaxation of SPEs, namely
\varepsilon
-SPEs
.
That notion will be built on the notion of
\varepsilon
-Nash equilibrium
: given a quantity
\varepsilon\geq 0
, a strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is an
\varepsilon
-Nash equilibrium, or
\varepsilon
-NE for short, if for each player
i
and every deviation
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
of player
i
from
{\bar{\sigma}}
, the inequality
\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle\leq\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle+\varepsilon
holds—i.e., the deviation
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
is not profitable
by more than
\varepsilon
.
We can then define
\varepsilon
-SPEs.

Definition

19

(
\varepsilon
-Subgame-perfect equilibrium)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
be a game, and let
\varepsilon\geq 0
.
The strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is an
\varepsilon
-subgame-perfect equilibrium
, or
\varepsilon
-SPE
for short, in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, if and only if for every history
h
in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h}
is an
\varepsilon
-NE in the subgame
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright h}
.

Remark

3
.

The definitions of
0
-NEs and
0
-SPEs coincide, respectively, with those of NEs and SPEs.

This part of this thesis is dedicated to finding and proving the complexities of the constrained existence problem of SPEs, and
\varepsilon
-SPEs when it is relevant, in the game classes we have introduced in
part

II
.

4.2  Negotiation

4.2.1 Origin of the concept

The idea behind the concept of negotiation seems to have emerged in two parallel ways, without a full formalization.

On the one hand, János Flesch and Arkadi Predtetchinski introduced in 2017 a characterization of SPEs in Borel games where the payoff functions have a finite range (such as Boolean games), based on an iterative process corresponding to what we call here the
negotiation function
, defined from what we refer to as the
abstract negotiation game

[
DBLP:journals/mor/FleschP17
]
.
When the payoff functions have an infinite range (as in mean-payoff or discounted-sum games), their work characterizes a set of plays that is known to contain only outcomes of
\varepsilon
-SPEs, for every
\varepsilon>0
, as well as all outcomes of SPEs.
We will show that it actually characterizes SPE outcomes in all games
with steady negotiation
.
Since their work was primarily conducted with the objective of proving existence results, Flesch and Predtetchinski’s approach does not provide a characterization that can be directly used for algorithmic purposes, as their iterative process may be infinite, and each iteration requires solving an infinite number of two-player zero-sum games, which can themselves be of infinite size.

On the other hand, in 2019, Marie van den Bogaard, Thomas Brihaye, Véronique Bruyère, Aline Goeminne, and Jean-François Raskin proved the
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-completeness of the constrained existence problem of SPEs in a class of games that we do not study here:
quantitative reachability games
, where players aim to reach a given set of vertices as quickly as possible
[
DBLP:conf/concur/BrihayeBGRB19
]
.
Their upper bound relied on an algorithm that labels each vertex with an integer, representing the number of steps within which the player controlling the vertex can, and therefore must, reach their target.
The algorithm then updates this labeling iteratively, leveraging the fact that a player may discover new ways to reach their target more quickly when other players, while trying to prevent them from doing so, are also attempting to satisfy their own requirements.
These updates, which correspond to one iteration of the negotiation function (in a case where Flesch and Predtetchinski’s work did not provide a full characterization, since quantitative reachability payoff functions have infinite range), continue until a fixed point is reached, which characterizes SPEs.

In both cases, the negotiation function was not explicitly formalized as a function but was instead an implicit part of an iterative process.
The reader will observe in the following chapters that reasoning about these iterations as a function, whose fixed points characterize SPEs, is useful in some cases where simply iterating the function is not the most efficient way to solve our problem, or could even fail to terminate, as is the case in mean-payoff games.

4.2.2 Requirements

In the method we will develop further, we will need to analyze the players’ behaviors when they have some
requirement
to satisfy.
Intuitively, one can see requirements as
rationality constraints
for the players, that is, a threshold payoff value under which a player will not accept to follow a play, because they would be better off deviating from it.

Definition

20

(Requirement)
.

A
requirement
on the game
\mathcal{G}
is a mapping
\lambda:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
.

For a given vertex
v
, the quantity
\lambda(v)
represents the minimal payoff that the player controlling
v
will require in a play starting from
v
.

Definition

21

(
\lambda
-consistency)
.

Let
\lambda
be a requirement on a game
\mathcal{G}
.
A play
\pi
in the game
\mathcal{G}
is
\lambda
-consistent
if and only if, for every player
i\in\Pi
and integer
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
\pi_{n}\in V_{i}
, we have
\mu_{i}(\pi_{\geq n})~\geq~\lambda(\pi_{n})
.
The set of
\lambda
-consistent plays from a vertex
v
is denoted by
\lambda\mathsf{Cons}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v}
, or
\lambda\mathsf{Cons}(v)
when the context is clear.

Such a requirement also induces a notion of rationality for strategy profiles aiming at punishing one player.

Definition

22

(
\lambda
-rationality)
.

Let
\lambda
be a requirement on a game
\mathcal{G}
.
Let
i\in\Pi
.
The strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
is
\lambda
-rational assuming
the strategy
\sigma_{i}
if and only if for every history
hv
compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
, the play
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}\rangle
is
\lambda
-consistent.
A strategy profile is simply
\lambda
-rational
when it is
\lambda
-rational assuming some strategy.
The set of
\lambda
-rational strategy profiles in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v}
is denoted by
\lambda\mathsf{Rat}_{-i}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v}
, or
\lambda\mathsf{Rat}(v)
when the context is clear.

Note that
\lambda
-rationality is a property of a strategy profile for all the players but one, player
i
. Intuitively, their rationality is justified by the fact that they collectively assume that player
i
will, eventually, play according to the strategy
\sigma_{i}
: if it is the case, then everyone gets their payoff satisfied.

Finally, let us define a particular requirement: the
vacuous requirement
, that requires nothing, and with which every play is consistent.

Definition

23

(Vacuous requirement)
.

In any game, the
vacuous requirement
, denoted by
\lambda_{0}
, is the requirement constantly equal to
-\infty
.

4.2.3 Negotiation

We will show that SPEs in prefix-independent games are characterized by the fixed points of a function on requirements. That function captures a
negotiation
process: when a player has a requirement to satisfy, another player can hope for a better payoff than what they can secure in general, and therefore update their own requirement.
Note that we always use the convention
\infop\emptyset=+\infty
.

Definition

24

(Negotiation function, steady negotiation)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a game.
The
negotiation function
is the function that transforms each requirement
\lambda
on
\mathcal{G}
into a requirement
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)
on
\mathcal{G}
, such that for each player
i\in\Pi
and every vertex
v\in V_{i}
, we have:

\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v)=\infop_{{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}\in\lambda\mathsf{Rat}(v)}\supop_{\sigma_{i}\in\mathsf{Strat}_{i}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v}}\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle.

If that infimum is realized for every requirement
\lambda
, player
i
and vertex
v\in V_{i}
such that
\lambda\mathsf{Rat}(v)\neq\emptyset
, then the game
\mathcal{G}
is called a game
with steady negotiation
.

The quantity
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v)
is then the worst case value that the player controlling
v
can ensure, assuming that the other players play
\lambda
-rationally.

Remark

4
.

The negotiation function satisfies the following properties.

•

It is monotone: if we have
\lambda\leq\lambda^{\prime}
, then we have
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)\leq\mathsf{nego}(\lambda^{\prime})
.

•

It is also non-decreasing: for every
\lambda
, we have
\lambda\leq\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)
.

•

There exists a
\lambda
-rational strategy profile from
v
against the player controlling
v
if and only if
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v)\neq+\infty
.

Example

4
.

Let us consider again the game depicted by Figure
13
, and let us consider the vacuous requirement
\lambda_{0}:v\rightarrow-\infty
.
Let us compute
\lambda_{1}=\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{0})
.

From the vertex
c
, the only payoff that can be obtained is
\lambda_{1}(c)=1
.
From the vertex
b
, player
\square
can go to the vertex
c
and get the payoff
\lambda_{1}(b)=1
.
From the vertex
a
, even if player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
goes to
b
, player
\square
can stay there, hence she cannot get more than
\lambda_{1}(a)=0
.

Let us now compute
\lambda_{2}=\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{1})
.
From
c
, the only payoff possible is still
\lambda_{2}(c)=1
, and from
b
, it is still possible to get
\lambda_{2}(b)=1
and nothing more.
But from the vertex
a
, a
\lambda
-rational strategy profile must necessarily plan to go to the vertex
c
if the vertex
b
is reached, hence
\lambda_{2}(a)=1
.

Finally, we have
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{2})=\lambda_{2}
.

4.3  Link between negotiation and equilibria

4.3.1 Nash equilibria

Although all our computational results about Nash equilibria have already been stated in
chapter

3
, let us show how we can, using the negotiation function, rephrase a folklore characterization of Nash equilibria outcomes: a play is a Nash equilibrium outcome if and only if it gives to every player at least the payoff that they could enforce by deviating, if all the other players try to punish them—i.e., if for every vertex it traverses, it gives to the player controlling that vertex its adversarial value.

Theorem

7
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a game with steady negotiation, and let
\pi
be a play in the game
\mathcal{G}
.
Then, the play
\pi
is an NE outcome if and only if
\pi
is
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{0})
-consistent.

Proof.

\blacktriangleright
If
\pi
is an NE outcome, then it is
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{0})
-consistent.

Let
{\bar{\sigma}}
be a Nash equilibrium in the game
\mathcal{G}
, and let
\pi=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
: let us prove that the play
\pi
is
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{0})
-consistent.
Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
i\in\Pi
be such that
\pi_{k}\in V_{i}
, and let us prove that
\mu_{i}\left(\pi_{\geq k}\right)\geq\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{0})(\pi_{k})
.
For any deviation
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
of player
i
from the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright\pi_{\leq k}}
, by definition of NEs, we have
\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright\pi_{\leq k}},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle\leq\mu_{i}(\pi)
.
Therefore, we have
\mu_{i}(\pi)\geq\supop_{\sigma^{\prime}_{i}}\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright\pi_{\leq k}},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle
, hence the inequality:

\mu_{i}(\pi)\geq\infop_{{\bar{\tau}}_{-i}}~\supop_{\tau_{i}}~\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\tau}}_{-i\upharpoonright\pi_{\leq k}},\tau_{i}\rangle,

i.e.
\mu_{i}(\pi)\geq\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{0})(\pi_{k})
: the play
\pi
is
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{0})
-consistent.

\blacktriangleright
If
\pi
is
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{0})
-consistent, then it is an NE outcome.

Let
\pi
be a
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{0})
-consistent play in the game
\mathcal{G}
.
Let us define a strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
generating
\pi
as follows.

–

First, we define
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle=\pi
.

–

Then, for each history of the form
\pi_{\leq k}v
with
v\neq\pi_{k+1}
, let
i
be the player controlling
\pi_{k}
.
Since the game
\mathcal{G}
is with steady negotiation, the infimum:

\infop_{{\bar{\tau}}_{-i}\in\lambda_{0}\mathsf{Rat}(\pi_{k})}~\supop_{\tau_{i}}~\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle=\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{0})(v)\neq+\infty

is a minimum.
Let
{\bar{\tau}}^{k}_{-i}
be
\lambda_{0}
-rational strategy profile from
\pi_{k}
realizing that minimum, and let
\tau^{k}_{i}
be some strategy from
\pi_{k}
.
Then, we define:

\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright\pi_{\leq k}v}\rangle=\langle{\bar{\tau}}^{k}_{\upharpoonright\pi_{k}v}\rangle.

–

For every other history
h
, the vertex
{\bar{\sigma}}(h)
is defined arbitrarily.

Let us prove that
{\bar{\sigma}}
is an NE: let
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
be a deviation of
\sigma_{i}
, and let
\pi^{\prime}=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle
.
Since the case
\pi^{\prime}=\pi
is trivial, we assume
\pi\neq\pi^{\prime}
and define
\pi_{\leq k}
as the longest common prefix of
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
.
Let
v=\pi^{\prime}_{k+1}\neq\pi_{k+1}
.
Then, the play
\pi^{\prime}_{\geq k+1}
is compatible with the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright\pi_{\leq k}v}={\bar{\tau}}_{-i\upharpoonright\pi_{k}v}
, hence the inequality:

\mu_{i}(\pi^{\prime})\leq\supop_{\tau_{i}^{k}}~\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\tau}}^{k}\rangle=\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{0})(\pi_{k}).

On the other hand, since the play
\pi
is
\lambda_{0}
-consistent, we have
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{0})(\pi_{k})\leq\mu_{i}(\pi)
, hence
\mu_{i}(\pi^{\prime})\leq\mu_{i}(\pi)
: the deviation
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
is not profitable, and the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is a Nash equilibrium.
∎

4.3.2 Link with (
\varepsilon
–)subgame-perfect equilibria

The notion of negotiation will enable us to find the SPEs, but also more generally the
\varepsilon
-SPEs, in a game. For that purpose, we need the notion of
\varepsilon
-fixed points of a function.

Definition

25

(
\varepsilon
-fixed point)
.

Let
\varepsilon\geq 0
, let
D
be a finite set and let
f:(\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\})^{D}\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\})^{D}
be a mapping. A tuple
\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
is an
\varepsilon
-fixed point
of
f
if for each
d\in D
, for
\bar{y}=f(\bar{x})
, we have
y_{d}\in[x_{d}-\varepsilon,x_{d}+\varepsilon]
.

Remark

5
.

The definition of
0
-fixed points coincide with that of fixed points.

We now have all the tools required to state and prove the following theorem.
We give it here in a form that applies only to prefix-independent games, even though the same result extends (at least when
\varepsilon=0
) to classes of games that do not observe that property, such as quantitative reachability games or discounted-sum games.
However, since we do not use this result in such classes, we favored the following proof, less general but simpler.

Theorem

8
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a game with steady negotiation, and let
\pi
be a play in the game
\mathcal{G}
.
Let
\varepsilon\geq 0
.
Then, the play
\pi
is an
\varepsilon
-SPE outcome if and only if
\pi
is
\lambda
-consistent, for some
\varepsilon
-fixed point
\lambda
of the negotiation function.

Proof.

\blacktriangleright
If
\pi
is an
\varepsilon
-SPE outcome, then it is
\lambda
-consistent for some
\varepsilon
-fixed point
\lambda
.

Let
{\bar{\sigma}}
be an
\varepsilon
-SPE in the game
\mathcal{G}
such that
\pi=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
.

Let us define a requirement
\lambda
by, for each
i\in\Pi
and
v\in V_{i}
:

\lambda(v)=\infop_{hv\in\mathsf{Hist}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}}\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}\rangle.

Then, for every history
hv
starting in
\pi_{0}
, the play
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}\rangle
is
\lambda
-consistent.
And in particular, the play
\pi
is.
Let us now prove that the requirement
\lambda
is an
\varepsilon
-fixed point of
\mathsf{nego}
.

Let
i\in\Pi
, let
v\in V_{i}
, and let us show that we have
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v)\leq\lambda(v)+\varepsilon
.
Since
{\bar{\sigma}}
is an
\varepsilon
-SPE, for every history
hv\in\mathsf{Hist}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright\pi_{0}}
, we have:

\supop_{\tau_{i}}~\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright hv},\tau_{i}\rangle\leq\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}\rangle+\varepsilon.

By taking the infima over
hv
, we deduce:

\infop_{hv\in\mathsf{Hist}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright\pi_{0}}}~\supop_{\tau_{i}}~\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright hv},\tau_{i}\rangle\leq\infop_{hv}\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}\rangle+\varepsilon=\lambda(v)+\varepsilon.

Let us now notice that all the strategy profiles of the form
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright hv}
are
\lambda
-rational.
Thus, we obtain:

\infop_{{\bar{\tau}}_{-i}\in\lambda\mathsf{Rat}(v)}~\supop_{\tau_{i}}~\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle\leq\lambda(v)+\varepsilon,

that is, we obtain
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v)\leq\lambda(v)+\varepsilon
, as desired.

\blacktriangleright
If
\pi
is
\lambda
-consistent for some fixed point
\lambda
, then
\pi
is an
\varepsilon
-SPE outcome.

A particular case: if there exists
v
accessible from
\pi_{0}
such that
\lambda(v)=+\infty
.

In that case, for each vertex
u
such that
uv\in E
, if the player controlling
u
chooses to go to
v
, no
\lambda
-consistent play can be proposed to them from there, hence there is no
\lambda
-rational strategy profile against that player from
u
, and
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(u)=+\infty
.
Since
\varepsilon
is finite and since
\lambda
is an
\varepsilon
-fixed point of the negotiation function, it follows that
\lambda(u)=+\infty
. Since
v
is accessible from
\pi_{0}
, we can repeat this argument and show that
\lambda(\pi_{0})=+\infty
; in that case, there is no
\lambda
-consistent play
\pi
from
u
, and then the proof is done.

Therefore, for the rest of the proof, we assume that for all
v
, we have
\lambda(v)\neq+\infty
.
As a consequence, since
\lambda
is an
\varepsilon
-fixed point of the function
\mathsf{nego}
, for each
v
accessible from
\pi_{0}
, we have
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v)\neq+\infty
; which implies that for each such
v
, there exists a
\lambda
-consistent strategy profile against the player controlling
v
, starting from
v
.

The rest of the proof constructs the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
and proves that it is an
\varepsilon
-SPE.
That construction is illustrated by Figure
14
.

\bullet
\pi_{0}
\pi
\bullet
u\in V_{i}
\bullet
\bullet
\left.\begin{matrix}\\
\\
\end{matrix}\right\}

{\bar{\tau}}^{u}
\bullet
w\in V_{j}
\left.\right\}

{\bar{\tau}}^{w}
\bullet
v\in V_{i}
\left.\right\}

{\bar{\tau}}^{v}
(if reset)

Figure 14
:
The construction of
{\bar{\sigma}}

Spare parts: the strategy profiles
{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}
.

Let us recall that since
\mathcal{G}
is a game with steady negotiation.
Then, let
i\in\Pi
and
v\in V_{i}
: since, by the previous point, we assume
\lambda\mathsf{Rat}(v)\neq\emptyset
for each
v
, we know that there exists a strategy profile
{\bar{\tau}}^{v}_{-i}
from
v
that is
\lambda
-rational assuming a strategy
\tau^{v}_{i}
and that satisfies the inequality:

\supop_{\tau_{i}}~\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\tau}}^{v}_{-i},\tau_{i}\rangle=\infop_{{\bar{\tau}}_{-i}\in\lambda\mathsf{Rat}(v)}~\supop_{\tau_{i}}~\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle=\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v).

In other words, there exists a worst
\lambda
-rational strategy profile against player
i
from the vertex
v
, with regards to player
i
’s payoff.

Our goal in this part of the proof is to construct a strategy profile
{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}_{-i}
, that is
\lambda
-rational assuming a strategy
\tau^{v*}_{i}
, and that will be used to punish player
i
when they deviate from
{\bar{\sigma}}
until another player deviates.
The strategy profile
{\bar{\tau}}^{v}_{-i}
and the strategy
\tau^{v}_{i}
are not sufficient for that purpose, because if some history
h
compatible with
{\bar{\tau}}^{v}_{-i}
is such that
\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\tau}}^{v}_{\upharpoonright h}\rangle<\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\tau}}^{v}\rangle
, then in the corresponding subgame, it may be possible for player
i
to deviate and get a payoff that would be smaller than or equal to the quantity
\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\tau}}^{v}\rangle
, but greater than
\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\tau}}^{v}_{\upharpoonright h}\rangle
.
On the contrary, the construction of the strategy profile
{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}_{-i}
will ensure that each time player
i
deviates, the other players punish them at least as harshly as they were planning to do before the deviation.

Let us construct inductively the strategy profile
{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}
.
We define it only on histories that are compatible with
{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}_{-i}
, since it can be defined arbitrarily on other histories.
We proceed by assembling the strategy profiles of the form
{\bar{\tau}}^{w}
for various
w\in V_{i}
, and the histories after which we follow a new
{\bar{\tau}}^{w}
will be called the
resets
of
{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}
: they will be histories of the form
hw^{\prime}
, where
h
is empty or
\mathsf{last}(h)=w
.

–

First, we set
\langle{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}\rangle=\langle{\bar{\tau}}^{v}\rangle
: the one-vertex history
v
is then the first reset of
{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}_{-i}
.

–

Then, for every history
hww^{\prime}
from
v
such that
h
is compatible with
{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}_{-i}
, that
w\in V_{i}
, and that
w^{\prime}\neq\tau^{v*}_{i}(hw)
: let us decompose
hww^{\prime}=h_{1}h_{2}
, so that the history
h_{1}\mathsf{first}(h_{2})
is the longest reset of
{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}_{-i}
among the prefixes of
hw
.
Or, in other words, so that the strategy profile
{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}_{\upharpoonright h_{1}\mathsf{first}(h_{2})}
has been defined as equal to
{\bar{\tau}}^{u}
over the prefixes of
h_{2}
until
w
, where
u=v
if
h_{1}
is empty, or
u=\mathsf{last}(h_{1})
otherwise.
By prefix-independence of
\mathcal{G}
and by definition of
{\bar{\tau}}^{u}
and
{\bar{\tau}}^{w}
, we have:

\infop_{{\bar{\tau}}_{-i}\in\lambda\mathsf{Rat}(w^{\prime})}\supop_{\tau_{i}}\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle\leq\supop_{\tau_{i}}\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\tau}}^{w}_{-i},\tau_{i}\rangle=\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(w).

Let us now separate two cases.

*

Suppose first that there is equality:

\infop_{{\bar{\tau}}_{-i}\in\lambda\mathsf{Rat}(w^{\prime})}\supop_{\tau_{i}}\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle=\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(w).

Then, we choose
\langle{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}_{\upharpoonright hww^{\prime}}\rangle=\langle{\bar{\tau}}^{u}_{\upharpoonright uh_{2}}\rangle
: the coalition of players against player
i
keeps following the same strategy profile.

*

Suppose now that the inequality is strict:

\infop_{{\bar{\tau}}_{-i}\in\lambda\mathsf{Rat}(w^{\prime})}\supop_{\tau_{i}}\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle<\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(w).

Then, we choose
\langle{\bar{\tau}}^{v^{*}}_{\upharpoonright hww^{\prime}}\rangle=\langle{\bar{\tau}}^{w}_{\upharpoonright ww^{\prime}}\rangle
: player
i
has done something that lowers the payoff they can ensure, and therefore the other players have to update their strategy profile in order to punish them more harshly.
The history
hw
is a reset of
{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}_{-i}
.

Since there are finitely many histories of each length, this process completely defines the strategy profile
{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}
.
Moreover, all the plays constructed are
\lambda
-consistent, hence the strategy profile
{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}_{-i}
is
\lambda
-rational assuming the strategy
\tau^{v*}_{i}
, as desired.

Construction of
{\bar{\sigma}}
.

Let us now construct inductively the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
itself: we will prove in the next part of the proof that it is an
\varepsilon
-SPE.
We proceed inductively, by defining all the plays
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}\rangle
, for
hv\in\mathsf{Hist}\mathcal{G}_{\pi_{0}}
with
v\neq{\bar{\sigma}}(h)
.
We maintain the induction hypothesis that such a play is always
\lambda
-consistent.

–

First, we choose
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle=\pi
, which satisfies the induction hypothesis.

–

Let now
huv
be a history such that the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
has been defined on all the prefixes of
hu
, which we now assume to be nonempty, but not on
huv
itself, and such that
v\neq{\bar{\sigma}}(hu)
.
Let
i
be the player controlling the vertex
u
.

Then, we define
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright huv}\rangle=\langle{\bar{\tau}}^{u*}_{\upharpoonright uv}\rangle
, and inductively, for every history
h^{\prime}w
starting from
v
and compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright huv}
, we define
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright huh^{\prime}w}\rangle=\langle{\bar{\tau}}^{u*}_{\upharpoonright uh^{\prime}w}\rangle
.
The strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright huv}
is then equal to
{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}_{\upharpoonright uv}
on any history compatible with
{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}_{-i}
.

Since there are finitely many histories of each length, this process completely defines
{\bar{\sigma}}
.

The strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is an
\varepsilon
-SPE.

Consider a history
h_{0}w\in\mathsf{Hist}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright\pi_{0}}
, a player
i\in\Pi
, and a deviation
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
of
\sigma_{i}
.
Let
\chi=h_{0}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h_{0}w}\rangle
, and let
\chi^{\prime}=h_{0}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright h_{0}w},\sigma^{\prime}_{i\upharpoonright h_{0}w}\rangle
.
We wish to prove the inequality
\mu_{i}(\chi^{\prime})\leq\mu_{i}(\chi)+\varepsilon
.
The different notations in this proof are illustrated by
fig.

15
.

\bullet
\pi_{0}
\pi
\bullet
\chi_{n}\in V_{i}
\chi
\bullet
w
\bullet
\chi_{p}\in V_{i}
\bullet
\chi_{m}\in V_{i}
\chi^{\prime}

Figure 15
:
The strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is an SPE.

First, if the play
\chi^{\prime}
is compatible with
\sigma_{i}
, then we have
\chi^{\prime}=\chi
and the proof is immediate.
Now, if it is not, we let
n
denote the least index such that
\chi^{\prime}_{n}\in V_{i}
and
\chi^{\prime}_{n+1}\neq\sigma_{i}(\chi^{\prime}_{\leq n})
, and such that the play
\chi^{\prime}_{\geq n}
is compatible with the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright\chi^{\prime}_{\leq n}}
.
Thus, the edge
\chi^{\prime}_{n}\chi^{\prime}_{n+1}
marks the time when player
i
begins to deviate unilaterally from
\sigma_{i}
.
However, note that
\chi^{\prime}_{\leq n}
can be both longer or shorter than
h_{0}w
: player
i
may have already deviated in
h_{0}w
, or may wait afterwards to effectively deviate.

Be that as it may, the history
\chi^{\prime}_{\leq n}
is a common prefix of the plays
\chi
and
\chi^{\prime}
, and the substrategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright\chi^{\prime}_{\leq n+1}}
has been defined during the construction of
{\bar{\sigma}}
as equal to
{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}_{\upharpoonright\chi^{\prime}_{n}\chi^{\prime}_{n+1}}
, where
v=\chi^{\prime}_{n}
, on any history compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright\chi^{\prime}_{\leq n+1}}
.

By construction of
{\bar{\tau}}^{v*}
, the sequence:

\left(\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(\chi^{\prime}_{k})\right)_{k\geq n,\chi^{\prime}_{k}\in V_{i}}

is non-increasing.
It is therefore ultimately constant (or is finite), because it can take only a finite number of values.
Consequently, there is a finite number of resets along the play
\chi^{\prime}_{\geq n}
.
Let then
\chi^{\prime}_{n}\dots\chi^{\prime}_{m+1}
be the last (longest) one.
Afterwards, the play
\chi^{\prime}_{\geq m+1}
is compatible with the strategy profile
{\bar{\tau}}^{\chi^{\prime}_{m}}_{-i}
.
By definition of that strategy profile, we have the inequality
\mu_{i}(\chi^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(\chi^{\prime}_{m})
.
We need now to prove the inequality
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(\chi^{\prime}_{m})\leq\mu_{i}(\chi)+\varepsilon
.

Let now
\chi_{\leq p}=\chi^{\prime}_{\leq p}
denote the longest common prefix of
\chi
and
\chi^{\prime}
.
Note that, then, we have
n\leq p
and
\chi_{p}\in V_{i}
.
Moreover, we have
\chi_{\geq p}=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright\chi_{\leq p}}\rangle
, which is
\lambda
-consistent.
As a consequence, we have the inequality
\mu_{i}(\chi)\geq\lambda(\chi_{p})
.

Finally, since the sequence of the quantities
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(\chi^{\prime}_{k})
with
\chi^{\prime}_{k}\in V_{i}
is non-increasing for
k\geq n
, we also have
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(\chi^{\prime}_{m-1})\leq\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(\chi_{p})
.
Consequently, we have:

\mu_{i}(\chi^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(\chi^{\prime}_{m})\leq\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(\chi_{p})\leq\lambda(\chi_{p})+\varepsilon\leq\mu_{i}(\chi)+\varepsilon.

The strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is an
\varepsilon
-SPE.
∎

Example

5
.

Let us consider again the game of Figure
13
.
The two NE outcomes that we identified in Example
3
are indeed the two plays from
a
that are
\lambda_{1}
-consistent: the play
a^{\omega}
and the play
abc^{\omega}
.
However, only the latter is
\lambda_{2}
-consistent, and
\lambda_{2}
is a fixed point of the negotiation function.

This theorem will enable us to design efficient algorithms for the (
\varepsilon
-)SPE constrained existence problems.
Indeed, given a game
\mathcal{G}
and two thresholds
\bar{x}
and
\bar{y}
, one can decide whether there exists an (
\varepsilon
-)SPE generating a payoff vector between
\bar{x}
and
\bar{y}
by:

•

guessing a requirement
\lambda
;

•

checking that there exists a
\lambda
-consistent play in the game
\mathcal{G}
that generates a payoff vector between
\bar{x}
and
\bar{y}
;

•

checking that
\lambda
is an (
\varepsilon
-)fixed point of the negotiation function.

The last point will usually be the one that induces more substantial work, since it requires a general method to compute the negotiation function.
Such a method will be provided by tools called
negotiation games
.

4.4  Negotiation games

4.4.1 The abstract negotiation game

Given a requirement
\lambda
and a vertex
v_{0}\in V
, the quantity
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v_{0})
can be characterized as the value of a
negotiation game
, a two-player zero-sum game opposing the player
Prover
, who simulates a
\lambda
-rational strategy profile and wants to minimize player
i
’s payoff (where
i
is the player controlling
v_{0}
), and the player
Challenger
, who simulates player
i
’s reaction by accepting or refusing Prover’s proposals.
First defined by János Flesch and Arkadi Predtetchinski
[
DBLP:journals/mor/FleschP17
]
(without being linked to the concept of negotiation function), the
abstract negotiation game
, written
\mathsf{abs}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
unfolds as follows.

•

From the vertex
v_{0}
, Prover chooses a
\lambda
-consistent play
\pi
and proposes it to Challenger.
If Prover has no play to propose, the game is over and Challenger gets the payoff
+\infty
.

•

Once a play
\pi
has been proposed, Challenger can accept it.
Or he can
deviate
, and choose a prefix
\pi_{\leq k}
with
\pi_{k}\in V_{i}
and a new edge
\pi_{k}v\in E
.

•

In the former case, the game is over.
In the latter, it starts again from the vertex
v
.

In the play that Prover and Challenger construct together,
Challenger’s objective consists in maximizing player
i
’s payoff, and Prover’s objective in minimizing it.
More formally, the abstract negotiation game
\mathsf{abs}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
is a game with an uncountable vertex space, defined as follows.

Definition

26

(Abstract negotiation game)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
be a game, let
i\in\Pi
, and let
\lambda
be a requirement on
\mathcal{G}
. The
abstract negotiation game
of
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
for player
i
with requirement
\lambda
is the two-player zero-sum game:

\mathsf{abs}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}=\left(\{\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{C}\},V^{\mathsf{a}},(V^{\mathsf{a}}_{\mathfrak{P}},V^{\mathsf{a}}_{\mathfrak{C}}),E^{\mathsf{a}},\mu^{\mathsf{a}}\right)_{\upharpoonright v_{0}},

where:

•

\mathfrak{P}
denotes the player
Prover
and
\mathfrak{C}
the player
Challenger
.

•

Prover’s vertices are the vertices of
\mathcal{G}
, and two special vertices
\top
and
\bot
.

•

Challenger’s vertices are the vertices of the form
[hv]
, where
hv
can be any history in the game
\mathcal{G}
, and the vertices of the form
[\pi]
, where
\pi
is a
\lambda
-consistent play in the game
\mathcal{G}
.

•

From the vertex
\bot
, the only available edge is the self-loop to
\bot
, and similarly from
\top
.
From a vertex of the form
v
, Prover can move to:

–

any vertex
[\pi]
where
\pi
starts from
v
(she proposes the play
\pi
);

–

the vertex
\bot
(she gives up, last option if she cannot propose any
\lambda
-consistent play).

•

From a vertex of the form
[\pi]
, Challenger can move to the vertex
\top
(he accepts the play
\pi
), or to any vertex of the form
[\pi_{\leq k}v]
, where
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\pi_{k}\in V_{i}
and
v\neq\pi_{k+1}
(he deviates to
v
).
From a vertex of the form
[hv]
