, he can only go to the vertex
v
.

•

In a play
\chi
in this game, if Prover gives up and takes an edge to the vertex
\top
, Challenger’s payoff is defined by
\mu^{\mathsf{a}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\chi)=+\infty
.
If Challenger finally accepts a proposal
\pi
, and takes the edge
[\pi]\top
, then his payoff is defined by
\mu^{\mathsf{a}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\chi)=\mu_{i}(\pi)
.
If he deviates infinitely often, then Prover’s proposals and his deviations construct a play
\dot{\chi}=\pi^{(0)}_{\leq k_{0}}\pi^{(1)}_{\leq k_{1}}\pi^{(2)}_{\leq k_{2}}\dots
.
Then, Challenger’s payoff is defined by
\mu^{\mathsf{a}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\chi)=\mu_{i}(\dot{\chi})
.
In all those cases, Prover’s payoff is the opposite of Challenger’s one.

The abstract negotiation game constitutes an alternative definition of the negotiation function.
Note that the following theorem does not use the notation
\mathsf{val}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, because the abstract negotiation game is not guaranteed to be Borel.

Theorem

9
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
be a game, let
\lambda
be a requirement on
\mathcal{G}
and let
i\in\Pi
be such that
v_{0}\in V_{i}
.
Then, we have:

\infop_{\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}\in\mathsf{Strat}_{\mathfrak{P}}\mathsf{abs}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}}~\supop_{\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}\in\mathsf{Strat}_{\mathfrak{C}}\mathsf{abs}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}}~\mu^{\mathsf{a}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle=\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v_{0}).

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, and let us prove that the following statements are equivalent.

1.

There exists a strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
such that for every strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
, we have
\mu^{\mathsf{a}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle<\alpha
.

2.

There exists a
\lambda
-rational strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
such that for every strategy
\sigma_{i}
, we have
\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle<\alpha
.

\blacktriangleright
Assertion
1
implies Assertion
2
.

Let
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
be such that for every strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
, we have
\mu^{\mathsf{a}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle<\alpha
.

In what follows, any history
h
compatible with an already defined strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
will be decomposed in:

h=v_{0}h^{(0)}v_{1}h^{(1)}\dots h^{(n-1)}v_{n}h^{(n)},

so that there exist plays
\pi^{(0)},\dots,\pi^{(n-1)},\chi
and a history:

[v_{0}]\left[\pi^{(0)}\right]\left[v_{1}h^{(1)}v_{2}\right]\dots\left[v_{n-1}h^{(n-1)}v_{n}\right]\left[v_{n}h^{(n)}\chi\right]

in the game
\mathsf{abs}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{v_{0}}
compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
: the existence and the unicity of that decomposition can be proved by induction. Intuitively, the history
h
is cut in histories which are prefixes of plays that can be proposed by Prover.

Then, let us define inductively the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
by
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}(h)=\chi_{0}
, with
\chi
defined from
h
as above, for every
h
such that
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
has been defined on the prefixes of
h
, and such that the last vertex of
h
is not controlled by player
i
.
Let us prove that
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
is the desired strategy profile.

The strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
is
\lambda
-rational.

Let us define
\sigma_{i}
so that for every history
hv
compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
, the play
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}\rangle
is
\lambda
-consistent.

For each history:

h=v_{0}h^{(0)}v_{1}h^{(1)}\dots h^{(n-1)}v_{n}h^{(n)}

compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
and ending in
V_{i}
, let
\sigma_{i}(h)=\chi_{0}
with
\chi
corresponding to the decomposition of
h
, so that by induction:

\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}h^{(0)}v_{1}h^{(1)}\dots h^{(n-1)}v_{n}}\rangle=v_{n}h^{(n)}\chi.

Let now
hv
be a history in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, and let us show that the play
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}\rangle
is
\lambda
-consistent. If we decompose:

hv=v_{0}h^{(0)}v_{1}h^{(1)}\dots h^{(n-1)}v_{n}h^{(n)}

with the same definition of
\chi
(note that the vertex
v
is now included in the decomposition), then we have
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}\rangle=v\chi
, and by definition of the abstract negotiation game, the play
v_{n}h^{(n)}\chi
is
\lambda
-consistent, and therefore so is the play
v\chi
.

The strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
keeps player
i
’s payoff under the value
\alpha
.

Let
\sigma_{i}
be some strategy for player
i
, and let
\pi=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
. We want to prove that
\mu_{i}(\pi)<\alpha
.

Let us define two finite or infinite sequences
\left(\pi^{(k)}\right)_{k\in K}
and
\left(h^{(k)}v_{k}\right)_{k\in K}
, where
K=\{1,\dots,n\}
or
K=\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
, by for every
k\in K
:

\left[\pi^{(k)}\right]=\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}\left([v_{0}]\left[\pi^{(0)}\right]\dots\left[\pi^{(k-1)}\right]\left[h^{(k)}v_{k}\right]\right)

and so that for every
k
, the history
h^{(k)}v_{k}
is the shortest prefix of
\pi
that is not a prefix of
h^{(1)}\dots h^{(k-1)}\pi^{(k-1)}
(or equivalently, the history
h^{(k)}
is the longest common prefix of
\pi
and
h^{(1)}\dots h^{(k-1)}\pi^{(k-1)}
).

Then, the length of the longest common prefix of
h^{(1)}\dots h^{(k-1)}\pi^{(k)}
and
\pi
increases with
k
, and the set
K
is finite if and only if there exists
n
such that
h^{(1)}\dots h^{(n-1)}\pi^{(n)}=\pi
.

In the infinite case, let:

\chi=[v_{0}]\left[\pi^{(0)}\right]\left[h^{(1)}v_{1}\right]\dots\left[\pi^{(k)}\right]\left[h^{(k)}v_{k}\right]\dots.

The play
\chi
is compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
, hence
\mu^{\mathsf{a}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\chi)<\alpha
, that is to say:

\mu_{i}\left(h^{(1)}h^{(2)}\dots\right)<\alpha,

i.e.
\mu_{i}(\pi)<\alpha
.

In the finite case, let:

\chi=[v_{0}]\left[\pi^{(0)}\right]\left[h^{(1)}v_{1}\right]\dots\left[\pi^{(n)}\right]\top^{\omega}.

For the same reason, we have
\mu^{\mathsf{a}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\chi)<\alpha
, i.e., we have
\mu_{i}\left(h^{(1)}\dots h^{(n)}\pi^{(n)}\right)=\mu_{i}(\pi)<\alpha
.

\blacktriangleright
Assertion
2
implies Assertion
1
.

Let
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
be a strategy profile that is
\lambda
-rational assuming a strategy
\sigma_{i}
, and that maintains player
i
’s payoff below the quantity
\alpha
.
Let us define a strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
for Prover in the abstract negotiation game.

Let
g=[v_{0}]\left[\pi^{(0)}\right]\left[h^{(1)}v_{1}\right]\left[\pi^{(1)}\right]\dots\left[h^{(n)}v_{n}\right]
be a history in the abstract game, ending in
V^{\mathsf{a}}_{\mathfrak{P}}
. Then, we define:

\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}(g)=\left[\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h^{(1)}\dots h^{(n)}v_{n}}\rangle\right].

If
g
is a history ending in
\top
, then we define
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}(g)=\top
, and similarly, if
g
ends in
\bot
, we define
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}(g)=\bot
.

Let us show that
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
is the strategy we were looking for.
Let
\chi
be a play compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
. Let us first note that the vertex
\bot
cannot appear in
\chi
. Then, the play
\chi
can only have two forms: a play in which Challenger eventually accepts Prover’s proposal, or an infinite sequence of proposals and deviations.

If Challenger eventually accepts Prover’s proposal.

If
\chi=[v_{0}]\left[\pi^{(0)}\right]\left[h^{(1)}v_{1}\right]\dots\left[\pi^{(n)}\right]\top^{\omega}
, then we have:

\pi^{(n)}=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h^{(1)}\dots h^{(n)}v_{n}}\rangle,

and the history
h^{(1)}\dots h^{(n)}v_{n}
in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
is compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
. By hypothesis, we have:

\mu_{i}\left(h^{(1)}\dots h^{(n)}\pi^{(n)}\right)<\alpha,

hence
\mu^{\mathsf{a}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\chi)<\alpha
.

If Challenger deviates infinitely often.

If
\chi=[v_{0}]\left[\pi^{(0)}\right]\dots\left[h^{(n)}v_{n}\right]\left[\pi^{(n)}\right]\dots
, then the play
\pi=h^{(1)}h^{(2)}\dots
is compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
, and by hypothesis we have
\mu_{i}(\pi)<\alpha
, hence
\mu^{\mathsf{a}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\chi)<\alpha
.
∎

Of course, the abstract negotiation game does not provide a straightforward way to compute the negotiation function, since its vertex space is infinite, and even uncountable, in the general case.
However, it gives a first intuition.

Example

6
.

Let us consider again the game depicted by Figure
13
, and consider the requirement
\lambda_{1}
, defined by
\lambda_{1}(a)=0
, and
\lambda_{1}(b)=\lambda_{1}(c)=1
.
From each of the three vertices, let us present a play of the abstract negotiation game where both Prover and Challenger play optimally.

From the vertex
c
, Prover proposes the play
c^{\omega}
, and Challenger cannot deviate from it: he may deviate from any vertex controlled by player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
, which is the case of the only vertex traversed by the play, but he has no alternative edge to use.
Therefore, he accepts the play, and gets the payoff
1
, hence
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{2})(c)=1
.

From the vertex
b
, Prover is not allowed to propose the play
b^{\omega}
, which is not
\lambda_{1}
-consistent (player
\square
should get the payoff
1
, and gets only the payoff
0
).
Therefore, she proposes the play
bc^{\omega}
, and Challenger accepts, hence
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{1})(b)=1
.

From the vertex
a
, Prover may propose the play
a^{\omega}
.
But then, Challenger can deviate to the vertex
b
, and from there, again, Prover has to propose a play that eventually reaches the vertex
c
, providing player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
, and therefore Challenger, the payoff
1
, hence
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{1})(a)=1
.

Thus, checking that a given requirement
\lambda
is an (
\varepsilon
-)fixed point of the negotiation function can be done by guessing a finite representation of a strategy for Prover in the abstract negotiation game, from each vertex
u
, that forces the player controlling the vertex
u
to get, at most, the payoff
\lambda(u)
(or
\lambda(u)+\varepsilon
).
Sometimes (when
\varepsilon
is clear from the context), we will abuse language and call such a strategy
winning
, assimilating the abstract negotiation game with a Boolean version in which Prover’s goal is to keep Challenger’s payoff under
\lambda(u)
(or
\lambda(u)+\varepsilon
)—
proving
, then, that
\lambda
is an (
\varepsilon
-)fixed point of the function
\mathsf{nego}
.
To obtain effective algorithms from that idea, we need therefore to prove that if such a strategy exists, there exists one that is simple, i.e. that has a finite, and small, representation.

4.4.2 The concrete negotiation game.

In the general case, the use that can be made of the abstract negotiation game is limited by its infinite (and uncountable) vertex space.
However, under the hypothesis that the game
\mathcal{G}
is prefix-independent, it can be turned into a game on a finite graph if Prover does not propose plays as a whole, but edge by edge.
In the
concrete negotiation game

\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}^{\mathsf{c}}}
, the vertices controlled by Prover have the form
(v,M)
, where
M\subseteq V
memorizes the vertices seen since the last time Challenger did deviate, in order to control that the play Prover is constructing from that point is
\lambda
-consistent: for each
u\in M
, Prover has to give to the player controlling
u
at least the payoff
\lambda(u)
.
Similarly, the vertices controlled by Challenger are of the form
(vv^{\prime},M)
, where
vv^{\prime}\in E
is an edge proposed by Prover.
The game unfolds as follows, initially from the vertex
v_{0}^{\mathsf{c}}=(v_{0},\{v_{0}\})
.

•

From the vertex
(v,M)
, Prover chooses an edge
vv^{\prime}
and proposes it to Challenger.
She therefore moves to Challenger’s vertex
(vv^{\prime},M)
.

•

Once an edge
vv^{\prime}
has been proposed, Challenger can accept it, moving to Prover’s vertex
(v^{\prime},M\cup\{v^{\prime}\})
.
Or, if
v\in V_{i}
, he can
deviate
, and choose a new edge
vw
, moving then to Prover’s vertex
(w,\{w\})
.

•

Then, the game starts again from that new vertex.

Again, those proposals and deviations draw a play in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, in which Challenger intends to maximize player
i
’s payoff, while Prover intends to minimize it.
We give a formal definition below.

Definition

27

(Concrete negotiation game)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a prefix-independent game played on a finite graph, let
i\in\Pi
and
v_{0}\in V_{i}
, and let
\lambda
be a requirement on
\mathcal{G}
.
The
concrete negotiation game
of
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
is the two-player zero-sum game
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v^{\mathsf{c}}_{0}}=\left(\{\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{C}\},V^{\mathsf{c}},(V^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{P}},V^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}),E^{\mathsf{c}},\mu^{\mathsf{c}}\right)_{\upharpoonright v^{\mathsf{c}}_{0}}
, defined as follows:

•

player
\mathfrak{P}
is called
Prover
, and player
\mathfrak{C}
is called
Challenger
.

•

The set of vertices controlled by Prover is
V^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{P}}=V\times 2^{V}
, where the vertex
v^{\mathsf{c}}=(v,M)
contains the information of the current vertex
v
on which Prover has to define the strategy profile, and the
memory

M
of the vertices that have been traversed so far since the last deviation, defining the requirements Prover has to satisfy.
The initial vertex is
v^{\mathsf{c}}_{0}=(v_{0},\{v_{0}\})
.

•

The set of vertices controlled by Challenger is
V^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}=E\times 2^{V}
, where in the vertex
v^{\mathsf{c}}=(uv,M)
, the edge
uv
is the edge proposed by Prover.

•

The set
E^{\mathsf{c}}
contains three types of edges:
proposals
,
acceptations
and
deviations
.

–

Proposals are edges in which Prover proposes an edge of the game
\mathcal{G}
:

\mathsf{Prop}=\left\{(v,M)(vv^{\prime},M)~\left|~vv^{\prime}\in E,M\in 2^{V}\right.\right\}.

–

Acceptations are edges in which Challenger accepts to follow the edge proposed by Prover (it is in particular his only possibility when that edge begins on a vertex that is not controlled by player
i
):

\mathsf{Acc}=\left\{(vv^{\prime},M)\left(v^{\prime},M\cup\{v^{\prime}\}\right)~\left|~vv^{\prime}\in E,M\in 2^{V}\right.\right\}.

Note that the memory is updated.

–

Deviations are edges in which Challenger refuses to follow the edge proposed by Prover, as he can if that edge begins in a vertex controlled by player
i
.
The memory is then erased, and only the new vertex the deviating edge leads to is memorized:

\mathsf{Dev}=\left\{(vv^{\prime},M)(w,\{w\})~\left|~v\in V_{i},w\neq v^{\prime},vv^{\prime},vw\in E,M\in 2^{V}\right.\right\}.

•

Let
g=(h_{0},M_{0})(h_{0}h^{\prime}_{0},M_{0})\dots(h_{n}h^{\prime}_{n},M_{n})
be a history in
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})
: the
projection
of the history
g
is the history
\dot{g}=h_{0}\dots h_{n}
in the game
\mathcal{G}
.
That definition is naturally extended to plays.

•

The payoff function
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}=-\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{P}}
measures player
i
’s payoff, with a winning condition if the constructed strategy profile is not
\lambda
-rational, that is to say if after finitely many player
i
’s deviations, it generates a play which is not
\lambda
-consistent:

–

we define
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)=+\infty
if after some index
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the play
\pi_{\geq 2n}
contains no deviation, and if the projection
\dot{\pi}_{\geq n}
is not
\lambda
-consistent
1
1

1

When we combine the notations
\dot{\pi}
and
\pi_{\geq n}
, the notation
\dot{\pi}
is applied first; that is, the play
\dot{\pi}_{\geq n}
is the projection of the play
\pi_{\geq 2n}
, not
\pi_{\geq n}
.
;

–

and
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)=\mu_{i}(\dot{\pi})
otherwise.

Like in the abstract negotiation game, the goal of Challenger is to find a
\lambda
-rational strategy profile that forces the worst possible payoff for player
i
, and the goal of Prover is to find a possibly deviating strategy for player
i
that gives them the highest possible payoff.

Remark

6
.

The concrete negotiation game has the following properties.

•

If the game
\mathcal{G}
is Borel, then the game
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})
is Borel.

•

When
\pi_{\geq 2n}
contains no deviation, the memory of its vertices is increasing, and therefore eventually equal to the memory
M=\mathsf{Occ}(\dot{\pi}_{\geq n})
.
If it is the longest such suffix of
\pi
, it means that the projection
\dot{\pi}_{\geq n}
is
\lambda
-consistent if and only if for each player
j
and each vertex
v\in V_{j}
, we have
\mu_{j}(\dot{\pi})\geq\lambda(v)
.

We can now prove the equivalent of
9
.
For convenience, we prove it only when the game
\mathcal{G}
is Borel.

Theorem

10
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Borel prefix-independent game played on a finite graph.
Let
\lambda
be a requirement, let
i
be a player and let
v_{0}\in V_{i}
.
Then, we have the equality
\mathsf{val}_{\mathfrak{C}}\left(\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v^{\mathsf{c}}_{0}}\right)=\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v_{0})
.
Moreover, if for each player
i
and every state
v_{0}\in V_{i}
, Prover has an optimal strategy in
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}^{\mathsf{c}}}
, then
\mathcal{G}
is a game with steady negotiation.

Proof.

\blacktriangleright
First direction: the inequality
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v_{0})\leq\mathsf{val}_{\mathfrak{C}}\left(\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v^{\mathsf{c}}_{0}}\right)
.

Let
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
be a strategy such that
\supop_{\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}}\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle\neq+\infty
, and let us define inductively the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
as follows: for every history
h\in\mathsf{Hist}_{-i}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
, there exists (by induction) exactly one history
g
compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
such that
\dot{g}=h
.
Let then
{\bar{\sigma}}(\dot{g})=w
for every history
g
compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
with
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}(g)=(vw,M)
for some
M
.
The strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is arbitrarily defined on other histories.
We prove that the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
is
\lambda
-rational assuming the strategy
\sigma_{i}
, and that
\supop_{\sigma^{\prime}_{i}}\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle\leq\supop_{\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}}\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle
.

The strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
is
\lambda
-rational, assuming the strategy
\sigma_{i}
.

Indeed, let us assume it is not.
Then, there exists a history
h=h_{0}\dots h_{n}
in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
such that the play
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h}\rangle
is not
\lambda
-consistent.
Then, let:

gv^{\mathsf{c}}=\left(h_{0},M_{0}\right)\left(h_{0}{\bar{\sigma}}(h_{0}),M_{0}\right)\dots\left(h_{n},M_{n}\right)

be the only history in
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v^{\mathsf{c}}_{0}}
compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
such that
\dot{g}=h
.
Let
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
be a strategy constructing the history
h
, defined by:

\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}\left(g_{0}\dots g_{2k-1}\right)=g_{2k}

for every
k
, and:

\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}\left(g^{\prime}(vw,M)\right)=(w,M\cup\{w\})

for any other history
g^{\prime}(vw,M)
.
Then, the play
\pi=\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle
contains finitely many deviations (Challenger stops the deviations after having constructed the history
h
), and the projection
\dot{\pi}_{\geq n}
is not
\lambda
-consistent. Therefore, we have
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)=+\infty
, which is false by hypothesis.

The inequality
\supop_{\sigma^{\prime}_{i}}\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle\leq\supop_{\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}}\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle
holds.

Let
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
be a strategy for player
i
, and let
\pi=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle
.
Let
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
be a strategy such that for every
k
:

\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}\left((\pi_{0},\cdot)(\pi_{0}\cdot,\cdot)\dots(\pi_{k}\cdot,\cdot)\right)=(\pi_{k+1},\cdot),

i.e. a strategy forcing the play
\pi
against
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
.
Then, since
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle\neq+\infty
by hypothesis on
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
, we have
\mu_{i}(\pi)=\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle
, hence
\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle\leq\supop_{\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}}\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle
, hence the desired inequality.

Steady negotiation.

Moreover, if
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
is optimal, then the
\lambda
-rational strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
realizes the infimum:

\infop_{{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}\in\lambda\mathsf{Rat}(v_{0})}\supop_{\sigma^{\prime}_{i}}\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle,

hence if there exists such an optimal strategy for every vertex
v_{0}
, then the game
\mathcal{G}
is with steady negotiation.

\blacktriangleright
Second direction: the inequality
\mathsf{val}_{\mathfrak{C}}\left(\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v^{\mathsf{c}}_{0}}\right)\leq\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v_{0})
.

Let
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
be a
\lambda
-rational strategy profile from
v_{0}
, assuming the strategy
\sigma_{i}
; let us define a strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
, by
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}(g(v,\cdot))=\left(v{\bar{\sigma}}(\dot{g}v),\cdot\right)
for every history
g
and for every
v\in V
.
Let us prove the inequality
\supop_{\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}}~\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle\leq\supop_{\sigma^{\prime}_{i}}~\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle.

Let
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
be a strategy for Challenger, and let
\pi=\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle
.
If
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)=+\infty
, then there exists
n
such that the play
\pi_{\geq 2n}
contains no deviation, i.e.
\dot{\pi}_{\geq n}=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright\dot{\pi}_{\leq n}}\rangle
, and that play is not
\lambda
-consistent, which is impossible.
Therefore, we have
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)\neq+\infty
, and as a consequence
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)=\mu_{i}(\dot{\pi})=\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle
for some strategy
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
, hence
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)\leq\supop_{\sigma^{\prime}_{i}}\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle
, hence the desired inequality.
∎

Example

7
.

Consider again the game of Figure
13
.
The arena of the concrete negotiation game from the vertex
a
is depicted by Figure
16
.
The blue vertices belong to Prover, the orange ones to Challenger.
The dashed arrows depict the deviations, and optimal strategies for both Prover and Challenger, for the requirement
\lambda_{1}
defined by
\lambda_{1}(a)=0
and
\lambda_{1}(b)=\lambda_{1}(c)=1
, are defined by the thick arrows.

a,\{a\}
ab,\{a\}
aa,\{a\}
b,\{a,b\}
b,\{b\}
bb,\{a,b\}
bb,\{b\}
bc,\{a,b\}
bc,\{b\}

c,\{b,c\}
cc,\{a,b,c\}
cc,\{b,c\}

Figure 16
:
A concrete negotiation game

Contrary to the abstract negotiation game, the concrete negotiation game has a finite vertex space.
However, the size of that vertex space is exponential in the size of the original game: constructing that game and solving it is therefore costly.
The concrete negotiation game will nevertheless be used to prove the fixed-parameter tractability of the SPE constrained existence problem in parity games (
12
), and to establish an intermediary result about the fixed points of the negotiation function in mean-payoff games (
16
).

Chapter 5:  Parity games

In this chapter, we use the tools given in
chapter

4
to prove
\mathsf{NP}
-completeness for the constrained existence problem of SPEs in parity games.
Note that, here, we are only interested in SPEs and not
\varepsilon
-SPEs, since parity games are Boolean games, in which the notion of
\varepsilon
-SPE has little relevance.

In order to design an efficient algorithm for that problem, we define an equivalence relation between histories and between plays; and, then, we show that in the abstract negotiation game, Prover can propose only plays that are simple representatives of their equivalence class, and propose always the same play from each vertex.

5.1  Reduced plays and reduced strategies

5.1.1 Definitions

The equivalence relation that we use is based on the order in which vertices appear.

Definition

28

(Occurrence-equivalence)
.

Two histories
h
and
h^{\prime}
are
occurrence-equivalent
, written
h\approx h^{\prime}
, if and only if
\mathsf{first}(h)=\mathsf{first}(h^{\prime})
,
\mathsf{last}(h)=\mathsf{last}(h^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{Occ}(h)=\mathsf{Occ}(h^{\prime})
.

Two plays
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
are
occurrence-equivalent
, written
\pi\approx\pi^{\prime}
, if and only if the three following conditions are satisfied:

•

we have
\mathsf{Inf}(\pi)=\mathsf{Inf}(\pi^{\prime})
;

•

for each history prefix of
\pi
, there exists a occurrence-equivalent history prefix of
\pi^{\prime}
;

•

for each history prefix of
\pi^{\prime}
, there exists a occurrence-equivalent history prefix of
\pi
.

Example

8
.

a
b
c
d
e
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Diamond}}{{1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Diamond}}{{1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Diamond}}{{1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Diamond}}{{1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Diamond}}{{0}}
(\lambda^{*})
0
1
1
0
0

Figure 17
:
A Büchi game.

Let us consider the game of Figure
17
.
In that game, the play
ab(cdce)^{\omega}
is occurrence-equivalent to the play
abcd(cdce)^{\omega}
, but not to the play
ab(cecd)^{\omega}
.
Indeed, the latter has the history
abce
as a prefix, which is not occurrence-equivalent to any prefix of
ab(cdce)^{\omega}
, in which the vertex
e
occurs only when the vertex
d
has already occurred.

Remark

7
.

The operators
\mathsf{Occ}
and
\mathsf{Inf}
, and any parity payoff function, are stable by occurrence-equivalence.

5.1.2 Representatives

The interest of that equivalence relation lies in the finite number of its equivalence classes, and by the existence of simple representatives for each of them.

Lemma

6
.

Let
\pi
be a play of
\mathcal{G}
.
There exists a lasso
hc^{\omega}\approx\pi
where the history
h
has length
|h|\leq n^{3}+n^{2}
and the cycle
c
has length
|c|\leq n^{2}
, where
n=\mathsf{card}V
.

Proof.

Let us write
W_{0}\subset\dots\subset W_{t}
for all the sets of the form
\mathsf{Occ}(\pi_{\leq k})
with
k\in\mathbb{N}
, without repetition.
Note that for each index
s\in\{0,\dots,t-1\}
, the set
W_{s+1}
contains the set
W_{s}
plus one additional vertex.

Let us construct the history
h
and the cycle
c
as follows, maintaining the hypothesis that for all
p
, the set
\mathsf{Occ}(h_{\leq p})
is equal to
W_{s}
for some
s
.

Base case.

First, we define
h_{0}=\pi_{0}
, and
\{h_{0}\}=W_{0}
.

Inductive case.

Then, when the prefix
h_{\leq p}
is constructed: let
s
be such that
\mathsf{Occ}(h_{\leq p})=W_{s}
, and let
k
be the minimal integer such that
\mathsf{Occ}(\pi_{\leq k})=W_{s}
.
Let
U
be the set of all the vertices
u
such that there exists
\ell
with
\mathsf{Occ}(\pi_{\leq\ell})=W_{s}
and
\pi_{\ell}=u
: any such
\ell
is greater than or equal to
k
, and
U\subseteq W_{s}
. Then, there exists at least one path from
\pi_{k}=h_{p}
that traverses all the vertices of
U
and only them. Let
h_{p}\dots h_{q}
be such a path with minimal length: it has at most length
n^{2}
.
If
s<t
, let now
\ell
be the minimal index greater than
k
such that
\mathsf{Occ}(\pi_{\leq\ell})=W_{s+1}
. Then, there exists a path from
h_{q}\in W_{s}
to
\pi_{\ell}
that uses only vertices of
U
: let
h_{q}\dots h_{r}
be such a path with minimal length. Then, it traverses all vertices at most once, and has therefore length at most
n
.
If
s=t
, let
h_{q}\dots h_{r}
be a path of minimal length from
h_{q}
to a vertex
h_{r}\in\mathsf{Inf}(\pi)
: for the same reasons as above, such a path exists and has length at most
n
. Then, we can stop here the construction of
h
, and observe that the vertex
h_{r}
belongs to the graph
(\mathsf{Inf}(\pi),E\cap\mathsf{Inf}(\pi)^{2})
, which is strongly connected. We can therefore choose a cycle
c
that traverses all its vertices and only them, and that has length at most
n^{2}
.

Conclusion.

By construction, the lasso
hc^{\omega}
is occurrence-equivalent to
\pi
, and satisfies the desired size conditions.
∎

We call such lassos
reduced plays
.
For each play
\pi
, we write
\tilde{\pi}
for an arbitrary occurrence-equivalent reduced play.
Then, given
\mathcal{G}
and a reduced play
\tilde{\pi}
, operations such as computing the vector
\mu(\tilde{\pi})
, the sets
\mathsf{Occ}(\tilde{\pi})
and
\mathsf{Inf}(\tilde{\pi})
, or checking whether the play
\tilde{\pi}
is
\lambda
-consistent, can be done in time
O(n^{3})
.

Example

9
.

In the game of Figure
17
, the play
ab(cdce)^{\omega}
is reduced, but the occurrence-equivalent play
ab^{150}\left(cdce\right)^{\omega}
is not.

Definition

29

(Reduced strategy)
.

A strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
for Prover in
\mathsf{abs}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})
is
reduced
if and only if it is stationary, and for each vertex
v
, the play
\pi
with
[\pi]=\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}([v])
is a reduced play.

If we have
\pi\approx\pi^{\prime}
, and if Challenger can deviate from the play
\pi
after the history
hv
, then he can also deviate in
\pi^{\prime}
after some history
h^{\prime}v
that traverses the same vertices.
Thus, Prover can play optimally while proposing only reduced plays, and by proposing always the same play from each vertex; that is, by following a reduced strategy.
Note that since we will use the abstract negotiation game to check whether a given requirement is a fixed point of the negotiation function, we abuse language here by considering that Prover
wins
if she maintains player
i
’s payoff below
\lambda(v_{0})
, even though the abstract negotiation game is not exactly Boolean.

Lemma

7
.

Prover has a winning strategy in the abstract negotiation game if and only if she has a reduced one.

Proof.

Let us consider the
reduced negotiation game
, i.e. the abstract negotiation game in which one would have removed all the vertices but:

–

Prover’s vertices, i.e., the set
V^{\mathsf{a}}_{\mathfrak{P}}=V\cup\{\top,\bot\}
;

–

those of the form
[\tilde{\pi}]
, where
\tilde{\pi}
is a reduced play;

–

those of the form
[hv]
, where
h
is a prefix of a
\lambda
-consistent reduced play
\tilde{\pi}
, and has minimal length among the occurrence-equivalent prefixes of
\tilde{\pi}
.

If the game
\mathcal{G}
has
n
vertices, then this game has at most
n^{n^{3}+2n^{2}}(n^{3}+3n^{2})+1
vertices.
Let us now notice that it has another interesting property.

Sublemma

1
.

In the reduced negotiation game, either Prover or Challenger has a stationary winning strategy.

Proof.

By Lemma
2
(and since the reduced game is Borel), that is the case if both Prover’s and Challenger’s objectives are convex.
Let therefore
\pi
and
\chi
be two plays in the reduced negotiation game, and
\xi
be a shuffling of
\pi
and
\chi
.
Then, we observe that the play
\dot{\xi}
is also a shuffling of the plays
\dot{\pi}
and
\dot{\chi}
.
The convexity of Prover’s and Challenger’s objectives is then a consequence of the convexity of parity objectives: the minimal color seen infinitely often by player
i
in
\dot{\xi}
is the minimum of the minimal colors seen infinitely often in
\dot{\pi}
and in
\dot{\chi}
.
∎

We can now prove our lemma by using the equivalence between the abstract and the reduced negotiation game.

If Prover has a winning strategy in the abstract negotiation game, she has one in the reduced negotiation game.

We proceed by contraposition: if Prover has no winning strategy in the reduced game, then, by
1
, Challenger has a stationary one: let us write it
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
.

Now, let us extend
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
into a stationary winning strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}^{\mathsf{a}}
in the abstract negotiation game.

Let
[\pi]\in V^{\mathsf{a}}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, and let
[hvw]=\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}([\tilde{\pi}])
.
By occurrence-equivalence, there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\pi_{k}=v
, and
\mathsf{Occ}(\pi_{\leq k})=\mathsf{Occ}(hv)
.
We then set
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}^{\mathsf{a}}([\pi])=[\pi_{\leq k}w]
.
Note that the vertices
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}^{\mathsf{a}}([\pi])=[\pi_{\leq k}w]
and
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}([\tilde{\pi}])=[hvw]
can be different, but they both have as unique successor the vertex
[w]
.

Let us prove, now, that
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}^{\mathsf{a}}
is winning: let
\chi^{\mathsf{a}}
be a play compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}^{\mathsf{a}}
.
When Prover proposes a play
\pi
, in the abstract game, against the strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}^{\mathsf{a}}
, and when she proposes the play
\tilde{\pi}
in the reduced game against the strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
, the same thing happens in both cases: either Challenger accepts in both games, or he deviates, and Prover has to propose a new play from the same vertex
w
.
Therefore, we can define from
\chi^{\mathsf{a}}
a play
\chi
compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
in the reduced game, in which each Challenger’s vertex
[\pi]
is replaced by the vertex
[\tilde{\pi}]
, and Prover’s vertices are replaced accordingly.

Since the play
\chi
is compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
, it is winning for Challenger: let us then prove that so is
\chi^{\mathsf{a}}
.
If
\chi^{\mathsf{a}}
has the form
g[\pi]\top^{\omega}
, then
\chi
has the form
g^{\prime}[\tilde{\pi}]\top^{\omega}
, hence
\tilde{\pi}
is winning for player
i
, and therefore
\pi
is winning for player
i
and
\chi^{\mathsf{a}}
is winning for Challenger.
If
\chi
never reaches the vertex
\top
, then we have
\dot{\chi}=h^{0}h^{1}\dots
and
\dot{\chi}^{\mathsf{a}}=h^{0\mathsf{a}}h^{1\mathsf{a}}\dots
where, for every
k
, the histories
h^{k}
and
h^{k\mathsf{a}}
are possibly different, but contain exactly the same vertices.
Then, the set of player
i
’s colors appearing infinitely often in
\dot{\chi}
and
\dot{\chi}^{\mathsf{a}}
are the same, and the play
\dot{\chi}^{\mathsf{a}}
is winning for player
i
, i.e. the play
\chi^{\mathsf{a}}
is winning for Challenger: the strategy
\tau^{\mathsf{a}}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is winning.

If Prover has a winning strategy in the reduced negotiation game, she has a reduced one in the abstract negotiation game.

Indeed, let
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
be a winning strategy for Prover in the reduced negotiation game.
As said above, we can assume that
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
is stationary.
Since in the abstract game, the only vertices controlled by Prover where she has several possible choices are the ones of the form
[v]
, for
v\in V
, we can see
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
as a reduced strategy in the abstract game: let us write it
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}^{\mathsf{a}}
in that case.
We now have to prove that
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}^{\mathsf{a}}
is also a winning strategy.

Let
\chi^{\mathsf{a}}
be a play in the abstract negotiation game compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}^{\mathsf{a}}
.
For any sequence of vertices
[v][\tilde{\pi}][hw][w]
that appears in
\chi^{\mathsf{a}}
, the history
h
is occurrence-equivalent to some prefix
\tilde{h}
of
\tilde{\pi}
such that
[\tilde{h}w]
is a vertex of the reduced game.
Therefore, we can transform the play
\chi^{\mathsf{a}}
into a play
\chi
of the reduced game, compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
, where each vertex of the form
[hw]
have been replaced by
[\tilde{h}w]
.
Since
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
is a winning strategy in the reduced negotiation game, the play
\chi
is winning for Prover.
Let us prove that so is
\chi^{\mathsf{a}}
.

If
\chi^{\mathsf{a}}
has the form
g[\tilde{\pi}]\top^{\omega}
, then
\chi
has the form
g^{\prime}[\tilde{\pi}]\top^{\omega}
, and since
\chi
is winning for Prover, the play
\tilde{\pi}
is losing for player
i
, and therefore the play
\chi^{\mathsf{a}}
is winning for Prover.
If
\chi^{\mathsf{a}}
has the form:

\chi^{\mathsf{a}}=[v_{0}][\tilde{\pi}^{0}][h^{0}v_{1}][v_{1}][\tilde{\pi}^{1}][h^{1}v_{2}]\dots

then we have:

\chi=[v_{0}][\tilde{\pi}^{0}][\tilde{h}^{0}v_{1}][v_{1}][\tilde{\pi}^{1}][\tilde{h}^{1}v_{2}]\dots

and since for each
k
, we have
\mathsf{Occ}(h^{k})=\mathsf{Occ}(\tilde{h}^{k})
, we find
\mathsf{Inf}(h^{0}h^{1}\dots)=\mathsf{Inf}(\tilde{h}^{0}\tilde{h}^{1}\dots)
and therefore, if the play
\chi
is winning for Prover, so is the play
\chi^{\mathsf{a}}
.
The strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}^{\mathsf{a}}
is a reduced winning strategy.

Therefore, to conclude, if Prover has a winning strategy in the abstract negotiation game, she has a reduced one.
∎

5.2  Checking that a reduced strategy is winning

We have established that Prover is winning the abstract negotiation game if and only if she has a reduced winning strategy.
Such a strategy has polynomial size and can thus be guessed in nondeterministic polynomial time.
We must now show that we can verify in deterministic polynomial time that a guessed strategy is winning.

Lemma

8
.

Given a parity game
\mathcal{G}
, a requirement
\lambda
, a player
i
, a vertex
v_{0}\in V_{i}
, and a reduced strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
in the abstract negotiation game
\mathsf{abs}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, deciding whether
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
is a winning strategy can be done in polynomial time.

Proof.

Let us consider the induced game
\mathsf{abs}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}[\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}]
, as defined in
section

2.4
.
In that game, there remain only:

–

Prover’s vertices, i.e., the set
V\cup\{\top,\bot\}
;

–

Challenger’s vertices of the form
[\tilde{\pi}]
, where there exists
v\in V
from which the strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
proposes the reduced play
\tilde{\pi}
;

–

Challenger’s vertices of the form
[hv]
accessible from one vertex
\tilde{\pi}
as defined in the previous item, that have minimal length among occurrence-equivalent prefixes of
\tilde{\pi}
;

and the edges binding those vertices.
It therefore contains at most
n+n+n(n^{3}+2n^{2})
vertices (at most
n
of the form
v
, at most
n
of the form
[\tilde{\pi}]
, and at most
n(n^{3}+2n^{2})
of the form
[hv]
: assuming each play
\tilde{\pi}
has the form
hc^{\omega}
with
|hc|\leq n^{3}+2n^{2}
, a minimal prefix is necessarily a prefix of
hc
).
Deciding whether Challenger can win against the strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
amounts, then, to looking for a play in that game that is winning for him, i.e. either (assuming
\lambda(v_{0})=0
—otherwise, Challenger cannot win except if Prover gives up, which is excluded by the definition of a reduced strategy):

–

that reaches a vertex
[\tilde{\pi}]
where
\tilde{\pi}
is won by player
i
and then goes to the vertex
\top
;

–

that has the form
[v_{0}][\tilde{\pi}^{0}][h^{0}v_{1}][v_{1}][\tilde{\pi}^{1}]\dots
where the play
h^{0}h^{1}\dots
is won by player
i
.

In the first case, the existence of such a play can clearly be decided in polynomial time.
In the second case, looking for such a play amounts to looking for a play satisfying the parity condition defined by
\kappa([v])=m
for each vertex
v\in V
, by
\kappa([\tilde{\pi}])=m
for each play
\tilde{\pi}
, and by
\kappa([hv])=\minop_{k}\kappa_{i}(h_{k})
, where
m
is the maximal color in the game
\mathcal{G}
.
That can also be done in polynomial time.
∎

We have then all the necessary ingredients for an algorithm solving the SPE constrained existence problem.
Indeed, given a game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, two thresholds
\bar{x},\bar{y}\in(\mathbb{Q}\cup\{\pm\infty\})^{\Pi}
and a requirement
\lambda
, if there exists a
\lambda
-consistent play that generates a payoff vector between
\bar{x}
and
\bar{y}
, then it is the case of every occurrence-equivalent play, and therefore, there exists a reduced one that satisfies those conditions.
Therefore, an algorithm solving the SPE constrained existence problem consists in:

•

guessing a requirement
\lambda
on
\mathcal{G}
with values
0
or
1
;

•

guessing a reduced strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}^{u}
in each abstract negotiation game
\mathsf{abs}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright u}
with
i\in\Pi
and
u\in V_{i}
;

•

guessing a reduced play
\tilde{\pi}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
;

•

checking that
\tilde{\pi}
is
\lambda
-consistent and that
\bar{x}\leq\mu(\tilde{\pi})\leq\bar{y}
;

•

checking that each
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}^{v}
is winning (and thus that
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)=\lambda
).

By Theorems
8
and Lemmas
7
and
8
, that algorithm is correct and runs in non-deterministic polynomial time.
Finally, it is already known, since the work of Erich Grädel and Michael Ummels
[
GU08
]
, that the constrained existence problem of SPEs in parity (and co-Büchi) games is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard, even with only one effective lower threshold.
Once again, a quick modification of their proof, by adding one player, entails hardness with only one effective upper threshold.
Hence the following.

Theorem

11
.

In parity games, the constrained existence problem of SPEs is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete.
Hardness still holds in co-Büchi games, and when there is no effective lower threshold, and only one effective upper threshold.

While the NE constrained existence problem in Büchi games has been proved to be decidable in polynomial time, the problem is, to the best of our knowledge, still open for the SPE constrained existence problem.

Open Problem

1
.

Is the SPE constrained existence problem in Büchi games decidable in polynomial time?

5.3  A deterministic upper bound

We end this chapter by mentioning an additional complexity result on the constrained existence problem of SPEs in parity games: it is fixed-parameter tractable.

Theorem

12
.

The SPE constrained existence problem on parity games is fixed-parameter tractable when the number of players and the number of colors are fixed.
More precisely, there exists a deterministic algorithm that solves that problem in time
O(2^{2^{pm}}n^{12})
, where
n
is the number of vertices, where
p
is the number of players and where
m
is the number of colors.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a parity game, let
i\in\Pi
, and let
u\in V_{i}
.
Let us assume, without loss of generality, that
m
is even, and that the vertices of
\mathcal{G}
or labeled by the colors
0,\dots,m-1
.
Let
\lambda
be a requirement.
The value of
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(u)
can be computed as the value of the corresponding concrete negotiation game.
Let us recall that in that game, Challenger wins a play
\chi
if and only if either:

–

player
i
wins the projection
\dot{\chi}
;

–

or Challenger stops deviating, and the play proposed by Prover after the last deviation is not
\lambda
-consistent.

Moreover, since parity games are Boolean, a vertex
v
that is visited after the last deviation induces either no constraint (if
\lambda(v)\leq 0
), or the constraint that the player controlling
v
must win (if
\lambda(v)>0
).
Thus, the game need only memorize the set of players
i
such that a vertex
v\in V_{i}
with
\lambda(v)>0
has been visited.
We therefore slightly modify the definition of the concrete game, to use a version in which the vertices are of the form
(v,M)
or
(uv,M)
with
M\subseteq\Pi
, instead of
M\subseteq V
, which gives it size at most
(n^{2}+n)2^{p}
instead of
(n^{2}+n)2^{n}
.

Thus, that game can be seen as a
multi-parity game
, where a color function is defined on edges (and not on vertices as it usually is) for each
dimension

d\in\Pi\cup\{\star\}
:

–

if
d=\star
, then for each edge
(u,M)(uv,M)
or
(uv,M)(w,M^{\prime})
, we define:

\hat{\kappa}_{\star}((u,M)(uv,M))=\hat{\kappa}_{\star}((uv,M)(w,M^{\prime}))=\kappa_{i}(u);

–

if
d=j\in\Pi
, then for each edge of the form
(u,M)(uv,M)
we define:

\hat{\kappa}_{j}((u,M)(uv,M))=m-1,

and for each edge of the form
(uv,M)(w,M^{\prime})
we define
\hat{\kappa}_{j}((uv,M)(w,M^{\prime}))=1
if
w\neq v
(i.e., if the edge is a deviation) and
\hat{\kappa}_{j}((uv,M)(w,M^{\prime}))=\kappa_{j}(uv)+1
if
w=v
(i.e., if it is not a deviation);

and where Challenger’s goal consists in satisfying
at least one
of the corresponding parity conditions.

It is a multi-parity game with colors on edges, but one can easily transform it into a game with colors on vertices, up to adding one vertex in the middle of each edge, i.e. decomposing each edge
u^{\mathsf{c}}v^{\mathsf{c}}
into two edges
u^{\mathsf{c}}\delta_{u^{\mathsf{c}}v^{\mathsf{c}}}
and
\delta_{u^{\mathsf{c}}v^{\mathsf{c}}}v^{\mathsf{c}}
.

Then, that game can also be interpreted as a Boolean Büchi game in the sense of
[
DBLP:conf/concur/BruyereHR18
]
, i.e. a two-player zero-sum game in which the objective of the first player (Prover) is to validate a Boolean formula whose atoms are Büchi conditions.
Indeed, Prover’s objective can be written:

\bigwedge_{d\in\Pi\cup\{\star\}}\bigvee_{k=0}^{\frac{m}{2}}\left(\mathsf{B}\{\delta_{u^{\mathsf{c}}v^{\mathsf{c}}}~|~\kappa_{d}(u^{\mathsf{c}}v^{\mathsf{c}})=2k\}\wedge\neg\mathsf{B}\{\delta_{u^{\mathsf{c}}v^{\mathsf{c}}}~|~\kappa_{d}(u^{\mathsf{c}}v^{\mathsf{c}})<2k\}\right),

where
\mathsf{B}(W)
is the Büchi objective associated to the set
W
, i.e. the objective of visiting infinitely often at least one vertex of
W
.
That formula has at most
dm
atoms, and has size
dm
, in a game of size
O(n^{2}2^{p})
, hence by Proposition 5 from
[
DBLP:conf/concur/BruyereHR18
]
, there exists a deterministic algorithm that decides which player has a winning strategy in time:

O\left(2^{2^{(p+1)m}}(p+1)m+\left(2^{(p+1)m2^{(p+1)m}}O(n^{2}2^{p})\right)^{5}\right)=2^{2^{O(pm)}}n^{10}.

Therefore, given a requirement
\lambda
, by constructing the concrete negotiation game and applying that algorithm on each vertex, it is possible to compute the requirement
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)
in time
2^{2^{O(pm)}}n^{11}
.
Thus, it is possible to compute the iterations of the negotiation function from the vacuous requirement
\lambda_{0}
: since the function
\mathsf{nego}
is non-decreasing, its least fixed point
\lambda^{*}
(whose existence is guaranteed by Tarski’s fixed point theorem, since the set of requirements is a complete lattice) will be reached in at most
n
steps, and will therefore be found in time
2^{2^{O(pm)}}n^{12}
.

Once
\lambda^{*}
has been computed, given two thresholds
\bar{x}
and
\bar{y}
, the SPE constrained existence problem can be solved by searching, for each tuple
\bar{z}\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}^{\Pi}
where
z_{i}
is even whenever
x_{i}=1
and odd whenever
y_{i}=0
, a play
\pi
in
\mathcal{G}
that avoids the set:

W_{\bar{z}}=\{v\in V_{i}~|~z_{i}\in 2\mathbb{Z}\mathrm{~and~}\lambda^{*}(v)=1\},

and such that for each
i
, we have
\minop\kappa_{i}(\mathsf{Inf}(\pi))=z_{i}
.
For a given tuple
\bar{z}
, the existence of a play can be decided by removing all the vertices of
W_{\bar{z}}
and the vertices
v
such that
\kappa_{i}(v)<z_{i}
for some
i
, then looking for a strongly connected component that contains at least one vertex
v
with
\kappa_{i}(v)=z_{i}
for each
i
, and finally check whether the vertices of that strongly connected component are accessible from the initial vertex in
\mathcal{G}
without visiting the vertices of
W_{\bar{z}}
.
All those computations can be done in time
O(n)
.
Thus, once
\lambda^{*}
has be computed, the SPE constrained existence problem can be solved in time
O(m^{p}n)
.

Given a parity game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
and two thresholds
\bar{x}
and
\bar{y}
, solving the SPE constrained existence problem can be done in time
2^{2^{O(pm)}}n^{12}
, and is therefore fixed-parameter tractable with parameters
m
and
p
.
∎

Chapter 6:  Mean-payoff games

Let us now move to the study of mean-payoff games.
Here, a similar algorithm will be used but with additional steps, due to the fact that optimal strategies in the abstract negotiation game may not be stationary.
Moreover, we will not only consider the constrained existence of SPEs, but also of
\varepsilon
-SPEs, where
\varepsilon\geq 0
is given with the instance.
We will see that this relaxation does not require specific additional tools, nor entail additionnal complexity.

6.1  Hardness

We first show a lower bound for our problem.

Lemma

9
.

The constrained existence problem of
\varepsilon
-SPE in mean-payoff games is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard, even when
\varepsilon
is fixed equal to
0
, and when there is no effective lower threshold and only one effective upper threshold.

Proof.

The structure of this proof is strongly inspired from several proofs from Michael Ummels, in articles that have already been cited above.
We proceed by reduction from the
\mathsf{NP}
-complete problem
Sat
.

Let
\varphi=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\bigvee_{j=1}^{m}L_{ij}
be a formula from propositional logic, written in conjunctive normal form, over the finite variable set
X
.
We construct a mean-payoff game
\mathcal{G}^{\varphi}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
that admits an SPE where the player
{\mathfrak{W}}
gets the payoff
1
, if and only if
\varphi
is satisfiable.

\blacktriangleright
Construction of the game
\mathcal{G}^{\varphi}

First, we define the set of players
\Pi=\{\mathfrak{S}\}\cup X
: every variable of
\varphi
is a player and there are two additional players
\mathfrak{S}
and
{\mathfrak{W}}
, called
Solver
and
Witness
, respectively.

Then, let us define the vertex space: for each clause
C_{i}
, with
i\in\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
, of
\varphi
, we define a vertex
C_{i}
that is controlled by Solver, and for each literal
L_{ij}
of
C_{i}
we define a vertex
(C_{i},L_{ij})
, that is controlled by the player
x
such that
L_{ij}=x
or
\neg x
.
Witness controls no vertex.
We add an edge from
C_{i}
to
(C_{i},L_{ij})
, and another one from
(C_{i},L_{ij})
to
C_{i+1}
.
Moreover, we add a sink vertex
\bot
, with an edge from it to itself, and edges from all the vertices of the form
(C,\neg x)
to
\bot
.

We define the reward function
r
on this game as follows:

–

r_{\mathfrak{S}}(\bot\bot)=0
, and
r_{\mathfrak{S}}(uv)=1
for any other edge
vw
;

–

r_{\mathfrak{W}}(\bot\bot)=1
, and
r_{\mathfrak{W}}(uv)=0
for any other edge
vw
;

–

for each player
x
, we have
r_{x}(uv)=0
for every edge leading to a vertex of the form
v=(C,x)
, and
r_{x}(uv)=1
for any other edge.

Note that Solver and Witness can only get the payoffs
0
or
1
, and that in every play, exactly one of them gets the payoff
1
.
Another player
x
gets the payoff
1
in a play that never visits (or finitely often, or infinitely often but with negligible frequence) a vertex of the form
(C,x)
.
Otherwise, he may get any payoff between
0.5
and
1
, depending on the frequence with which such a vertex is visited.
Finally, we initialize that game in
v_{0}=C_{1}
.

Example

10
.

The game
\mathcal{G}^{\varphi}
, when
\varphi
is the tautology
(x_{1}\vee\neg x_{1})\wedge\dots\wedge(x_{6}\vee\neg x_{6})
, is represented by Figure
18
.
The rewards that are not written are equal to
1
, or to
0
in the case of Witness.

C_{1}
x_{1}
\neg x_{1}
C_{2}
x_{2}
\neg x_{2}
C_{3}
x_{3}
\neg x_{3}
C_{4}
x_{4}
\neg x_{4}
C_{5}
x_{5}
\neg x_{5}
C_{6}
x_{6}
\neg x_{6}
\bot
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x_{1}}}{{0}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x_{2}}}{{0}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x_{3}}}{{0}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x_{4}}}{{0}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x_{5}}}{{0}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x_{6}}}{{0}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathfrak{S}}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{W}}}}{{1}}

Figure 18
:
The game
\mathcal{G}^{\varphi}

Now, let us prove that there is an SPE in
\mathcal{G}^{\varphi}_{\upharpoonright C_{1}}
in which Solver gets the payoff
1
, if and only if the formula
\varphi
is satisfiable, that is, if there exists a valuation
\nu:X\rightarrow\{0,1\}
that satisfies it.

\blacktriangleright
If there is an SPE in
\mathcal{G}^{\varphi}_{\upharpoonright C_{1}}
in which Witness gets the payoff
0
, then
\varphi
is satisfiable.

Let us write
{\bar{\sigma}}
for such an SPE, and let
\pi=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
.
Since
\mu_{\mathfrak{W}}(\pi)=0
, the sink vertex
\bot
is never visited.
Let us define a valuation
\nu
on
X
as follows: for each variable
x
, we have
\nu(x)=1
if and only if
\mu_{x}(\pi)<1
.

Now, let
C
be a clause of
\varphi
: since
C
, as a vertex, is necessarily visited infinitely often and with a fixed frequence in the play
\pi
(because no player ever go to the sink vertex
\bot
), one of its successors, say
(C,L)
, is visited with a non-negligible frequence (more formally, the time between two occurrences of
(C,L)
is bounded).
If
L
is a positive literal, say
x
, then by definition of
\nu
, we have
\nu(x)=1
and the clause
C
is satisfied.

If
L
has the form
\neg x
, then each time the vertex
(C,\neg x)
is traversed, player
x
has the possibility to deviate and to go to the sink vertex
\bot
, where he is sure to get the payoff
1
.
Since
{\bar{\sigma}}
is an SPE, it means that he already gets the payoff
1
in the play
\pi
.
By definition of
\nu
, we then have
\nu(x)=0
, hence the literal
\neg x
is satisfied, hence so is the clause
C
.

The valuation
\nu
satisfies all the clauses of
\varphi
, and therefore satisfies the formula
\varphi
itself.

\blacktriangleright
If
\varphi
is satisfiable, then there is an SPE in which Witness gets payoff
0
.

Let
\nu
be a valuation satisfying
\varphi
, and let us define a strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
by:

–

\sigma_{\mathfrak{S}}(hC)=(C,L)
for each history
hC
where
C
is a clause of
\varphi
, where
L
is a literal of
C
that is satisfied in the valuation
\nu
;

–

and
\sigma_{x}(h(C,\neg x))=\bot
if and only if
\nu(x)=1
for each history
h(C,\neg x)
where
C
is a clause of
\varphi
and
x
is a variable.

Any other vertex has only one successor, hence we now have completely defined a strategy profile.
Now, let us prove it is an SPE, in which Witness gets the payoff
0
.

Let
hC
be a history, where
C
is a clause of
\varphi
.
We want to prove that
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hC}
is a Nash equilibrium, in which Witness gets the payoff
0
.
Let
\pi=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hC}\rangle
.
If
\mu_{\mathfrak{W}}(\pi)=1
, i.e. if
\pi
is of the form
hD(D,\neg x)\bot^{\omega}
, then by definition of
{\bar{\sigma}}
we have
\nu(x)=0
.
But then, we cannot have
\sigma_{\mathfrak{S}}(D)=(D,\neg x)
: contradiction.
The play
\pi
never reaches the vertex
\bot
, and Witness gets the payoff
0
.
Since Witness controls no vertex, he has no profitable deviation.
On her side, Solver gets the payoff
1
, and as a consequence she does not have any profitable deviation.

Now, if another player
x
has a profitable deviation, it means that he does not get the payoff
1
in
\pi
, and therefore that some vertex of the form
(D,x)
is visited infinitely often.
But then, if Solver chooses to go to the vertex
(D,x)
, it means that the literal
x
is satisfied in
\nu
, i.e. that
\nu(x)=1
.
In that case, if some clause
D^{\prime}
contains the literal
\neg x
, it is not a literal satisfied by
\nu
, and therefore the strategy
\sigma_{\mathfrak{S}}
, as we defined it, never chooses the edge to the vertex
(D^{\prime},\neg x)
, where player
x
could have the possibility to deviate from his strategy.
Contradiction.

Finally, after a history of the form
h(C,L)
, either:

–

we have
L=\neg x
with
\nu(x)=1
, and in that case, we have
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h(C,L)}\rangle=(C,L)\bot^{\omega}
, player
x
gets the payoff
1
, and no player has a profitable deviation;

–

or
L
is a positive literal, and then there exists only one edge from the vertex
(C,L)
to another clause
D
, and we go back to the previous case;

–

or we have
L=\neg x
with
\nu(x)=0
, and in that case, we have
\sigma_{x}(C,L)=D
where
D
is the following clause, and by the first case the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h(C,\neg x)D}
is a Nash equilibrium.
Moreover, since the literal
\neg x
is not satisfied in
\nu
, the play
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h(C,\neg x)D}\rangle
does never traverse again any vertex of the form
(D^{\prime},\neg x)
, hence player
x
wins, and therefore has no profitable deviation: the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h(C,\neg x)}
is a Nash equilibrium.

The constrained existence problem of SPEs, and therefore of
\varepsilon
-SPEs, is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard in mean-payoff games.
∎

The rest of this chapter is dedicated to prove that the same problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-easy, and therefore
\mathsf{NP}
-complete.

6.2  Manipulating sets of payoff vectors

This section provides us with a toolbox to compute and manipulate the sets of payoff vectors that can be achieved in a mean-payoff game.

6.2.1 Achievable payoff vectors in a mean-payoff game

A first important result that we need is the characterization of the set of possible payoff vectors in a mean-payoff game, which has been introduced in
[
DBLP:conf/concur/ChatterjeeDEHR10
]
.
Let us recall that given a graph
(V,E)
, we denote by
\mathsf{SCyc}(V,E)
the set of simple cycles it contains.
Given a finite set
D
of dimensions and a set
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{D}
, we write
\mathsf{Conv}X
for the convex hull of
X
, i.e., the set of vectors
\bar{y}
such that there exists a family of positive real numbers
(\alpha_{\bar{x}})_{\bar{x}\in X}
that satisfies the equalities
\sum_{\bar{x}\in X}\alpha_{\bar{x}}=1
and
\sum_{\bar{x}\in X}\alpha_{\bar{x}}\bar{x}=\bar{y}
.
We will often use the subscript notation
\mathsf{Conv}_{x\in X}f(x)
for the set
\mathsf{Conv}f(X)
.

Definition

30

(Downward sealing)
.

Given a set
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{D}
, the
downward sealing
of
Y
is the set:

\!\,{}^{\llcorner}Y=\left\{\left.\left(\minop_{\bar{z}\in Z}z_{d}\right)_{d\in D}~\right|~Z\mathrm{~is~a~finite~subset~of~}Y\right\}.

Lemma

10

(
[
DBLP:conf/concur/ChatterjeeDEHR10
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a mean-payoff game, whose underlying graph is strongly connected.
Then, we have the equality:

\mu\left(\mathsf{Plays}\mathcal{G}\right)=\!\,^{\llcorner}\left(\underset{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(V,E)}{\mathsf{Conv}}\mathsf{mp}(c)\right).

Example

11
.

If the set
Y
is depicted by the blue area in Figure
6.19(b)
, then the set
\!\,{}^{\llcorner}Y
is obtained by adding the gray area.
As a consequence, if
\mathcal{G}
is the game depicted by Figure
6.19(a)
, then the blue and the gray area in Figure
6.19(b)
form the set of achievable payoffs in
\mathcal{G}
.

Indeed, following exclusively one of the three simple cycles
a
,
ab
and
b
of the game graph during a play yields the payoffs
01,10
and
22
, respectively.
By combining those cycles with well chosen frequencies, one can obtain any payoff in the convex hull of those three points.
It is also possible to obtain the point
00
by using the properties of the limit inferior: it is for instance the payoff vector of the play
a^{2}b^{4}a^{16}b^{256}\dots a^{2^{2^{n}}}b^{2^{2^{n+1}}}\dots
.
Then, by combining that play with the simple cycles, one can construct a play that yields any payoff in the convex hull of the four points
(0,0),(0,1),(1,0)
, and
(2,2)
.

a
b
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{2}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{2}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{0}}

(a)

The game
\mathcal{G}

{\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}}
{\Box}

2

(b)

The payoffs of plays and SPE outcomes in
\mathcal{G}

Figure 19
:
An example of mean-payoff game

6.2.2 Equations and inequations

As the experienced reader might have guessed after reading the previous subsection, our algorithms for mean-payoff games will require cautious manipulation of sets of real numbers.
The proof of Theorem
17
below, in particular, requires manipulation of
polytopes
, e.g. downward sealings of convex hulls (from Lemma
10
), expressed as solution sets of
systems of linear inequations
.

Definition

31

(Linear equations, inequations, systems)
.

Let
D
be a finite set.
A
linear equation
in
\mathbb{R}^{D}
is a pair
(\bar{a},b)\in\left(\mathbb{R}^{D}\setminus\left\{\bar{0}\right\}\right)\times\mathbb{R}
.
The
solution set
of the equation
(\bar{a},b)
is the set
\mathsf{Sol}_{=}(\bar{a},b)=\{\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}~|~\bar{a}\cdot\bar{x}=b\}
, where
\cdot
denotes the canonical scalar product on the euclidian space
\mathbb{R}^{D}
.
A set
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{D}
is a
hyperplane
of
\mathbb{R}^{D}
if it is the solution set of some linear equation.
A
system of linear equations
is a finite set
\Sigma
of linear equations.
The
solution set
of the system
\Sigma
is the set
\mathsf{Sol}_{=}\Sigma=\bigcap_{(\bar{a},b)\in\Sigma}\mathsf{Sol}_{=}(\bar{a},b)
.
A set
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{D}
is a
linear subspace
of
\mathbb{R}^{D}
if it is the solution set of some system of linear equations.

A
linear inequation
in
\mathbb{R}^{D}
is also a pair
(\bar{a},b)\in\left(\mathbb{R}^{D}\setminus\left\{\bar{0}\right\}\right)\times\mathbb{R}
.
The
solution set
of the inequation
(\bar{a},b)
is the set
\mathsf{Sol}_{\geq}(\bar{a},b)=\{\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}~|~\bar{a}\cdot\bar{x}\geq b\}
.
A set
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{D}
is a
half-space
of
\mathbb{R}^{D}
if it is the solution set of some linear inequation.
A
system of linear inequations
is a finite set
\Sigma
of linear inequations.
The
solution set
of the system
\Sigma
is the set
\mathsf{Sol}_{\geq}\Sigma=\bigcap_{(\bar{a},b)\in\Sigma}\mathsf{Sol}_{\geq}(\bar{a},b)
.
A set
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{D}
is a
polyhedron
of
\mathbb{R}^{D}
if it is the solution set of some system of linear inequations
\Sigma
.
A
vertex
of
X
is a point
\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
such that
\{\bar{x}\}=\mathsf{Sol}_{=}(\Sigma^{\prime})
for some subset
\Sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma
.
A
polytope
is a bounded polyhedron.

Remark

8
.

•

Polyhedra are closed sets.

•

The polytopes of
\mathbb{R}^{D}
are exactly the sets of the form
\mathsf{Conv}(S)
, where
S
is a finite subset of
\mathbb{R}^{D}
.

•

A same pair
(\bar{a},b)
may alternatively be seen as a linear equation or a linear inequation.

6.2.3 Lemmas

A first important result is the following: when the set
X
is a polyhedron, so is
\!\,{}^{\llcorner}X
.
Given a system of inequations defining the polyhedron
X
, it is therefore possible to find a system of inequations defining
\!\,{}^{\llcorner}X
.
An exponential blowup might appear in the cardinality of that system, but not in the size of individual inequations.
Let us recall that what we mean with the
size
of a number, or of a set, has been defined precisely in
section

2.1
.

Lemma

11

(
[
DBLP:conf/concur/ChatterjeeDEHR10
]
)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a system of inequations, and let
X=\mathsf{Sol}_{\geq}(\Sigma)
.
The set
\!\,{}^{\llcorner}X
is itself a polyhedron, and there exists a system of inequations
\Sigma^{\prime}
such that
\!\,{}^{\llcorner}X=\mathsf{Sol}_{\geq}(\Sigma^{\prime})
and that for every
(\bar{a}^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\Sigma^{\prime}
, there exists
(\bar{a},b)\in\Sigma
with
\lVert(\bar{a}^{\prime},b^{\prime})\rVert\leq\lVert(\bar{a},b)\rVert
.

The previous lemma will be completed by the following, which bounds polynomially the size of the vertices of a polyhedron given the system of inequations that defines it.

Lemma

12

(
[
DBLP:conf/cav/BrenguierR15
]
, Theorem 1)
.

There exists a polynomial
P_{1}
such that, for every system of equations
\Sigma
, there exists a point
\bar{x}\in\mathsf{Sol}_{=}\Sigma
, such that
\lVert\bar{x}\rVert\leq P_{1}\left(\maxop_{(\bar{a},b)\in\Sigma}\lVert(\bar{a},b)\rVert\right)
.

Corollary

1
.

For every system of inequations
\Sigma
, each vertex
\bar{x}
of the polyhedron
\mathsf{Sol}_{\geq}(\Sigma)
has size:

\lVert x\rVert\leq P_{1}\left(\maxop_{(\bar{a},b)\in\Sigma}\lVert(\bar{a},b)\rVert\right).

Note that in Lemma
11
, in Lemma
12
and in Corollary
1
, the number of equations or inequations has no influence.
In the reverse direction, the size of the inequations defining a polyhedron can be polynomially bounded given the size of its vertices.

Lemma

13
.

There exists a polynomial
P_{2}
such that, for each finite set
D
and every finite subset
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{D}
, there exists a system of linear inequations
\Sigma
, such that
\mathsf{Sol}_{\geq}(\Sigma)=\mathsf{Conv}(X)
and
\lVert(\bar{a},b)\rVert\leq P_{2}(\lVert X\rVert)
for every
(\bar{a},b)\in\Sigma
.

Proof.

First, let us recall the notion of
facet
: a facet of a polytope
P
is a subset of
P
of dimension
\dim P-1
and of the form
P\cap H
, where
H
is a hyperplane defined by an equation
(\bar{a},b)
, such that
P\subseteq\mathsf{Sol}_{\geq}(\bar{a},b)
.

Each facet of the polytope
\mathsf{Conv}(X)
is of the form
\mathsf{Conv}\{\bar{x}_{1},\dots,\bar{x}_{n}\}
, where
\bar{x}_{1},\dots,\bar{x}_{n}
are vertices of
X
and
n\geq d=\mathsf{card}D
.
Let
\Phi
be the set of those facets.
We can then write (see for example
[
brondsted1983convex
]
for a proof):

\mathsf{Conv}(X)=\mathsf{Sol}_{\geq}\left\{(\bar{a}_{F},b_{F})~|~F\in\Phi\right\},

where for each
F
, the equation
(\bar{a}_{F},b_{F})
defines the hyperplane to which the facet
F
belongs.
Let us now study the complexity of each of those equations (or inequations).

For a given facet
F=\mathsf{Conv}\{\bar{x}_{1},\dots,\bar{x}_{n}\}
, let us choose
d
points
\bar{y}_{1},\dots,\bar{y}_{d}\in\{\bar{x}_{1},\dots,\bar{x}_{n}\}
that are linearly independent.
The equation
(\bar{a}_{F},b_{F})
has the points
\bar{y}_{1},\dots,\bar{y}_{d}
among its solutions, i.e. it satisfies:

\forall i\in\{1,\dots,d\},\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{Fj}y_{ij}-b_{F}=0.

Those
d
equalities can themselve be understood as equations that are satisfied by the pair
(\bar{a}_{F},b_{F})
.
Let us add a
(d+1)
-th equation: for some dimension
i_{0}
, we have
a_{Fi_{0}}\neq 0
, and by multiplying if needed by a nonzero factor, we can assume
a_{Fi_{0}}=1
.
By Lemma
12
, there exists a pair
(\bar{a},b)
satisfying that system of
d+1
equations and the inequality
\lVert(\bar{a},b)\rVert\leq P_{1}\left(2d+5+\maxop_{i}\left(\sum_{j}\lVert x_{ij}\rVert\right)\right)\leq P_{1}(\lVert X\rVert)
.
That pair is an equation of the hyperplane containing
F
.

As a consequence, the polynomial
P_{2}=P_{1}
satisfies the desired inequalities.
∎

6.3  About negotiation in mean-payoff games

We now give some observations about the behavior of the negotiation function in mean-payoff games.
We first show a useful result: in the concrete negotiation game, stationary strategies are sufficient for Challenger (
14
).
We infer from that result that mean-payoff games are games with steady negotiation (
15
), a result that is not as trivial as it was for parity games, and that is still necessary to apply
8
, and use the negotiation function as a tool to characterize SPEs and
\varepsilon
-SPEs.
We end the section with two important examples, which should help the reader forge intuition on how the negotiation function can be used on mean-payoff games, before moving toward an algorithm.

6.3.1 Stationary optimal strategies for Challenger

We first give a useful result for the sequel: in the concrete negotiation game, Challenger has a stationary optimal strategy.

Lemma

14
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
be a mean-payoff game, let
i
be a player, and let
\lambda
be a requirement.
In the corresponding concrete negotiation game, there exists a stationary strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
that is optimal for Challenger.

Proof.

The structure of that proof is inspired from the proof of Lemma 14 in
[
DBLP:journals/iandc/VelnerC0HRR15
]
.

Let
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}
be the payoff function defined by:

–

\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)=+\infty
if there exists
n
such that
\pi_{\geq 2n}
contains no deviation, and such that the play
\dot{\pi}_{\geq n}
is not
\lambda
-consistent.

–

\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)=\limsupop_{n}\mathsf{mp}_{i}(\dot{\pi}_{\leq n})
otherwise.

The payoff function
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}
is then defined as
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, but with a limit superior instead of inferior.
We will first prove that Challenger has a stationary optimal strategy to maximize
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}
, and then show that such a strategy is also optimal to maximize
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}
.

\blacktriangleright
The payoff function
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}
is concave.

Indeed, let
\pi
and
\chi
be two plays in
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, and let
\xi
be a shuffling of those plays.
Let us check that
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\xi)\leq\maxop\{\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi),\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\chi)\}
.

If either
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)=+\infty
or
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\chi)=+\infty
, it is immediate.
Otherwise, we also have
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\xi)\neq+\infty
: if either
\pi
or
\chi
contains infinitely many deviations, then so does
\xi
.
If both contain finitely many deviations, then so does
\xi
: the vertices of
\xi
have therefore eventually the same memory
M
, which is also the memory of, eventually, the vertices of both
\pi
and
\chi
.
Now, since
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi),\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\chi)\neq+\infty
, we have
\mu_{j}(\dot{\pi}),\mu_{j}(\dot{\chi})\geq\lambda(v)
for each player
j
and every
v\in M\cap V_{j}
.
Since mean-payoff functions are convex, it is also the case for the play
\dot{\xi}
, which is a shuffling of
\dot{\pi}
and
\dot{\chi}
.
Hence
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\xi)\neq+\infty
.

Therefore, we have
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\xi)=\limsupop_{n}\mathsf{mp}_{i}(\dot{\xi}_{\leq n})
, as well as
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)=\limsupop_{n}\mathsf{mp}_{i}(\dot{\pi}_{\leq n})
and
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\dot{\chi})=\limsupop_{n}\mathsf{mp}_{i}(\dot{\chi}_{\leq n})
.
Since, as shown in
[
DBLP:journals/iandc/VelnerC0HRR15
]
, mean-payoff functions defined with a limit superior are concave, it implies
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\xi)\leq\maxop\{\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi),\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\chi)\}
: the payoff function
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}
is concave.

Therefore, by Lemma
2
Challenger has a stationary strategy that is optimal with regards to the payoff function
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}
: let us write it
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
.

\blacktriangleright
The stationary strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
is also optimal with regards to
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}
.

Note that for every play
\pi
, we have
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)\leq\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)
, and therefore
\mathsf{val}_{\mathfrak{C}}\left(\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v^{\mathsf{c}}_{0}}\right)\leq\alpha
, where
\alpha
is the value of the game
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v^{\mathsf{c}}_{0}}
with the payoff function
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}
instead of
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}
.
Therefore, we have proven that
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
is optimal with regards to
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}
if we prove that
\infop_{\tau_{\mathsf{P}}}\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle\geq\alpha
.

Let
\pi
be a play compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
, i.e., a play in the game
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v^{\mathsf{c}}_{0}}[\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}]
.
If
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)=+\infty
, then clearly
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)\geq\alpha
.
Otherwise, we have
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)=\mu_{i}(\dot{\pi})
, and by Lemma
10
, we have:

\mu_{i}(\dot{\pi})\geq\minop\left\{\mathsf{mp}_{i}(\dot{c})\mid c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v^{\mathsf{c}}_{0}}[\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}])\right\}.

Now, for each cycle
c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v^{\mathsf{c}}_{0}}[\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}])
, there exists a history
g
such that the play
gc^{\omega}
is compatible with the strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
, and therefore satisfies
\nu_{\mathfrak{C}}(gc^{\omega})\geq\alpha
, and consequently
\mathsf{mp}_{i}(\dot{c})\geq\alpha
.
Therefore, we have
\mu_{i}(\dot{\pi})\geq\alpha
, and the strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
is optimal with regards to the payoff function
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

6.3.2 Steady negotiation

Using this lemma, computing
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)
for any given
\lambda
amounts to looking for an optimal play for Prover in the game
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}^{\mathsf{c}}}[\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}]
.
We show that there exists an optimal such play; and that this proves that mean-payoff games are games with steady negotiation, which will enable us to apply
8
.

Lemma

15
.

Mean-payoff games are games with steady negotiation.

Proof.

According to Theorem
10
, to prove that mean-payoff games are games with steady negotiation, it suffices to prove that Prover always has an optimal strategy in every concrete negotiation game constructed from a mean-payoff game.
We have already noted that mean-payoff games are Borel, and that when a game is Borel, every concrete negotiation game constructed from it is Borel.
By
1
, it is then equivalent to prove that, in every concrete negotiation game
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}^{\mathsf{c}}}
built from a mean-payoff game
\mathcal{G}
, against every strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
of Challenger, Prover has a strategy to obtain at least the payoff
\mathsf{val}_{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}^{\mathsf{c}}})
, i.e. a strategy so that Challenger obtains at most the payoff
\mathsf{val}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}^{\mathsf{c}}})
.
Finally, by
14
, it is then sufficient to prove that result only for stationary strategies of Challenger.

Let therefore
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
be a stationary strategy of Prover.
By definition of the adversarial value, we have:

\infop\left\{\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)\mid\pi\in\mathsf{Plays}\left(\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}^{\mathsf{c}}}[\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}]\right)\right\}\leq\mathsf{val}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}^{\mathsf{c}}}).

We must now prove that this infimum is a minimum, i.e. that Prover can choose a worst play for Challenger in the game
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}^{\mathsf{c}}}[\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}]
.

For any play
\pi
in that graph, there exists a strongly connected component
K
of
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}^{\mathsf{c}}}[\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}]
such that after a finite number of steps, the play
\pi
remains in
K
.
Since there are finitely many strongly connected components in a graph, there is a
worst
such strongly connected component, i.e. a strongly connected component
K
such that we have:

\infop\left\{\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)\mid\pi\in\mathsf{Plays}\left(\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}^{\mathsf{c}}}[\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}]\right)\right\}=\infop\left\{\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)\mid\pi\in\mathsf{Plays}(K)\right\}.

We must now prove that there is a worst play in
K
.
Let us distinguish two cases.

If there is at least one deviation in
K
.

Then, for every play
\pi
in
K
, it is possible to transform the play
\pi
into a play
\pi^{\prime}
with
\mu(\dot{\pi}^{\prime})=\mu(\dot{\pi})
, which contains infinitely many deviations: it suffices to add round trips to a deviation, endlessly, but less and less often.
Therefore, the outcomes
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)
of plays in
K
are exactly the mean-payoffs
\mu_{i}(\dot{\pi})
of plays in
K
(plus possibly
+\infty
); and in particular, the lowest payoff that can be given to Challenger in
K
is the quantity (using
10
):

\minop_{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(K)}\mathsf{mp}_{i}(\dot{c}),

which is achieved by the play
c^{\omega}
, where
c
is a simple cycle realizing that minimum.

If there is no deviation in
K
.

Then, let us notice that deviations are the only edges that go from a vertex of the form
(\cdot,M)
to a vertex of the form
(\cdot,M^{\prime})
with
M^{\prime}\subset M
.
Therefore, necessarily, there exists a set
M\subseteq V
such that all vertices in
K
have the form
(\cdot,M)
.

By Lemma
10
, the set of possible values of
\mu(\dot{\pi})
for all plays
\pi
in
K
is exactly the set:

X=\!\,^{\llcorner}\left(\underset{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(K)}{\mathsf{Conv}}\mu(\dot{c}^{\omega})\right).

Since all plays in
K
contain finitely many deviations (actually none), for every play
\pi
in
K
, we have
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)=+\infty
if and only if there exists
j\in\Pi
and
u\in V_{j}\cap M
such that
\mu_{j}(\dot{\pi})<\lambda(u)
.
Then, the lowest payoff that can be given to Challenger in
K
is the quantity:

\infop\left\{x_{i}~|~\bar{x}\in X,\forall j\in\Pi,\forall u\in V_{j}\cap M,x_{j}\geq\lambda(u)\right\},

and since that set is closed, this infimum is a minimum, which concludes the proof.
∎

Note that the proof is constructive, suggesting an algorithm to compute the negotiation function.
We will however not use the concrete negotiation game for that purpose, because of its prohibitive size: it will only be used for intermediary results.

6.3.3 First application: an example with no SPE

It is a well-known fact that there are mean-payoff games that do not contain SPEs, while they are guaranteed to exist in Borel Boolean games such as parity or energy games
[
GU08
]
, and while NEs are guaranteed to exist in mean-payoff games
[
DBLP:conf/lfcs/BrihayePS13
]
.
We present here a classical example of such a game, along with a proof of the non-existence of SPEs based on the negotiation function.

Theorem

13

(
[
SOLAN2003911
]
)
.

There exists a mean-payoff game with no SPE.

Proof.

a
c
b
d
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{3}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{3}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{2}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{1}}
(\lambda_{0})
-\infty
-\infty
-\infty
-\infty
(\lambda_{1})
1
2
1
2
(\lambda_{2})
2
2
1
2
(\lambda_{3})
2
3
1
2
(\lambda_{4})
+\infty
+\infty
1
2

Figure 20
:
A game without SPE

Let us consider the game depicted by Figure
20
.
On the two first lines below the vertices, we present the requirements
\lambda_{0}
and
\lambda_{1}=\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{0})
.
The latter is easy to compute since for each
v
, we have the equality
\lambda_{1}(v)=\mathsf{val}(v)
.
Let us now compute the following iterations.

\blacktriangleright
Computation of
\lambda_{2}=\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{1})

From the vertex
c
, there exists exactly one
\lambda_{1}
-rational strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-\circ}=\sigma_{\Box}
, which is the empty strategy since player
\Box
never has to choose anything.
Against that strategy, the best and the only payoff player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
can get is
1
, hence
\lambda_{2}(c)=1
.
For the same reasons, we have
\lambda_{2}(d)=2
.

From the vertex
b
, player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
can force
\Box
to get the payoff
2
or less, with the strategy (profile)
\sigma_{\circ}:h\mapstochar\rightarrow c
. Such a strategy (profile) is
\lambda_{1}
-rational, assuming the strategy
\sigma_{\Box}:h\mapstochar\rightarrow d
.
Therefore, we have
\lambda_{2}(b)=2
.

Finally, from the vertex
a
, player
\Box
can force
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
to get the payoff
2
or less, with the strategy profile
\sigma_{\Box}:h\mapstochar\rightarrow d
.
Such a strategy is
\lambda_{1}
-rational, assuming the strategy
\sigma_{\circ}:h\mapstochar\rightarrow c
.
However, he cannot force her to get less than the payoff
2
, because she can force the access to the vertex
b
, and the only
\lambda_{1}
-consistent plays from
b
are the plays with the form
(ba)^{k}bd^{\omega}
.
Therefore, we have
\lambda_{2}(a)=2
.

\blacktriangleright
Computation of
\lambda_{3}=\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{2})

The computation of
\lambda_{3}(c)=1
and
\lambda_{3}(d)=2
is immediate.

From the vertex
a
, player
