\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
cannot obtain more than the payoff
2
against the strategy
\sigma_{\square}:h\mapstochar\rightarrow d
.
And the strategy profile composed of that strategy is still
\lambda_{2}
-rational, assuming the strategy
\sigma_{\circ}:h\mapstochar\rightarrow c
.
We therefore have
\lambda_{3}(a)=2
.

From the vertex
b
, on the other hand, the only
\lambda_{2}
-rational strategy profile for player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
is composed of the strategy
\sigma_{\circ}:ha\mapstochar\rightarrow b
, which is
\lambda_{2}
-rational assuming the strategy
\sigma_{\Box}:h\mapstochar\rightarrow d
: taking the edge from
a
to
c
is now unacceptable for player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
.
Consequently, using the strategy
\sigma^{\prime}_{\Box}:hb\mapstochar\rightarrow a
, player
\Box
can force the payoff
3
, hence
\lambda_{3}(b)=3
.

\blacktriangleright
Computation of
\lambda_{4}=\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{3})
and conclusion

Finally, let us observe that there is no
\lambda_{3}
-consistent play, and therefore no
\lambda_{3}
-rational strategy profile, from the vertices
a
and
b
, hence
\lambda_{4}(a)=\lambda_{4}(b)=+\infty
.
The requirement
\lambda_{4}
is then a fixed point of the negotiation function.

By a quick induction, we can show that every fixed point of the negotiation function is always greater than or equal to all the iterated
\mathsf{nego}^{k}(\lambda_{0})
, and therefore every SPE outcome from the vertices
a
or
b
is necessarily
\lambda_{4}
-consistent.
Since there is no such play, there is no SPE that starts from one of those vertices.
∎

6.3.4 The impossibility of an algorithm based on iterations

The two previous subsections suggest an algorithm based on the computation of the
negotiation sequence
, i.e. the iterations of the negotiation function on the vacuous requirement
\lambda_{0}
, until a fixed point is reached.
Such an algorithm would be analogous to the one described in the proof of
12
.
However, such an approach cannot be used on mean-payoff games, because there are mean-payoff games in which the negotiation sequence does never reach any fixed point.

Theorem

14
.

There exists a mean-payoff game on which the negotiation sequence is not ultimately constant.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be the game of Figure
21
, and let us compute the negotiation sequence
(\lambda_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Since all player
\Diamond
’s rewards are equal to
0
, for all
n>0
, we have
\lambda_{n}(c)=\lambda_{n}(d)=\lambda_{n}(e)=\lambda_{n}(f)=0
.
Moreover, by symmetry of the game, we always have
\lambda_{n}(a)=\lambda_{n}(b)
. Therefore, to compute the negotiation sequence, it suffices to compute
\lambda_{n+1}(a)
as a function of
\lambda_{n}(b)
, knowing that
\lambda_{1}(a)=\lambda_{1}(b)=1
, and therefore that for all
n>0
, we have
\lambda_{n}(a)=\lambda_{n}(b)\geq 1
.
For convenience, we compute here the negotiation function using the abstract negotiation game.

c
d
a
b
e
f
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Diamond}}{{0}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Diamond}}{{0}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Diamond}}{{0}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Diamond}}{{0}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Diamond}}{{0}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Diamond}}{{0}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Diamond}}{{0}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Diamond}}{{0}}

Figure 21
:
A game where the negotiation sequence is not ultimately constant

From
a
, the worst play that Prover can propose would be a combination of the cycles
cd
and
d
giving her exactly
1
.
But then, Challenger would deviate to go to
b
, from which, if Prover always proposes plays in the strongly connected component containing
c
and
d
, Challenger will always deviate and generate the play
(ab)^{\omega}
, and then get the payoff
2
.

Then, in order to give player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
a payoff lower than
2
, Prover has to go to the vertex
e
.
Since player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
does not control any vertex in that strongly connected component, the play that Prover will propose will be accepted: she will, then, propose the worst possible combination of the cycles
ef
and
f
for player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
, such that player
\Box
gets at least his requirement
\lambda_{n}(b)
.
The payoff
\lambda_{n+1}(a)
is then the minimal solution of the system:

\left\{\begin{matrix}\lambda_{n+1}(a)=x+2(1-x)\\
2(1-x)\geq\lambda_{n}(b)\\
0\leq x\leq 1\end{matrix}\right.

that is to say
\lambda_{n+1}(a)=1+\frac{\lambda_{n}(b)}{2}=1+\frac{\lambda_{n}(a)}{2}
. By induction, we obtain for all
n>0
:

\lambda_{n}(a)=\lambda_{n}(b)=2-\frac{1}{2^{n-1}}

which converges to
2
but never reaches it.
∎

Along with the prohibitive size of the concrete negotiation game, this theorem suggests that, if the constrained existence problem of SPEs in mean-payoff games is fixed-parameter tractable, a proof of that result must use a completely different approach.
We leave that problem open.

Open Problem

2
.

Is the constrained existence problem of (
\varepsilon
-)SPEs in mean-payoff games fixed-parameter tractable, when the number of players is fixed?

6.4  Size of the least
\varepsilon
–fixed point

Now that the necessary conceptual tools are given, we present a technical but important result about mean-payoff games: the size of the least
\varepsilon
-fixed point of the negotiation function in a mean-payoff game
\mathcal{G}
is bounded by a polynomial of the size of
\mathcal{G}
and
\varepsilon
.
The first piece of the witnesses identifying positive instances of the
\varepsilon
-SPE constrained existence problem will then be an
\varepsilon
-fixed point of the negotiation function of polynomial size.

6.4.1 Existence

Before proving anything about its size, we must first prove that the negotiation function always has a least
\varepsilon
-fixed point.

Lemma

16
.

Let
\varepsilon\geq 0
.
On every game, the function
\mathsf{nego}
has a least
\varepsilon
-fixed point.

Proof.

The following proof is a generalization of a classical proof of Tarski’s fixed point theorem.
Let
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
be the set of
\varepsilon
-fixed points of the negotiation function.
The set
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
is not empty, since it contains at least the requirement
v\mapstochar\rightarrow+\infty
.
Let
\lambda_{\varepsilon}
be the requirement defined by:

\lambda_{\varepsilon}:v\mapstochar\rightarrow\infop_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\varepsilon}}\lambda(v).

For every
\varepsilon
-fixed point
\lambda
of the negotiation function, and for each vertex
v
, we have then
\lambda_{\varepsilon}(v)\leq\lambda(v)
, and therefore
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{\varepsilon})(v)\leq\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v)
since
\mathsf{nego}
is monotone; and therefore, we have
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{\varepsilon})(v)\leq\lambda(v)+\varepsilon
.
As a consequence, we have:

\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{\varepsilon})(v)\leq\infop_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\varepsilon}}\lambda(v)+\varepsilon=\lambda_{\varepsilon}(v)+\varepsilon.

The requirement
\lambda_{\varepsilon}
is an
\varepsilon
-fixed point of the negotiation function, and is therefore the least of them.
∎

6.4.2 Size

Lemma

17
.

There exists a polynomial
P_{3}
such that for every mean-payoff game
\mathcal{G}
, the least
\varepsilon
-fixed point
\lambda_{\varepsilon}
of the negotiation function has size
\lVert\lambda_{\varepsilon}\rVert\leq P_{3}(\lVert\mathcal{G}\rVert+\lVert\varepsilon\rVert)
.

Proof.

This proof will use a characterization of
\lambda_{\varepsilon}
as a vertex (or, more exactly, a well-chosen projection of a vertex) in a union of polyhedra, to which we will apply
1
.
Those polyhedra will be defined using the concrete negotiation games, and the fact that, by
14
, stationary strategies are optimal for Challenger in those games.

\blacktriangleright
Reminders about the concrete negotiation game

Without loss of generality, we assume that all the rewards
r_{i}(uv)
for
i\in\Pi
and
uv\in E
are integers—otherwise, we can multiply all the rewards by the least common denominator, compute the least
\varepsilon
-fixed point in the resulting game, and finally divide each of its values by the least common denominator.

Let now
i\in\Pi
and
v\in V_{i}
, let
\lambda
be a requirement, and let us consider the corresponding concrete negotiation game
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright(v,\{v\})}
.
By
14
, we know that Challenger has a stationary optimal strategy in that game.
Let then
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
be a stationary strategy for Challenger in
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright(v,\{v\})}
and let us consider the game
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{(v,\{v\})}[\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}]
.
Facing the strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
, Prover must choose an optimal play in that game, i.e., since the concrete negotiation game is prefix-independent, a strongly connected component
K
of its underlying graph, and a good combination of cycles in
K
.
There are, then, two possibilities: either Challenger does not deviate anywhere in
K
, and then, Prover must find a play that minimizes player
i
’s payoff while remaining
\lambda
-consistent.
Or, there is such a deviation, and it is then possible for Prover to visit that deviation infinitely often (if necessary, with negligible frequency), and thus to ignore the constraints defined by
\lambda
.

\blacktriangleright
Size of mean-payoffs of cycles

Let therefore
K
be a strongly connected component of
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{(v,\{v\})}[\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}]
.
Let
c=c_{1}\dots c_{n}
be a simple cycle of
K
: necessarily, we have
n\leq\mathsf{card}V^{\mathsf{c}}=\mathsf{card}V2^{\mathsf{card}V}
.
Therefore, we have, for each player
j
:

\displaystyle\lVert\mathsf{mp}_{j}(\dot{c})\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}}r_{j}(\dot{c}_{k}\dot{c}_{k+1})\right\rVert

\displaystyle=1+\left\lceil\mathsf{log}_{2}\left(\left|\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}}r_{j}(\dot{c}_{k}\dot{c}_{k+1})\right|+1\right)\right\rceil+\lceil\mathsf{log}_{2}(n)\rceil

\displaystyle\leq 1+\left\lceil\mathsf{log}_{2}\left(\left|2^{\mathsf{card}V}\sum_{e\in E}r_{j}(e)\right|+1\right)\right\rceil+\lceil\mathsf{card}V\mathsf{log}_{2}(\mathsf{card}V)\rceil

\displaystyle\leq 1+\mathsf{card}V+\sum_{e\in E}\left\lceil\mathsf{log}_{2}\left(|r_{j}(e)|+1\right)\right\rceil+(\mathsf{card}V)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(\mathsf{card}E)^{3}+\sum_{e\in E}\left\lceil\mathsf{log}_{2}\left(|r_{j}(e)|+1\right)\right\rceil

\displaystyle\leq\lVert r_{j}\rVert^{3}\leq\lVert\mathcal{G}\rVert^{3}.

\blacktriangleright
Feasible payoff vectors in a strongly connected component

Let us now define the polytope:

F_{K}=\!\,^{\llcorner}\left(\underset{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(K)}{\mathsf{Conv}}\mathsf{mp}(\dot{c})\right).

By Lemma
10
, the polytope
F_{K}
is exactly the set of the payoff vectors of the projections of plays in the strongly connected component
K
.
By Lemma
13
, the polytope:

\underset{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(K)}{\mathsf{Conv}}\mathsf{mp}(\dot{c})

is the solution set of a system of inequations whose sizes are bounded by a polynomial function of
\lVert\{\mathsf{mp}(\dot{c})~|~c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(K)\}\rVert
, i.e., according to the previous point, of
\lVert\mathcal{G}\rVert
.
By Lemma
11
, this is also the case for the polytope
F_{K}
.

\blacktriangleright
Consistent payoff vectors in a strongly connected component

If the strongly connected component
K
contains no deviation, then all the vertices of
K
share the same memory: there is a set
M_{K}\subseteq V
such that all the vertices of
K
have the form
(\cdot,M_{K})
.
If
K
contains a deviation, it might not be the case: then, we define
M_{K}=\emptyset
.
Thus, in both cases, the set
M_{K}
defines the set of constraints a play must satisfy so that Challenger does not get the payoff
+\infty
: the vertices memorized so far if there is no deviation, and nothing if there is a deviation.

Given a requirement
\lambda
, we can now define the polytope of
consistent
payoff vectors:

C_{K\lambda}=\left\{\bar{x}\in F_{K}~|~\forall j,\forall u\in V_{i}\cap M_{K},x_{i}\geq\lambda(u)\right\}.

That polytope is the set of tuples
\bar{x}
such that there exists a play
\pi
in
K
realizing
\mu(\dot{\pi})=\bar{x}
,
and

\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)\neq+\infty
.
We can then write:

\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v)=\supop_{\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}}\infop_{K}\infop_{\bar{x}\in C_{K\lambda}}x_{i}.

The inequations defining
C_{K\lambda}
are the same as those defining
F_{K}
, plus the inequations of the form
x_{i}\geq\lambda(u)
.

\blacktriangleright
Union, intersection and product

Let us now work in the space
\mathbb{R}^{V\times\Pi}
.
Given a requirement
\lambda
, we define the set:

X_{\lambda}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\Pi\\
v\in V_{i}\end{subarray}}\bigcap_{\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}\in\mathsf{Sta}\left(\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright(v,\{v\})}\right)}\bigcup_{K}C_{K\lambda}^{\uparrow},

where
Y\mapstochar\rightarrow Y^{\uparrow}
denotes the upward closure operation, i.e.
Y^{\uparrow}=\{z~|~\exists y\in Y,z\geq y\}
.
Then, for every tuple of tuples
\bar{\bar{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{V\times\Pi}
, we have
\bar{\bar{x}}\in X_{\lambda}
if and only if for each
i
and
v\in V_{i}
, for every stationary strategy of Challenger, there exists a play
\pi
compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
satisfying
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)\neq+\infty
and
\mu(\dot{\pi})\leq\bar{x}_{v}
.
Therefore, for each
i
and
v\in V_{i}
, we have
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v)=\infop\left\{x_{vi}~\left|~\bar{\bar{x}}\in X_{\lambda}\right.\right\}
.

In terms of inequations, the set
X_{\lambda}
is a union of polyhedra which are all defined by inequations that are inequations defining some
C_{K\lambda}
, padded with
0
to fit with the dimension change.

\blacktriangleright

\varepsilon
-fixed points

To each tuple of tuples
\bar{\bar{x}}
, we associate the requirement
\lambda_{\bar{\bar{x}}}
defined, for each
i\in\Pi
and
v\in V_{i}
, by
\lambda_{\bar{\bar{x}}}(v)=x_{vi}-\varepsilon
.
Now, let us consider the diagonal set:

X=\left\{\bar{\bar{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{V\times\Pi}~\left|~\bar{\bar{x}}\in X_{\lambda_{\bar{\bar{x}}}}\right.\right\}.

In terms of inequations, the set
X
is a union of polyhedra defined by the same inequations as
X_{\lambda}
, but where those of the form
x_{ui}\geq\lambda(v)
are replaced by equations of the form
x_{ui}\geq x_{vi}
, each of size
2+4\mathsf{card}V\mathsf{card}\Pi
.

We can now link the set
X
to
\varepsilon
-fixed points of the negotiation function.

Sublemma

2
.

Let
\lambda
be a requirement.
Then, it is an
\varepsilon
-fixed point of the negotiation function if and only if we have
\lambda=\lambda_{\bar{\bar{x}}}
for some
\bar{\bar{x}}\in X
.

Proof.

*

If there exists
\bar{\bar{x}}\in X
such that
\lambda=\lambda_{\bar{\bar{x}}}
, then we have
\bar{\bar{x}}\in X_{\lambda_{\bar{\bar{x}}}}
, and by the previous point, for each
i
and
v\in V_{i}
, we have
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{\bar{\bar{x}}})(v)\leq x_{vi}=\lambda_{\bar{\bar{x}}}(v)+\varepsilon
.
Therefore,
\lambda
is an
\varepsilon
-fixed point of the negotiation function.

*

Conversely, if for each
i
and
v\in V_{i}
, we have
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v)\leq\lambda(v)+\varepsilon
, then according to the previous point and since the set
X_{\lambda}
is closed, there exists a tuple of tuples
\bar{\bar{x}}^{(v)}\in X_{\lambda}
such that
x^{(v)}_{vi}=\lambda(v)+\varepsilon
.
Then, since
X_{\lambda}
is defined as a cartesian product over
v
, the tuple of tuples
\bar{\bar{x}}=\left(\bar{x}^{(v)}_{v}\right)_{v}
does also belong to
X_{\lambda}
, and satisfies
\lambda=\lambda_{\bar{\bar{x}}}
.

∎

Then, in particular, the least
\varepsilon
-fixed point
\lambda_{\varepsilon}
is the (unique) minimal element in the set
\left\{\lambda_{\bar{\bar{x}}}~\left|~\bar{\bar{x}}\in X\right.\right\}
.
The set
X
is itself a union of polyhedra: consequently, the linear mapping
\bar{\bar{x}}\mapstochar\rightarrow\sum_{v}\lambda_{\bar{\bar{x}}}(v)
finds its minimum over
X
on some vertex
\bar{\bar{x}}
of one of those polyhedra, which is therefore such that
\lambda_{\varepsilon}=\lambda_{\bar{\bar{x}}}
.
By Corollary
1
, such a vertex has size bounded by a polynomial function of the maximal size of the inequations defining
X
, and therefore a polynomial function of
\lVert\mathcal{G}\rVert+\lVert\varepsilon\rVert
.
∎

With this proof, we are now done with our utilization of the concrete negotiation game.
Like in the case of parity games, the negotiation function will rather be computed using simple strategies in the abstract negotiation game.

6.5  Constrained existence of a
\lambda
–consistent play

We claim that a non-deterministic algorithm can recognize the positive instances of the
\varepsilon
-SPE constrained existence problem by guessing an
\varepsilon
-fixed point
\lambda
of the negotiation function, as first piece of a three-piece witness.
Once
\lambda
has been guessed, following Theorem
8
, two assertions must be checked: on the one hand, that there exists a
\lambda
-consistent play between the two desired thresholds, and on the other hand, that
\lambda
is actually an
\varepsilon
-fixed point of the negotiation function.
The latter will be handled later through a new notion of reduced strategy.
Now, we tackle the former, and provide the second piece of our notion of witness: to prove the existence of a
\lambda
-consistent play
\pi
with
\bar{x}\leq\mu(\pi)\leq\bar{y}
, we need to guess the sets
W=\mathsf{Inf}(\pi)
and
W^{\prime}=\mathsf{Occ}(\pi)
, and a tuple of tuples
\bar{\bar{\alpha}}\in[0,1]^{\Pi\times\mathsf{SCyc}(W)}
indicating how
\pi
combines the cycles of
W
(we liberally assimilate the set
W
to the induced subgraph
(W,E\cap(W\times W))
to simplify notations), i.e. such that we have:

\mu(\pi)=\left(\minop_{j\in\Pi}\sum_{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(W)}\alpha_{jc}\mathsf{mp}_{i}(c)\right)_{i}.

Lemma

18
.

There exists a polynomial
P_{4}
such that for every mean-payoff game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, for every
\bar{x},\bar{y}\in(\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\})^{\Pi}
, and for every requirement
\lambda
on
\mathcal{G}
, there exists a
\lambda
-consistent play
\pi
in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
satisfying
\bar{x}\leq\mu(\pi)\leq\bar{y}
if and only if there exist two sets
W\subseteq W^{\prime}\subseteq V
and a tuple of tuples
\bar{\bar{\alpha}}\in[0,1]^{\Pi\times\mathsf{SCyc}(W)}
such that:

•

the set
W
is strongly connected in
(V,E)
, and accessible from the vertex
v_{0}
using only vertices of
W^{\prime}
;

•

for each player
i
, we have
\sum_{c}\alpha_{ic}=1
, and:

x_{i}\leq\minop_{j\in\Pi}\sum_{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(W)}\alpha_{jc}\mathsf{mp}_{i}(c)\leq y_{i};

•

for each player
i
and
v\in W\cap V_{i}
, we have:

\minop_{j\in\Pi}\sum_{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(W)}\alpha_{jc}\mathsf{mp}_{i}(c)\geq\lambda(v);

•

and finally, we have
\lVert\bar{\bar{\alpha}}\rVert\leq P_{4}(\lVert\mathcal{G},\bar{x},\bar{y},\lambda\rVert)
.

Proof.

Let us first notice that given a set
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{\Pi}
, the elements of the set
\!\,{}^{\llcorner}(\mathsf{Conv}X)
are exactly the tuples of the form:

\left(\minop_{j\in\Pi}\sum_{x\in X}\alpha_{jx}x_{i}\right)_{i\in\Pi}

for some tuple
\bar{\bar{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Pi\times X}
satisfying
\sum_{x}\alpha_{ix}=1
for each
i
.

Now, let us assume that the sets
W
,
W^{\prime}
and the tuple
\bar{\bar{\alpha}}
exist.

Then, there exists a play
\chi
with
\mathsf{Occ}(\chi)=\mathsf{Inf}(\chi)=W
with payoff vector:

\mu(\chi)=\left(\minop_{j\in\Pi}\sum_{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(W)}\alpha_{jx}\mathsf{mp}_{i}(c)\right)_{i\in\Pi}.

Moreover, since
W
is accessible from
v_{0}
using only vertices of
W^{\prime}
, there exists a history
h\chi_{0}
from
v_{0}
to
\chi_{0}
with
\mathsf{Occ}(h)=W^{\prime}
.
Then, the play
\pi=h\chi
is
\lambda
-consistent and satisfies
\bar{x}\leq\mu(\pi)\leq\bar{y}
.

Conversely, let us assume that the play
\pi
exists.

Let
W=\mathsf{Inf}(\pi)
and
W^{\prime}=\mathsf{Occ}(\pi)
.
The polytope:

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=\left\{\mu(\chi)~\left|~\begin{matrix}\chi\in\lambda\mathsf{Cons}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}},\\
\mathsf{Inf}(\chi)=W,\\
\mathsf{Occ}(\chi)=W^{\prime},\\
\mathrm{and~}\bar{x}\leq\mu(\chi)\leq\bar{y}\end{matrix}\right.\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\bar{z}\in\!\,^{\llcorner}\left(\underset{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(W)}{\mathsf{Conv}}\mathsf{mp}(c)\right)~\left|~\begin{matrix}\bar{x}\leq\bar{z}\leq\bar{y},\mathrm{~and}\\
\forall i,\forall v\in W^{\prime}\cap V_{i},z_{i}\geq\lambda(v)\end{matrix}\right.\right\}

(the equality holds by Lemma
10
) is nonempty (it contains at least the vector
\mu(\pi)
).
By Lemma
13
, the set
\mathsf{Conv}_{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(W)}\mathsf{mp}(c)
is defined by a system of inequations which all have size:

\lVert(\bar{a},b)\rVert\leq P_{2}\left(\maxop_{c}\lVert\mathsf{mp}(c)\rVert\right).

Since by Lemma
11
, the inequations defining the polytope
\!\,{}^{\llcorner}\left(\mathsf{Conv}_{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(W)}\mathsf{mp}(c)\right)
are not larger, there exists a polynomial
P_{6}
, independent of
\mathcal{G},\bar{x},\bar{y}
and
\lambda
, such that the polytope
Z
is defined by a system of inequations
\Sigma
such that for every
(\bar{a},b)\in\Sigma
, we have
\lVert(\bar{a},b)\rVert\leq P_{6}(\lVert(\mathcal{G},\bar{x},\bar{y},\lambda)\rVert)
.
Therefore, by Corollary
1
, the polytope
Z
admits a vertex
\bar{z}
of size
\lVert\bar{z}\rVert\leq P_{1}(P_{6}(\lVert(\mathcal{G},\bar{x},\bar{y},\lambda)\rVert))
.

Then, since we have
\bar{z}\in\!\,^{\llcorner}\left(\underset{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(W)}{\mathsf{Conv}}\mathsf{mp}(c)\right)
, that vertex is, according to Definition
30
, of the form:

\bar{z}=\left(\minop_{j}\sum_{c}\alpha_{jc}\mathsf{mp}_{i}(c)\right)_{i}

for some tuple of tuples
\bar{\bar{\alpha}}\in[0,1]^{\Pi\times\mathsf{SCyc}(W)}
with
\sum_{c}\alpha_{ic}=1
and having, by Corollary
1
again, size:

\lVert\bar{\bar{\alpha}}\rVert\leq P_{1}\left(\maxop_{i\in\Pi}\sum_{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(W)}\lVert\mathsf{mp}_{i}(c)\rVert+\lVert z_{i}\rVert\right),

i.e.
\lVert\bar{\bar{\alpha}}\rVert\leq P_{4}(\lVert(\mathcal{G},\bar{x},\bar{y},\lambda)\rVert)
for some polynomial
P_{4}
independent of
\mathcal{G},\bar{x},\bar{y}
and
\lambda
.
∎

Now, we need the third and last piece of our witness, which will be the evidence of the fact that the requirement
\lambda
is an
\varepsilon
-fixed point of the negotiation function.

6.6  Computing the negotiation function

6.6.1 A disturbing example

Let us study more deeply the game
\mathcal{G}
depicted by Figure
6.19(a)
: consider the requirement
\lambda
, which maps both
a
and
b
to the value
1
.
We would like to know whether
\lambda
is a fixed point of the negotiation function; or in other words, using the symmetry of the game, whether there exists a strategy for Prover, in the abstract negotiation game from vertex
a
, that forces player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
to get at most the payoff
1
.
Prover can propose, for example, the play
(aaabbb)^{\omega}
, that is
\lambda
-consistent, and in which player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
gets exactly the payoff
1
.
But then, Challenger can make player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
deviate and go directly to
b
.
That strategy will give player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
a payoff greater than
1
against every finite-memory strategy of Prover.

Now, consider the following strategy of Prover: propose the play
(aaabbb)^{\omega}
.
Then, whenever Challenger deviates, and reaches prematurely the vertex
b
, propose the play
b^{|h|^{2}}(aaabbb)^{\omega}
, where
h
is the history in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright a}
that has already been drawn by Prover and Challenger’s previous interactions.
Such a play is
\lambda
-consistent, and even if Challenger deviates to go to the vertex
b
whenever he has the opportunity to do so, the play that will be generated is
abab^{9}ab^{169}ab^{33489}\dots
, in which player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
’s payoff is still
1
.
The requirement
\lambda
is therefore a fixed point of the negotiation function (hence the set of SPE payoff vectors is the red-circled area in
fig.

6.19(b)
), but Prover requires infinite memory to maintain it.
The algorithm that we used for parity games in
chapter

5
can therefore not be used as such.

However, we can see in this example that the plays proposed by Prover are still very similar: only the number of repetitions of the loop
b
does increase.
More generally, one observes that Prover can play optimally while always proposing a play of the form
hc^{n}\pi
, where the history
h
, the cycle
c
and the play
\pi
are constant, and only the number
n
increases, quadratically with time—so that Challenger’s payoff is dominated by the mean-payoff
\mathsf{mp}_{i}(c)
if he deviates infinitely often.
This will define a new notion of reduced strategy, inspired with what we did in
chapter

5
but more sophisticated.

6.6.2 Reduced strategies for mean-payoff games

Let us first define the notion of punishment family, the brick from which our reduced strategies will be built.

Definition

32

(Punishment family)
.

A
punishment family
is a set of plays of the form:

\left\{\left.hc^{n}\pi~\right|~n>0,\mu(\pi)=\bar{x},\mathsf{Occ}(\pi)=W\right\}

where
h
is a simple history, where
c
is a (nonempty) simple cycle, and where
W\subseteq V
and
\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Pi}
.
The cycle
c
is called its
punishing cycle
.
For every
\beta\in\mathbb{N}
, a
\beta
-punishment family
is a punishment family with
\lVert\bar{x}\rVert\leq\beta
.
A
\beta
-punishment family is represented by the data
h
,
c
,
\bar{x}
and
W
, and that representation has a size smaller than or equal to the quantity
3\mathsf{card}V\lceil\mathsf{log}_{2}(\mathsf{card}V+1)\rceil+\beta
.

We write
hc^{\infty}\pi
for the punishment family
\{hc^{n}\pi^{\prime}~|~n>0,\mu(\pi^{\prime})=\mu(\pi),\mathsf{Occ}(\pi^{\prime})=\mathsf{Occ}(\pi)\}
.
Beware that the play
\pi
matters only for its payoff vector and the vertices it traverses: if we have
\mathsf{Occ}(\pi)=\mathsf{Occ}(\pi^{\prime})
and
\mu(\pi)=\mu(\pi^{\prime})
, then we have
hc^{\infty}\pi=hc^{\infty}\pi^{\prime}
.
We write
\mu(hc^{\infty}\pi)
for the common payoff vector of all elements of the set
hc^{\infty}\pi
, and we will say that
hc^{\infty}\pi
is
\lambda
-consistent if all its elements are (which is the case as soon as one of its elements is).
Furthermore, let us clarify that a punishment family is not an equivalence class: for example, in the game of Figure
6.19(a)
, the play
ab^{\omega}
belongs to both sets
a^{\infty}b^{\omega}
and
ab^{\infty}b^{\omega}
, which are distinct.

We can now define the
reduced negotiation game
, where Prover proposes
\beta
-punishment families instead of plays.

Definition

33

(Reduced negotiation game)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a mean-payoff game, let
\lambda
be a requirement, let
i
be a player, let
v_{0}\in V_{i}
and let
\beta
be a natural integer.
The corresponding
reduced negotiation game
is the game
\mathsf{red}^{\beta}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}=(\{\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{C}\},V^{\mathsf{r}},(V^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{P}},V^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}),E^{\mathsf{r}},\mu^{\mathsf{r}})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, where:

•

the vertices controlled by Prover are the vertices of
\mathcal{G}
, i.e. the set
V^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{P}}=V
;

•

the vertices controlled by Challenger are the vertices:

–

of the form
[hc^{\infty}\pi]
, where
hc^{\infty}\pi
is a
\lambda
-consistent
\beta
-punishment family;

–

of the form
(c,u)
, where
hc^{\infty}\pi
is a
\lambda
-consistent
\beta
-punishment family, and where there exists a vertex
\pi_{k}\in V_{i}
along the play
\pi
such that
\pi_{k}u\in E
;

–

of the form
[h^{\prime}v]
, where
hc^{\infty}\pi
is a
\lambda
-consistent
\beta
-punishment family, and the history
h^{\prime}v
is such that
h^{\prime}
is a prefix of the history
hc
, and
\mathsf{last}(h^{\prime})\in V_{i}
;

–

\top
and
\bot
;

•

with the same notations, the set
E^{\mathsf{r}}
contains the edges of the form:

–

v[hc^{\infty}\pi]
(Prover proposes a punishment family);

–

v\bot
(Prover gives up);

–

[hc^{\infty}\pi]\top
(Challenger accepts Prover’s proposal);

–

[hc^{\infty}\pi][h^{\prime}v]
(Challenger deviates before the punishing cycle—
pre-cycle deviation
);

–

[hc^{\infty}\pi](c,u)
(Challenger deviates after the punishing cycle—
post-cycle deviation
);

–

[h^{\prime}v]v
and
(c,u)u
(Prover has now to propose a new play);

–

\top\top
and
\bot\bot
(the play is over);

•

given a history
g=g_{0}\dots g_{n}\in\mathsf{Hist}\,\mathsf{red}^{\beta}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})
that does not reach the vertex
\bot
, we denote by
\dot{g}=h^{(1)}\dots h^{(n)}
the history or play in
\mathcal{G}
defined by, for each
k
:

–

if
g_{k-1}g_{k}=v[hc^{\infty}\pi]
, then
h^{(k)}
is empty;

–

if
g_{k-1}g_{k}=[hc^{\infty}\pi]\top
, then
h^{(k)}\dots h^{(n)}=hc^{\left|h^{(1)}\dots h^{(k-1)}h\right|^{2}}\pi
(the number of times the cycle
c
is repeated depends quadratically on time);

–

if
g_{k-1}g_{k}=[hc^{\infty}\pi][h^{\prime}v]
, then
h^{(k)}=h^{\prime}
;

–

if
g_{k-1}g_{k}=[hc^{\infty}\pi](c,v)
, then
h^{(k)}=hc^{\left|h^{(1)}\dots h^{(k-1)}h\right|^{2}}h^{\prime}
, where
h^{\prime}
is one of the shortest histories such that
\mathsf{Occ}(h^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{Occ}(\pi)
,
\mathsf{last}(h^{\prime})\in V_{i}
and
\mathsf{last}(h^{\prime})v\in E
;

–

if
g_{k-1}g_{k}=[h^{\prime}v]v
or
(c,v)v
, then
h^{(k)}
is empty;

and that definition is naturally extended to plays: for example, if
\mathcal{G}
is the game of Figure
6.19(a)
and if
\pi=a[ab^{\infty}a^{\omega}][aa]a[ab^{\infty}a^{\omega}](b,b)b[b^{\infty}a^{\omega}]\top^{\omega}
, then we write
\dot{\pi}=a\cdot ab^{2^{2}}\cdot ab^{7^{2}}a^{\omega}=a^{2}b^{4}ab^{49}a^{\omega}
;

•

the payoff function
\mu^{\mathsf{r}}
is defined, for each play
\pi
, by
\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)=-\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{P}}(\pi)=+\infty
if
\pi
reaches the vertex
\bot
, and by
\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)=-\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{P}}(\pi)=\mu_{i}(\dot{\pi})
otherwise.

Example

12
.

Figure
22
illustrates a (small) part of the game
\mathsf{red}_{\lambda\circ}^{2}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright a}
, where
\mathcal{G}
is the game of Figure
6.19(a)
, and
\lambda(a)=\lambda(b)=1
.
Blue vertices are owned by Prover, orange ones by Challenger.
When Prover proposes the punishment family
ab^{\infty}(a^{3}b^{3})^{\omega}
, the function
\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}
interprets it as the play
ab^{|h|^{2}}(a^{3}b^{3})^{\omega}
, where
h
is the history that has already been constructed so far.

a
ab^{\infty}(a^{3}b^{3})^{\omega}
aa
(b,b)
b
…
…
…
…
\top
\bot

Figure 22
:
The reduced negotiation game

Remark

9
.

Reduced negotiation games are Borel, and are played on a finite graph.

We will now prove that the reduced negotiation game captures the negotiation function, as do the abstract and concrete ones.
For that purpose, we first need the following key result.

Lemma

19
.

In a reduced negotiation game, Prover has a stationary optimal strategy.

Proof.

This lemma is a consequence of Lemma
2
: the payoff function
\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is concave.
Indeed, let
\xi
be a shuffling of two plays
\pi
and
\chi
.
If either
\pi
or
\chi
reaches the vertex
\bot
(in which case both do), then we immediately have
\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\dot{\xi})\leq\maxop\{\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\dot{\pi}),\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\dot{\chi})\}=+\infty
.
Otherwise, the play
\dot{\xi}
is a shuffling of
\dot{\pi}
and
\dot{\chi}
, and since mean-payoff objectives defined with a limit inferior are convex, we have
\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\dot{\xi})\leq\maxop\{\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\dot{\pi}),\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\dot{\chi})\}
.
∎

This lemma enables us to prove that the reduced negotiation game is equivalent to the other negotiation games.

Lemma

20
.

There exists a polynomial
P_{5}
such that for every mean-payoff game
\mathcal{G}
, every requirement
\lambda
with rational values, each player
i
and each
v_{0}\in V_{i}
, for every
\beta\geq P_{5}(\lVert\mathcal{G}\rVert+\lVert\lambda\rVert)
, we have
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v_{0})=\mathsf{val}_{\mathfrak{C}}\left(\mathsf{red}^{\beta}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\right).

Proof.

For every mean-payoff game
\mathcal{G}
and every requirement
\lambda
, we assume:

\beta\geq P_{1}\circ P_{2}\left(\lVert\left\{\mathsf{mp}(c)~|~c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(\mathcal{G})\right\}\rVert\right)

and for each
v\in V
:

\beta\geq\lVert\lambda(v)\rVert+3,

which are indeed quantities that are bounded by a polynomial of
\lVert\mathcal{G}\rVert+\lVert\lambda\rVert
.

Contrary to what we did with parity games in the proof of
7
, here, the equivalence between the reduced and the abstract game cannot be established directly (the proof would be too technical): we need to get back to the definition of the negotiation function.

\blacktriangleright
First direction:
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v_{0})\geq\mathsf{val}_{\mathfrak{C}}\left(\mathsf{red}^{\beta}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\right).

Let
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
be a strategy profile in
\mathcal{G}
that is
\lambda
-rational assuming a strategy
\sigma_{i}
, and let
x=\supop_{\sigma^{\prime}_{i}}\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle
.
We wish to prove that there exists a strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
in the reduced negotiation game such that
\supop_{\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}}\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle\leq x
.
Thus, we will have proved that the quantity
\mathsf{val}_{\mathfrak{C}}\left(\mathsf{red}^{\beta}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\right)
is smaller than or equal to every such
x
, and therefore smaller than or equal to
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v_{0})
.

Let us define simultaneously the strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
and a mapping
\varphi:\mathsf{Hist}_{\mathfrak{P}}\left(\mathsf{red}^{\beta}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\right)\rightarrow\mathsf{Hist}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, such that for each history
g
, the punishment family
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}(g)
will be defined from the play
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright\varphi(g)}\rangle
.
We guarantee inductively that if
g\in\mathsf{Hist}_{\mathfrak{P}}\left(\mathsf{red}^{\beta}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\right)
is compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
, then
\varphi(g)\in\mathsf{Hist}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
is compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
.
First, let us define
\varphi(v_{0})=v_{0}
.

Let
g\in\mathsf{Hist}_{\mathfrak{P}}\left(\mathsf{red}^{\beta}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\right)
be a history compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
as it has been defined so far, and such that
\varphi(g)
has already been defined.
Let
\chi^{0}=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright\varphi(g)}\rangle
.
By induction hypothesis, the history
\varphi(g)
is compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
, hence the play
\chi^{0}
is
\lambda
-consistent, and satisfies
\mu_{i}(\chi^{0})\leq x
.

Let
\chi^{0}_{\leq\ell}
be the shortest prefix of
\chi^{0}
that is not simple, i.e. such that there exists
k<\ell
with
\chi^{0}_{k}=\chi^{0}_{\ell}
.
If we have
\mathsf{mp}_{i}(\chi^{0}_{k+1}\dots\chi^{0}_{\ell})\leq x
, then we define:

\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}(g)=\left[\chi^{0}_{\leq k}\left(\chi^{0}_{k+1}\dots\chi^{0}_{\ell}\right)^{\infty}\pi\right],

where
\pi
is a play such that
\mathsf{Occ}(\pi)=\mathsf{Occ}\left(\chi^{0}_{>\ell}\right)
, that
\mu_{i}(\pi)\leq x
, and that
\lVert\mu(\pi)\rVert\leq\beta
.
Such a play exists, because the polytope:

Z=\left\{\mu(\pi)~\left|~\begin{matrix}\forall j,\forall v\in V_{j}\cap\mathsf{Occ}(\chi^{0}),\mu_{j}(\pi)\geq\lambda(v),\\
\mathrm{and~}\mathsf{Occ}(\pi)=\mathsf{Occ}\left(\chi^{0}_{>\ell}\right)\end{matrix}\right.\right\}

is nonempty (it contains the vector
\mu(\chi^{0}_{>\ell})
), and has at least one vertex
\bar{z}
with
z_{i}\leq x
(because we have
\mu_{i}(\chi^{0}_{>\ell})\leq x
), which by Lemma
13
and Corollary
1
has size
\lVert\bar{z}\rVert\leq\beta
.

Otherwise, if we have
\mathsf{mp}_{i}(\chi^{0}_{k+1}\dots\chi^{0}_{\ell})>x
, we define
\chi^{1}=\chi^{0}_{\leq k}\chi^{0}_{>\ell}
, and we iterate the process, which does necessarily terminate since we have
\mu_{i}(\chi^{0})\leq x
.
As a consequence, it effectively defines the proposal:

\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}(g)=\left[\chi^{n}_{\leq k}\left(\chi^{n}_{k+1}\dots\chi^{n}_{\ell}\right)^{\infty}\pi\right],

for some
n
.
Then, for each prefix
hv
, we define:

\varphi\left(g\left[\chi^{n}_{\leq k}\left(\chi^{n}_{k+1}\dots\chi^{n}_{\ell}\right)^{\infty}\pi\right][hv]v\right)=\varphi(g)\chi^{0}_{\leq m},

where
\chi^{0}_{\leq m}
is the prefix of
\chi^{0}
from which
n
simple cycles have been pulled out to obtain the prefix
h
of
\chi^{n}
; and similarly, for each pair
(c,v)
, we define:

\varphi\left(g\left[\chi^{n}_{\leq k}\left(\chi^{n}_{k+1}\dots\chi^{n}_{\ell}\right)^{\infty}\pi\right](c,v)v\right)=\varphi(g)\chi^{0}_{\leq p},

where
\chi^{0}_{\leq p}
is the prefix of
\chi^{0}
from which
n
simple cycles have been pulled out to obtain the shortest prefix
\chi^{n}_{\leq p}
of
\chi^{n}
such that
\chi^{n}_{p}\in V_{i}
and
\chi^{n}_{p}v\in E
.

Thus, the mapping
\varphi
is defined on every history compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
, and the image of such a history is always a history compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
.
We define it arbitrarily on other histories.
Note that for each history
g
, the history
\dot{g}
can be obtained from
\varphi(g)
by pulling out cycles
c
satisfying
\mathsf{mp}_{i}(c)>x
, and adding cycles
d
with
\mathsf{mp}_{i}(d)\leq x
.
As a consequence, if we have
\mathsf{mp}_{i}(\varphi(g))\leq x
, then we have
\mathsf{mp}_{i}(\dot{g})\leq x
—and the same result is true when we naturally extend the mapping
\varphi
to plays.

Let us now prove that we have
\supop_{\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}}\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle\leq\supop_{\sigma^{\prime}_{i}}\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle
.
Let
\xi
be a play compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
:

–

the vertex
\bot
does not appear in
\xi
, because Prover’s strategy does never use a edge to it.

–

If the play
\xi
has the form
\xi=g[hc^{\infty}\pi]\top^{\omega}
: then, we have
\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\xi)=\mu_{i}(\pi)\leq x
.

–

If the play
\xi
is made of infinitely many deviations: the play
\varphi(\xi)
is compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
, hence
\mu_{i}(\varphi(\xi))\leq x
; which implies
\mu_{i}(\dot{\chi})\leq x
, i.e.
\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\xi)\leq x
.

\blacktriangleright
Second direction:
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v_{0})\leq\mathsf{val}_{\mathfrak{C}}\left(\mathsf{red}^{\beta}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\right).

Let
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
be a stationary strategy for Prover in the reduced negotiation game, and let
y=\supop_{\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}}\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle
.
We want to show that
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v_{0})\leq y
: by Lemma
19
, it will be enough to conclude.
If we have
y=+\infty
, it is clear.
Let us assume
y\neq+\infty
.
Then, we will define a strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
, where
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
is
\lambda
-rational assuming
\sigma_{i}
, such that
\supop_{\sigma^{\prime}_{i}}\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle\leq y
.
We proceed inductively by defining the play
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}\rangle
for each history
hv
compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
such that
h
is empty, or
\mathsf{last}(h)\in V_{i}
and
v\neq\sigma_{i}(h)
.
Such a history is called a
bud history
.
After other histories, the strategy profile can be defined arbitrarily.
To that end, we construct a mapping
\psi
which maps each bud history to a history
\psi(hv)\in\mathsf{Hist}_{\mathfrak{P}}\left(\mathsf{red}_{\lambda i}^{\beta}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\right)
that is compatible with
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
.
This mapping will induce a definition of
{\bar{\sigma}}
: since
y\neq+\infty
, we have
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}(\psi(hv))\neq\bot
: let then
[h^{\prime}c^{\infty}\pi]=\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}(\psi(hv))
.
We then define
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}\rangle=h^{\prime}c^{|hh^{\prime}|^{2}}\pi
, which is a
\lambda
-consistent play since
h^{\prime}c^{\infty}\pi
is a
\lambda
-consistent punishment family, by definition of the reduced negotiation game.

Let now
h_{0}v
be a bud history: we assume that
{\bar{\sigma}}
has been defined on every prefix of
h_{0}
, but not on
h_{0}v
itself.
If
h_{0}
is empty, that is if
h_{0}v=v_{0}
, then we define
\psi(h_{0}v)=\psi(v_{0})=v_{0}
.
Otherwise, let us write
h_{0}=h_{1}wh_{2}
, where
h_{1}w
is the longest prefix of
h_{0}
that is a bud history—that is, its longest prefix such that
\psi(h_{1}w)
has been defined, or its shortest prefix such that
wh_{2}
is compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h_{1}w}
.
Let
g=\psi(h_{1}w)
, and let
[hc^{\infty}\pi]=\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}(g)
.
We have defined
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h_{1}w}\rangle=hc^{|h_{1}h|^{2}}\pi
, and consequently, the history
wh_{2}
is a prefix of that play.
If it is a prefix of the history
hc
, then we define
\psi(h_{0}v)=g[hc^{\infty}\pi][wh_{2}v]v
.
Otherwise, we define
\psi(h_{0}v)=g[hc^{\infty}\pi](c,v)v
.

Now, the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
has been defined, and since all the punishment families proposed by Prover are
\lambda
-consistent, the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
is
\lambda
-rational assuming
\sigma_{i}
.
Let
\chi
be a play compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i}
, and let us prove that
\mu_{i}(\chi)\leq y
.
If
\chi
has finitely many prefixes that are bud histories, then let
\chi_{\leq n}
be the longest one: we have
\chi_{\geq n}=hc^{n+|h|}\pi
, where
[hc^{\infty}\pi]=\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}(\psi(\chi_{\leq n}))
.
Then, we have
\mu_{i}(\chi)=\mu_{i}(\pi)\leq y
.

Now, if
\chi
has infinitely many such prefixes, then there exists a unique play
\pi
in the reduced negotiation game such that for any prefix
\chi_{\leq n}
of
\chi
that is a bud history, the history
\psi(\chi_{\leq n})
is a prefix of
\pi
.
Then, if
\pi
contains finitely many post-cycle deviations, then there exist two indices
m
and
n
such that
\chi_{\geq m}=\dot{\pi}_{\geq n}
, hence
\mu_{i}(\chi)=\mu_{i}(\dot{\pi})\leq y
.

Finally, if
\pi
contains infinitely many post-cycle deviations, i.e. infinitely many occurrences of a vertex of the form
(c,v)
, then let us choose such vertex that minimizes the quantity
\mathsf{mp}_{i}(c)
.
The play
\chi
has the form:

\chi=h_{0}c^{k_{0}^{2}}h_{1}c^{k_{1}^{2}}h_{2}\dots,

where for each
n
, we have
k_{n}=\left|h_{0}c^{k_{0}^{2}}\dots c^{k_{n-1}^{2}}h_{n}\right|
.
Then, if we write
M=\maxop r_{i}
, we have:

\mathsf{mp}_{i}\left(h_{0}c^{k_{0}^{2}}\dots h_{n}c^{k_{n}^{2}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{k_{n}+k_{n}^{2}|c|-1}\left(k_{n}M+\left(k_{n}^{2}|c|-1\right)\mathsf{mp}_{i}(c)\right),

which converges to
\mathsf{mp}_{i}(c)
when
n
tends to
+\infty
, hence
\mu_{i}(\chi)\leq\mathsf{mp}_{i}(c)
.
Now, since
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
is stationary, there exists a play of the form
gd^{\omega}
that is compatible with it, and such that
(c,v)\in\mathsf{Occ}(d)\subseteq\mathsf{Inf}(\pi)
; and by definition of
y
, we have
\mathsf{mp}_{i}(\dot{d})=\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(gd^{\omega})\leq y
.
By minimality of
\mathsf{mp}_{i}(c)
, we have
\mathsf{mp}_{i}(\dot{d})=\mathsf{mp}_{i}(c)
, hence
\mathsf{mp}_{i}(c)\leq y
, and therefore
\mu_{i}(\chi)\leq y
.
∎

Thus, a given requirement
\lambda
is an
\varepsilon
-fixed point of the negotiation function if and only if for each
i
and
v\in V_{i}
, there exists a stationary strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}
in the game
\mathsf{red}_{\lambda i}^{\beta}
, with
\beta=P_{5}(\lVert\mathcal{G}\rVert+\lVert\lambda\rVert)
, such that
\supop_{\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}}\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle\leq\lambda(v)+\varepsilon
.
The reduced negotiation game has an exponential size, but it contains only
\mathsf{card}V
vertices that are controlled by Prover: stationary strategies for Prover are therefore objects of polynomial size.
Such strategies will be called
reduced
strategies, and can alternatively be seen as simple strategies in the asbtract negotiation game, as suggested in the end of
section

6.6.1
.
They constitute the third and last piece of our notion of witness.

6.7  Algorithm and complexity

We are now in a position to define formally our notion of witness.

Definition

34

(Witness)
.

Let
I=(\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}},\bar{x},\bar{y},\varepsilon)
be an instance of the
\varepsilon
-SPE constrained existence problem.
A
witness
for
I
is a tuple
\left(W,W^{\prime},\bar{\bar{\alpha}},\lambda,(\tau^{v}_{\mathfrak{P}})_{v}\right)
, where:

•

W\subseteq W^{\prime}\subseteq V
;

•

\bar{\bar{\alpha}}\in[0,1]^{\Pi\times\mathsf{SCyc}(W)}
;

•

\lambda
is a requirement;

•

and each
\tau^{v}_{\mathfrak{P}}
is a stationary strategy in the game
\mathsf{red}^{\beta}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v}
, where
\beta=P_{5}(\lVert\mathcal{G}\rVert+\lVert\lambda\rVert)
.

A witness is
valid
if:

•

each strategy
\tau^{v}_{\mathfrak{P}}
satisfies the inequality
\supop_{\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}}\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle\tau^{v}_{\mathfrak{P}},\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}\rangle\leq\lambda(v)+\varepsilon
;

•

the sets
W
and
W^{\prime}
and the tuple of tuples
\bar{\bar{\alpha}}
satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem
18
.

The
\varepsilon
-SPE constrained existence problem will be
\mathsf{NP}
-easy if we show, first, that there exists a valid witness of polynomial size if and only if the instance is positive, and second, that the validity of a witness can be decided in polynomial time.
The former is a consequence of
8
,
17
,
18
,
19
and
20
.

Lemma

21
.

There exists a polynomial
P_{6}
such that an instance
I
of the
\varepsilon
-SPE constrained existence problem admits a valid witness of size at most
P_{6}(\lVert I\rVert)
if and only if it is a positive instance.

Let us now tackle the latter.

Lemma

22
.

Given an instance of the
\varepsilon
-SPE constrained existence problem and a witness for it, deciding whether that witness is valid is
\mathsf{P}
-easy.

Proof.

The validity of a witness has been defined by two conditions.
As regards the second one, all the hypotheses of Lemma
18
can be checked in polynomial time with classical algorithms.
Let us now show how the first condition can also be checked in polynomial time.

Let us recall that in our algorithm for parity games, at the same point, we constructed the game induced by each strategy guessed for Prover, and searched for a strategy for Challenger, i.e. for a play in that game, that would contradict the claim that Prover’s strategy is winning.
Such a play could be seen as a winning path for either a reachability or a parity objective.
Here, the idea will be similar, but we need to consider Prover’s strategy in the reduced negotiation game, so that it indeed induces a finite graph; and then, the search of a path for Challenger will be slightly more complicated.

Let
n=\mathsf{card}V
.
Given a stationary strategy
\tau^{v}_{\mathfrak{P}}
of Prover in a reduced negotiation game, one can construct in a time polynomial in
\lVert\tau^{v}_{\mathfrak{P}}\rVert
the game
\mathsf{red}_{\lambda i}^{\beta}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v}[\tau^{v}_{\mathfrak{P}}]
.
The underlying graph of that game has indeed a polynomial size, because it is composed only of:

–

at most
n
vertices of the form
w\in V
;

–

at most
n
vertices of the form
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}(w)
(either equal to
\bot
or of the form
[hc^{\infty}\pi]
);

–

at most
n^{2}
vertices of the form
(c,w)
;

–

at most
2n^{2}
vertices of the form
[h^{\prime}w^{\prime}]
, where
h^{\prime}
is a prefix of the history
hc
for some punishment family
[hc^{\infty}\pi]=\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}(w)
;

–

possibly the vertices
\top
and
\bot
.

We call this connected graph the
deviation graph
.
Note that if among those vertices, there is the vertex
\bot
, then since the vertices that are not accessible have been removed, we have
\supop_{\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}}\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle=+\infty
and the problem can be solved immediately.
In what follows, we assume that it is not the case, i.e. that for each
w
, the vertex
\tau_{\mathfrak{P}}(w)
has the form
[hc^{\infty}\pi]
.
Deciding whether
\supop_{\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}}\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle\leq\alpha
is then equivalent to deciding whether there exists a path
\pi
, in that graph, such that we have
\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)>\alpha
.
Such a play can have three forms.

–

It can end in the vertex
\top
, i.e. with Challenger accepting Prover’s proposal.
The existence of such a play can be decided immediately, by checking whether in the deviation graph, there exists a vertex of the form
[hc^{\infty}\pi]
with
\mu_{i}(\pi)>\alpha
.

–

It can avoid the vertex
\top
, and comprise finitely many post-cycle deviations.
This is the case if and only if there exists a cycle
d
in the deviation graph, without post-cycle deviations, such that we have
\mathsf{mp}_{i}(\dot{d})>\alpha
.
The existence of such a cycle can be decided in polynomial time with Karp’s algorithm
[
DBLP:journals/dm/Karp78
]
.

–

It can avoid the vertex
\top
, and comprise infinitely many post-cycle deviations.
In that case, we have
\mu^{\mathsf{r}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\pi)\leq\mathsf{mp}_{i}(c)
for each vertex of the form
(c,w)
appearing infinitely often along
\pi
; then, there exists a cycle
d
in the deviation graph, such that every vertex of the form
(c,w)
along
d
satisfies
\mathsf{mp}_{i}(c)>\alpha
.
Conversely, if such a cycle exists, then
\pi
exists.
The existence of such a cycle can be decided in polynomial time with Karp’s algorithm.

Therefore, the existence of such a play is decidable in polynomial time.
∎

Thus, given an instance of the
\varepsilon
-SPE constrained existence problem, a valid witness can be guessed and checked in polynomial time.
Applying
9
, we finally obtain the following theorem.

Theorem

15
.

The
\varepsilon
-SPE constrained existence problem in mean-payoff games is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete.
Hardness still holds when there is only one effective upper threshold.

Chapter 7:  Energy and discounted-sum games

We now tackle subgame-perfect equilibria in energy and in discounted-sum games.
Here, the negotiation function will not be used, and our results and proofs will mostly be extensions of those presented in
part

II
.

7.1  Discounted-sum games

7.1.1 Hardness

We showed in
part

II
that the constrained existence problem of NEs in discounted-sum games was at least as hard as the target discounted-sum problem, whose decidability remains an open problem.
That result also holds when we replace NEs with SPEs.

Theorem

16
.

The TDS problem reduces to the constrained existence problem of SPEs in discounted-sum games.
This hardness result still holds when there is no effective lower threshold, and only one effective upper threshold.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of
3
.
∎

7.1.2 Co-recursive enumerability

Since, by
16
, we know that finding an algorithm for the SPE constrained existence problem would imply solving a long-standing open problem, we only present a semi-algorithm that recognizes its negative instances, leaving decidability as an open question.

Theorem

17
.

In discounted-sum games, the constrained existence problem of SPEs is co-recursively enumerable.

Proof.

We present here a modified version of the semi-algorithm presented in the proof of
4
, so that it recognizes the negative instances of the SPE constrained existence problem.

\blacktriangleright
Algorithm

Let
\left(h^{(n)}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a recursive enumeration of the nonempty histories in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
by increasing order of lengths (we can, for example, order histories of the same length with the lexicographic order induced by some arbitrary order on vertices).

Now, let
T
be the infinite tree whose nodes of depth
n+1
are all possible
n
-uples
\left({\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(0)}),\dots,{\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(n)})\right)
, and where the children of the node
\left({\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(0)}),\dots,{\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(n)})\right)
are the nodes of the form:

\left({\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(0)}),\dots,{\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(n)}),{\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(n+1)})\right).

Thus, every node partially defines a complete strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
, and every infinite branch entirely defines it—and every complete strategy profile is defined by an infinite branch.

A node
\left({\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(0)}),\dots,{\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(n)})\right)
is called
subgame-irrational
if there exist two indices
\ell,m\leq n
, with
|h^{(\ell)}|=|h^{(m)}|=p
, an index
k\leq p
and a player
i
such that:

–

we have
h^{(\ell)}_{\leq k}=h^{(m)}_{\leq k}
;

–

the history
h^{(\ell)}_{\geq k}
is compatible with the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h^{(\ell)}_{\leq k}}
, as partially defined by the node;

–

the history
h^{(m)}_{\geq k}
is compatible with the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright h^{(m)}_{\leq k}}
;

–

and finally, we have:

\mathsf{ds}_{i}\left(h^{(m)}\right)-\mathsf{ds}_{i}\left(h^{(\ell)}\right)>2M\beta^{p-1}.

The same node is called
off-topic
if for some player
i
we have
\mathsf{ds}_{i}\left(h\right)>y_{i}-M\beta^{|h|-1}
, or
\mathsf{ds}_{i}(h)<x_{i}+M\beta^{|h|-1}
, where
h
is the longest history that is compatible with
{\bar{\sigma}}
as defined so far.

Our algorithm consists in constructing the tree
T^{\prime}
, obtained from the tree
T
by cutting every branch after the first subgame-irrational or off-topic node, and terminating once that construction is finished.

\blacktriangleright
Correctness

Our correctness proof will use
3
, that was proved inside the proof of
4
.

To show that our algorithm is correct, we must prove that the tree
T^{\prime}
will be finite if and only if we have a negative instance of the constrained existence problem.
By Kőnig’s lemma, that will be the case if and only if every branch is finite, i.e., if and and only if every branch of the tree
T
contains either an off-topic or a Nash-irrational node.
Therefore, we will be done if we prove that, given a branch of
T
and the corresponding strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
, the branch contains no such node if and only if the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is an SPE and satisfies
\bar{x}\leq\mu\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle\leq\bar{y}
.

Off-topic nodes.

Using
3
, we know that we have
\bar{x}\leq\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle\leq\bar{y}
if and only if the corresponding branch contains no off-topic node.

If the branch contains a subgame-irrational node, then the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is not an SPE.

Let us assume that some branch contains a subgame-irrational node
\left({\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(0)}),\dots,{\bar{\sigma}}(h^{(n)})\right)
.
Let us use the notations
k,\ell,m,p
and
i
from the definition of subgame-irrationality.
Let us define
\pi=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h^{(\ell)}_{\leq k}}\rangle
: note that the history
h^{(\ell)}_{\geq k}
is a prefix of length
p-k
of the play
\pi
.
Similarly, let us extend the history
h^{(m)}_{\geq k}
into some play
\pi^{\prime}
, compatible with the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright h^{(m)}_{\leq k}}
.
By
3
, the inequality:

\mathsf{ds}_{i}\left(h^{(m)}\right)-\mathsf{ds}_{i}\left(h^{(\ell)}\right)>2M\beta^{p-1}

implies
\mu_{i}(h^{(\ell)}_{<k}\pi^{\prime})>\mu_{i}(h^{(\ell)}_{<k}\pi)
, and therefore the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is not an SPE.

Conversely, if
{\bar{\sigma}}
is not an SPE, then there exists a history
hv
, a player
i
and a strategy
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
such that we have:

\mu_{i}(h\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}\rangle)<\mu_{i}(h\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright hv},\sigma^{\prime}_{i\upharpoonright hv}\rangle).

Then, let
\pi=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}\rangle
, and let
\pi^{\prime}=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright hv},\sigma^{\prime}_{i\upharpoonright hv}\rangle
: since we have
\mu_{i}(h\pi)<\mu_{i}(h\pi^{\prime})
, by
3
, there exists an index
q
such that:

\mathsf{ds}_{i}\left(h\pi_{\leq q}\right)+M\beta^{|h|+q-1}<\mathsf{ds}_{i}\left(\pi^{\prime}_{\leq q}\right)-M\beta^{|h|+q-1}.

Let now
\ell
and
m
be the indices such that
h^{(\ell)}=h\pi_{\leq q}
, and
h^{(m)}=h\pi^{\prime}_{\leq q}
.
Let
p=|h^{(\ell)}|=|h^{(m)}|
, and let
k=|h|
.
Then, we have
\mathsf{ds}_{i}\left(h^{(m)}_{\geq k}\right)-\mathsf{ds}_{i}\left(h^{(\ell)}_{\geq k}\right)>2M\beta^{p-1}
: along the branch corresponding to
{\bar{\sigma}}
, the node of depth
\maxop\{\ell,m\}
is subgame-irrational.
Which ends the proof.
∎

7.2  Energy games

7.2.1 Undecidability

We proved in
part

II
that the constrained existence problem of Nash equilibria in energy games is undecidable, since counter machines can be encoded as energy games.
It should therefore not come as a surprise that the same problem for SPEs is also undecidable.
We prove however a slightly stronger result: undecidability holds even when the game contains only two players.

Theorem

18
.

In energy games, the SPE constrained existence problem is undecidable, even on games with only two players, and even with no effective lower threshold and only one effective upper threshold.

Proof.

We proceed by reduction from the halting problem of a two-counter machine.

q_{0}

(a)

Gadget for the initial state

q_{\mathsf{f}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C_{2}}}{{-1}}

(b)

Gadget for the final state

q
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C}}{{1}}

(c)

Gadget for incrementations of counter
C\in\{C_{1},C_{2}\}

q
(if
C_{1}>0
)
q^{\prime}
(if
C_{1}=0
)
a
\triangle
\triangledown
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C_{1}}}{{-1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C_{1}}}{{-1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C_{1}}}{{-1}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C_{2}}}{{-1}}

(d)

Gadget for tests of counter
C_{1}

q
q^{\prime\prime}
(if
C_{2}=0
)
\triangle
q^{\prime\prime\prime}
(if
C_{2}>0
)
b
c
\triangle
\triangledown\!_{1}
\triangledown
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C_{2}}}{{-1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C_{2}}}{{-1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C_{2}}}{{-1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C_{1}}}{{-1}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C_{2}}}{{-1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C_{1}}}{{-1}}

(e)

Gadget for tests of counter
C_{2}

Figure 23
:
Gadgets

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a two-counter machine.
We define an energy game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright q_{0}}
with two players, player
C_{1}
and player
C_{2}
, by assembling the gadgets presented in
Figures

7.23(a)
,
7.23(b)
,
7.23(d)
,
7.23(e)
and
7.23(c)
.
The rewards that are not presented are equal to
0
, the round vertices are those controlled by player
C_{1}
, and the square ones are those controlled by player
C_{2}
.
For each state of the machine
\mathcal{K}
, we define from one to three vertices, plus six additional vertices, written
a
,
b
,
c
,
\triangle
,
\triangledown
, and
\triangledown\!_{1}
.

Then, a play in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright q_{0}}
that does not reach one of the sink vertices
\triangle
,
\triangledown
, or
\triangledown\!_{1}
simulates a sequence of transitions of the machine
\mathcal{K}
, that can be a run or not: at each step, the counter
C
is captured by the energy level of player
C
.
Let us now prove that the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright q_{0}}
admits an SPE where player
C_{2}
loses if and only if the machine
\mathcal{K}
terminates.

\blacktriangleright
If such an SPE exists, then the machine
\mathcal{K}
terminates.

Let us write
{\bar{\sigma}}
for such an SPE, and let
\pi=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
.
Let us show that the play
\pi
simulates a run of
\mathcal{K}
.
Since
\pi
is lost by player
C_{2}
, there are
a priori
three possibilities.

The play
\pi
reaches the vertex
\triangledown
.

Then, player
C_{1}
loses, and has therefore a profitable deviation by looping on
\pi_{0}=q_{0}
, which is impossible.

Player
C_{1}
makes player
C_{2}
lose by going to a vertex of the form
q^{\prime\prime\prime}
, when his energy level is zero.

Thus, the play
\pi
simulates a spurious run of
\mathcal{K}
, since such an action amounts to faking a test of
C_{2}
above zero.
But then, player
C_{2}
has a profitable deviation by going to
b
: there, player
C_{1}
cannot go to the vertex
\triangledown
, because it would make her lose, while she can win by going to the vertex
c
; indeed, from there, player
C_{2}
cannot go to the vertex
\triangledown\!_{1}
because it would make him lose, since he has no more energy, while he can go to the vertex
\triangle
and win—and let player
C_{1}
win.
Therefore, this case is also impossible.

The play
\pi
reaches the vertex
q_{\mathsf{f}}
.

Then, it simulates a correct run of the machine
\mathcal{K}
, that reaches the state
q_{\mathsf{f}}
.
Indeed, we have already shown that
\pi
cannot fake a test of
C_{2}
above
0
.
It cannot fake a test of
C_{1}
above
0
either, because then, player
C_{1}
would lose, while she can win by looping on
q_{0}
.
It cannot fake a test of
C_{2}
to
0
, because then, from the vertex
q^{\prime\prime}
, if player
C_{2}
’s energy is greater than
0
, he has a profitable deviation by going to the vertex
\triangle
.
Finally, it cannot fake a test of
C_{1}
to
0
, because then, from the vertex
q^{\prime}
, if player
C_{1}
’s energy is greater than
0
, player
C_{2}
has a profitable deviation by going to
a
, from where player
C_{1}
cannot go to the vertex
\triangledown
, because it would make her lose while she can win by going to
\triangle
.
Therefore, the machine
\mathcal{K}
terminates.

\blacktriangleright
If the machine
\mathcal{K}
terminates, then there is an SPE where player
C_{2}
loses.

If the machine
\mathcal{K}
terminates, let us define a strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright q_{0}}
as follows.

–

In tests of counter
C_{1}
, player
C_{1}
goes to
q^{\prime}
if and only if her energy level is zero or
\bot
; from
q^{\prime}
, player
C_{2}
goes to the vertex
a
if and only if player
C_{1}
’s energy level is positive.

–

In tests of counter
C_{2}
, player
C_{1}
goes to
q^{\prime\prime}
if and only if the energy level of player
C_{2}
is zero or
\bot
; from
q^{\prime\prime}
, player
C_{2}
goes to the vertex
\triangle
if and only if his energy level is positive, and from
q^{\prime\prime\prime}
, he goes to
b
if and only if it is zero or
\bot
.

–

From the vertex
a
, player
C_{1}
goes to the vertex
\triangledown
if and only if her energy level is zero or
\bot
.

–

From the vertex
b
, player
C_{1}
goes to the vertex
\triangledown
if and only if the energy level of player
C_{2}
is positive.

–

From the vertex
c
, player
C_{2}
goes to the vertex
\triangledown\!_{1}
if and only if his energy level is positive.

Let us show that the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is an SPE.
Let
hv
be a history from the vertex
q_{0}
: let us prove that
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}
is an NE.

If
v
is a vertex of the form
q
.

Then, the play
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}\rangle
simulates the correct run of the machine
\mathcal{K}
from
q
when the counters are initialized to
\mathsf{el}_{C_{1}}(hv)
and
\mathsf{el}_{C_{2}}(hv)
(if one of those energy levels is
\bot
, then it simulates the correct run of
\mathcal{K}
where that counter is locked to
0
).
Therefore, player
C_{1}
wins (or has already lost), hence she cannot have a profitable deviation.
As for player
C_{2}
, he cannot have a profitable deviation from a vertex of the form
q^{\prime}
: when such a vertex is reached, player
C_{1}
’s energy is zero or
\bot
, hence if player
C_{2}
chooses to go to
a
, player
C_{1}
will go to
\triangledown
and he will lose.
He cannot have a profitable deviation from a vertex of the form
q^{\prime\prime}
either: when such a vertex is reached, he has a zero or
\bot
energy level, hence if he chooses to go to the vertex
\triangle
, he loses.
Finally, he cannot have a profitable deviation from a vertex of the form
q^{\prime\prime\prime}
: when such a vertex is reached, he has a positive energy level, hence if he goes to the vertex
b
, player
C_{1}
will go to the vertex
\triangledown
and he will lose.

If
v
is a vertex of the form
q^{\prime}
.

Then, if player
C_{1}
’s energy level is positive, player
C_{2}
goes to
a
, then player
C_{1}
goes to
\triangle
, and both player
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
win—and have therefore no profitable deviation.
Otherwise, the substrategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}
simulates a correct run of
\mathcal{K}
, and we can use the same arguments as in the previous point.

If
v
is a vertex of the form
q^{\prime\prime}
.

Then, if player
C_{2}
’s energy level is positive, he goes to
\triangle
and wins—and no player has a profitable deviation.
Otherwise, the substrategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}
simulates a correct run of
\mathcal{K}
, and we can use the same arguments than in the first point.

If
v
is a vertex of the form
q^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

Then, if player
C_{2}
’s energy level is zero or
\bot
, player
C_{2}
goes to
b
, then player
C_{1}
goes to
c
, and finally player
C_{2}
goes to
\triangle
, and both player
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
win—they have therefore no profitable deviation.
Otherwise, the substrategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hv}
simulates a correct run of
\mathcal{K}
, and we can use the same arguments than in the first point.

If
v=a
.

Then, either player
C_{1}
has a positive energy level, and then she goes to
\triangle
and wins, or she has a zero or
\bot
energy level, and then she cannot win.

If
v=b
.

Then, either player
C_{2}
has a zero or
\bot
energy level, and then the play ends in
\triangle
and both players win (or have already lost), or he has a positive energy level, and then player
C_{1}
cannot win, since player
C_{2}
plans to go to
\triangledown\!_{1}
.

If
v=c
.

Then, either
C_{2}
has a positive energy level, and he goes to
\triangledown\!_{1}
and wins, or he has a zero energy level, and he goes to
\triangle
and wins, or he has already lost.

If
v=\triangledown
,
\triangledown\!_{1}
,
q_{\mathsf{f}}
or
\triangle
.

Then, the proof is immediate.

Therefore, the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is an SPE, that simulates the run of the machine
\mathcal{K}
.
That run terminates, hence the play
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
reaches the vertex
q_{\mathsf{f}}
, and is lost by player
C_{2}
.
Which concludes the proof.
∎

7.2.2 About recursive enumerability

Like for NEs, this proof shows that, in particular, the SPE constrained existence problem is not co-recursively enumerable in energy games.
It might still be the case that it is recursively enumerable.
That would in particular be the case if finite memory was sufficient for an SPE to achieve a given payoff vector, when that is possible, as in the case of NEs.
Unfortunately, one cannot follow this approach, because that statement is false: in order to be able to punish some player, without making another player lose, an SPE may have to memorize their energy levels, and therefore require infinite memory.

Theorem

19
.

There exists an energy game in which there exists an SPE such that no finite-memory SPE generates the same payoff vector.

Proof.

In the energy game presented in Figure
24
, let us show that there exists an SPE that makes player
\Box
lose, but that no finite-memory SPE can achieve that result.

a
b
c
d
e
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Diamond}}{{1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Diamond}}{{1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{-1}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{-1}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Diamond}}{{-1}}

Figure 24
:
A game where infinite memory is necessary to make player
\Box
lose

\blacktriangleright
There is an SPE in which player
\Box
loses.

Consider the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
defined by
{\bar{\sigma}}(a)=c
, by
{\bar{\sigma}}(hca)=b
for every history
hca
, by
{\bar{\sigma}}(hb)=d
for every history
hb
, by
{\bar{\sigma}}(hba)=c
for every history
hba
, by
{\bar{\sigma}}(hc)=d
for every history
hc
, and finally by
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright hd}\rangle=d^{\mathsf{el}_{\circ}(hd)+1}e^{\omega}
for every history
hd
.

Intuitively: in every subgame, player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
makes player
\Box
and player
\Diamond
lose.
But to do so, she needs one of those players to cooperate with her: for example, in the main subgame
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright a}
, she wants to make player
\Box
lose, and to do so, she traverses the vertex
c
to go to the vertex
d
.
But then, player
\Diamond
may deviate, and go back to the vertex
a
.
Then, player
\Diamond
has to be punished: and to do so, player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
must go to the vertex
d
through the vertex
b
, with player
\Box
’s cooperation…and so on.
Once the vertex
d
is reached (which will eventually be the case in every subgame), player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
’s energy level is equal to player
\Box
’s and player
\Diamond
’s one, plus 1.
Thus, to make those players lose without losing herself, player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
loops on the vertex
d
exactly the right number of times, before going to the vertex
e
.

Thus, the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is an SPE in which player
\Box
loses—but it is not a finite-memory SPE.

\blacktriangleright
There is no finite-memory SPE in which player
\Box
loses.

Let
{\bar{\tau}}
be a finite-memory SPE in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright a}
, compatible with a memory structure
\mathcal{M}
, and let us assume toward contradiction that
\mu_{\Box}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle=0
.
Since we have
\mu_{\Box}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle=0
(and therefore
\mu_{\diamond}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle=0
, since players
\Box
and
\Diamond
always receive the same rewards), we also have, by induction and because
{\bar{\tau}}
is an SPE, the equality
\mu_{\Box}\langle{\bar{\tau}}_{\upharpoonright ha}\rangle=\mu_{\diamond}\langle{\bar{\tau}}_{\upharpoonright ha}\rangle=0
for every history
h
that is compatible with
\sigma_{\circ}
.

Let now
n
be the number of states of the memory structure
\mathcal{M}
, and let us consider a history
ha
with
|h|=2n
(and therefore
\mathsf{el}_{\circ}(ha)=\mathsf{el}_{\Box}(ha)=\mathsf{el}_{\diamond}(ha)=n
).
By the previous proposition, we know that
\mu_{\Box}\langle{\bar{\tau}}_{\upharpoonright ha}\rangle=\mu_{\diamond}\langle{\bar{\tau}}_{\upharpoonright ha}\rangle=0
, i.e. that the play
\langle{\bar{\tau}}_{\upharpoonright ha}\rangle
reaches the vertex
d
, and takes the edge
dd
more than
n
times.
Since the memory structure
\mathcal{M}
has only
n
states, it means that that play actually loops on the vertex
d
infinitely often, and is therefore also lost by player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
.
Then, player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
has a profitable deviation in that subgame, by going to the vertex
e
: contradiction.

There exists an SPE that makes player
\Box
lose in that game, but no finite-memory one.
∎

We leave therefore the question open.

Open Problem

3
.

Is the constrained existence problem of SPEs in energy games recursively enumerable?

Chapter 8:  About rational verification and its limits

We have now established all our results concerning the constrained existence problem of NEs and SPEs.
Before turning to more specialized notions of equilibria, as we will do in
part

IV
, we use this chapter to discuss a possible application of our results:
rational verification
.
Our observations will lead us to define a new decision problem, for which we also provide tight complexity bounds.

8.1  Rational verification

Verification
, without further precision, usually refers to the study of computational systems (typically abstracted as collections of automata or machine models) with the goal of verifying whether they satisfy some property, called a
specification
.

A particular case of verification is
reactive verification
, already described in
section

1.1
, where the computational model under study interacts with an
environment
that may behave unpredictably.
In such a case, one seeks to check whether the system satisfies the specification against every possible behavior of the environment.
Illustrations of this concept often involve coffee machines; for the sake of detoxification, let us instead consider an elevator.
Its specification could be that, for every
i
, if at some point in time a user presses button
i
, then the elevator must eventually reach floor
i
(or reach it within a specified duration).
If the elevator is located in a place where its use is essential, and where a malfunction could have serious consequences—such as in a hospital—it is reasonable to require that the elevator always guarantees this specification, even if a mischievous child persistently presses button 1.
Such an example motivates the study of two-player zero-sum games, which serve as a relevant metaphor for reactive verification: if the system (the elevator) and the environment (the child) are two players, we can define the system’s objective as the specification, and we then want to verify whether the system’s strategy is winning.

Now, let us imagine that our system is a self-driving car.
It would have an obvious Boolean specification: it must not cause the death of any human, whether the passenger or any other person nearby.
This specification can be refined into a quantitative objective by defining priorities: for example, we may require that it does not kill any human, and if that condition is satisfied, we may further wish for it to reach a given destination from location
A
to location
B
, if possible within a reasonable duration.
In such a framework, it might seem excessively cautious to demand that the system satisfies its specification under
every
possible behavior of the environment—or equivalently, to treat the environment as a purely adversarial entity whose sole aim is to cause the specification to fail.
If we assume, for instance, that all drivers in the world suddenly conspire to assassinate the passenger, then the most rational strategy, given the defined priorities, would be to keep the car safely locked in a garage.
This example illustrates that, in some cases, it might be useful to employ the game metaphor without modeling the environment as a purely adversarial player, but rather as a player or coalition of players with their own objectives, acting rationally according to some notion of
rationality
.

This is the motivation of
rational verification
.

8.2  Definitions

Let us consider a game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
with one specific player, called
Leader
, and denoted by
{\mathfrak{L}}\in\Pi
.
Our decision problem will take as input a strategy
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}
for Leader (given as a memory structure), and ask whether that strategy ensures a given specification (encoded by Leader’s payoff function and a threshold) against all
rational
responses of the other players.

We must therefore define an adapted notion of rationality, that does not qualify a strategy profile for all players, but for all players except Leader.

Definition

35

(
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed NE,
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed SPE)
.

A strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is a
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed Nash equilibrium
, or
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed NE
, if for each player
i\neq{\mathfrak{L}}
and every strategy
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
, we have
\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\rangle\leq\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
.
It is a
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed subgame-perfect equilibrium
, or
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed SPE
, if for every history
h
, the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h}
is a
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed NE.

We can then define the rational verification problem, for a given class of game
\mathcal{C}
and a given
rationality concept

\rho\in\{\mathsf{Nash},\mathsf{SubgamePerfect}\}
.
A (
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed)
\rho
-equilibrium
is a Nash equilibrium if
\rho=\mathsf{Nash}
, and an SPE if
\rho=\mathsf{SubgamePerfect}
.
Let us recall that memory structures have been defined in
6
, and that a non-deterministic memory structure
\mathcal{M}
for a player
i
, from a given vertex
v_{0}
, induces a set
\mathsf{Ind}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
of strategies for player
i
.

Problem

3

((Deterministic)
\rho
-rational verification problem in the class
\mathcal{C}
)
.

Given a game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\in\mathcal{C}
, a threshold
t\in\mathbb{Q}
and a (deterministic) memory structure
\mathcal{M}
for Leader on
\mathcal{G}
, does every
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed
\rho
-equilibrium
{\bar{\sigma}}
with
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathsf{Ind}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
satisfy the inequality
\mu_{\mathfrak{L}}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle>t
?

8.3  Link with the constrained existence problem and complexities

Rational verification problems are phrased in a way that makes them particularly annoying to treat directly, since their instances contain two graph structures: the game
\mathcal{G}
, and the memory structure
\mathcal{M}
.
However, the experienced reader may have noticed that this annoyance can easily be removed, by considering the product of those two structures, or equivalently, by incorporating the memory structure
\mathcal{M}
in the game, so that Leader’s moves are constrained by the game structure itself.
To capture non-determinism, a fictional player must be added, who will choose which strategy
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathsf{Ind}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
Leader will follow, without having any interest in that choice (his payoff function will be constantly zero).
Since that player may choose the very strategy that does not guarantee the specification, if there is one, we call him
Demon
.
We shall see that such a construction links the rational verification problem to the constrained existence problem, as studied in the previous chapters.

Definition

36

(Product game)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
be a game, and let
\mathcal{M}
be a memory structure for Leader in
\mathcal{G}
.
Their
product game
is the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{M}=(\Pi\cup\{{\mathfrak{D}}\},V^{\times},(V^{\times}_{i})_{i},E^{\times},\mu^{\times})_{\upharpoonright(v_{0},q_{0})}
where the player
{\mathfrak{D}}
, called
Demon
, chooses how the memory structure
\mathcal{M}
will run.
Formally:

•

the vertex set is
V^{\times}=(V\times Q)\cup(V\times Q\times Q)
;

•

Leader controls the set
V^{\times}_{\mathfrak{L}}=\emptyset
, each player
i\neq{\mathfrak{L}},{\mathfrak{D}}
controls the set
V^{\times}_{i}=V_{i}\times Q\times Q
, and Demon controls the set
V^{\times}_{\mathfrak{D}}=(V\times Q)\cup(V_{\mathfrak{L}}\times Q\times Q)
;

•

the set
E^{\times}
contains:

–

the edge
(u,p)(u,p,q)
for each transition
(p,u,q)\in\Delta
(if
u\not\in V_{\mathfrak{L}}
), or
(p,u,q,v)\in\Delta
(if
u\in V_{\mathfrak{L}}
);

–

the edge
(u,p,q)(v,q)
for each transition
(p,u,q,v)\in\Delta
(if
u\in V_{\mathfrak{L}}
);

–

the edge
(u,p,q)(v,q)
for each transition
(p,u,q)\in\Delta
, and each edge
uv\in E
(if
u\not\in V_{\mathfrak{L}}
);

•

each payoff function
\mu^{\times}_{i}
maps every play
(\pi_{0},q_{0})(\pi_{0},q_{0},q_{1})(\pi_{1},q_{1})\dots
to the payoff
