\mu_{i}(\pi_{0}\pi_{1}\dots)
if
i\neq{\mathfrak{D}}
, and to the payoff
0
if
i={\mathfrak{D}}
.

b,q_{1}
a,q_{0},q_{1}

b,q_{1},q_{1}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{1}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{1}}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{1}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{1}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{1}}

:
A product game

Example

13
.

Figure
25
depicts the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{M}
, when
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
is the game of Figure
4
(
section

2.2
) and
\mathcal{M}
the memory structure of Figure
5
(
section

2.4
).
Leader is then assimilated to player
\Box
, and Demon’s vertices are represented by dark red boxes.
The unreachable vertices have been omitted, and we have given only the non-zero rewards.
Since, from the vertex
(a,q_{0},q_{1})
, player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
has always the possibility to go to the vertex
(b,q_{1})
and to get the payoff
1
, it can be shown that every NE and every SPE in that game gives player
\Box
the payoff
1
.
As we will see now, that means that the strategies induced by the memory structure
\mathcal{M}
guarantee the payoff
1
to player
\Box
against Nash-rational or subgame-perfect rational responses, i.e., that the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, the memory structure
\mathcal{M}
, and the quantity
1-\varepsilon
for every
\varepsilon>0
, form a positive instance of the Nash and subgame-perfect rational verification problems.

Theorem

20
.

Let
\rho\in\{\mathsf{Nash},\mathsf{SubgamePerfect}\}
.
Let
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
be a game, let
\mathcal{M}
be a memory structure for Leader in
\mathcal{G}
, and let
t\in\mathbb{Q}
.
Then, every
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed
\rho
-equilibrium
{\bar{\sigma}}
with
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathsf{Ind}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
satisfies
\mu_{\mathfrak{L}}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle>t
if and only if every
\rho
-equilibrium
{\bar{\tau}}
in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{M}
satisfies
\mu^{\times}_{\mathfrak{L}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle>t
.

Proof.

We present the proof when
\rho=\mathsf{SubgamePerfect}
; the proof for
\rho=\mathsf{Nash}
is analogous.

\blacktriangleright
If every
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed SPE
{\bar{\sigma}}
with
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathsf{Ind}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
satisfies
\mu_{\mathfrak{L}}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle>t
, then every SPE
{\bar{\tau}}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{M}
satisfies
\mu^{\times}_{\mathfrak{L}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle>t
.

Let
{\bar{\tau}}
be an SPE in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{M}
.
Let us define a strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
as follows: for every history
h=h_{0}\dots h_{n}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, let
g=(h_{0},q_{0})(h_{0},q_{0},q_{1})\dots(h_{n},q_{n-1},q_{n})
be the unique history of that form in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{M}
such that we have
(h_{k},q_{k},q_{k+1})=\tau_{\mathfrak{D}}((h_{0},q_{0})\dots(h_{k},q_{k}))
for each
k
, and let
(v,q_{n})={\bar{\tau}}(g)
.
Then, we define
{\bar{\sigma}}(h)=v
.

Since the only edges available for Demon in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{M}
are those that are induced by
\mathcal{M}
, we have
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathsf{Ind}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
.
Moreover, the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is a
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed SPE: let
h=h_{0}\dots h_{n}
be a history from
v_{0}
that is compatible with the strategy
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}
, let
i\in\Pi\setminus\{{\mathfrak{L}}\}
and let
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
be a deviation of
\sigma_{i}
.
We want to prove the inequality
\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright h},\sigma^{\prime}_{i\upharpoonright h}\rangle\leq\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h}\rangle
.
Then, let us define the history
g
as above, and let
\tau^{\prime}_{i}
be the strategy that simulates
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{M}
, i.e., that maps each history of the form
g(v_{1},q_{n})(v_{1},q_{n},q_{n+1})\dots(v_{k},q_{n+k-1},q_{n+k})
to the vertex
(\sigma^{\prime}_{i}(hv_{1}\dots v_{k}),q_{n+k})
.
Since the strategy profile
{\bar{\tau}}
is an SPE, we have:

\mu^{\times}_{i}(g_{<2n+1}\langle{\bar{\tau}}_{-i\upharpoonright g},\tau^{\prime}_{i\upharpoonright g}\rangle)\leq\mu^{\times}_{i}(g_{<2n+1}\langle{\bar{\tau}}_{\upharpoonright g}\rangle),

and therefore:

\mu_{i}(h_{<n}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright h},\sigma^{\prime}_{i\upharpoonright h}\rangle)\leq\mu_{i}(h_{<n}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h}\rangle).

Thus, the strategy
{\bar{\sigma}}
is a
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed SPE that satisfies
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathsf{Ind}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
.
By hypothesis, it comes that we have
\mu_{\mathfrak{L}}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle>t
, and therefore
\mu^{\times}_{\mathfrak{L}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle>t
.

\blacktriangleright
If every SPE
{\bar{\tau}}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{M}
satisfies
\mu^{\times}_{\mathfrak{L}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle>t
, then every
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed SPE with
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathsf{Ind}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
satisfies
\mu_{\mathfrak{L}}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle>t
.

Indeed, let
{\bar{\sigma}}
be a
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed SPE in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
with
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathsf{Ind}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
.
We write
h\mapstochar\rightarrow q_{h}
for the mapping establishing the fact that the strategy
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}
is induced by the memory structure
\mathcal{M}
, as defined in
6
.
Let us define a strategy profile
{\bar{\tau}}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{M}
as follows: for every history of the form
g=(h_{0},q_{0})(h_{0},q_{0},q_{1})\dots(h_{n},q_{n-1})
, we define
{\bar{\tau}}(g)=(h_{n},q_{n-1},q_{h_{0}\dots h_{n}})
.
For every history of the form
g=(h_{0},q_{0})\dots(h_{n},q_{n-1},q_{n})
, we define
{\bar{\tau}}(g)=({\bar{\sigma}}(h_{0}\dots h_{n}),q_{n})
.

Then, the strategy profile
{\bar{\tau}}
is an SPE: let
g=g_{0}\dots g_{m}
be a history in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{M}
, let
i
be a player and let
\tau^{\prime}_{i}
be a deviation of
\tau_{i}
.
If
i={\mathfrak{L}}
, then we have:

\mu^{\times}_{i}(g_{<m}\langle{\bar{\tau}}_{-i\upharpoonright g},\tau^{\prime}_{i\upharpoonright g}\rangle)\leq\mu^{\times}_{i}(g_{<m}\langle{\bar{\tau}}_{\upharpoonright g}\rangle),

because Leader does not control any vertex in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{M}
, hence actually
\tau^{\prime}_{i}=\tau_{i}
.
Likewise if
i={\mathfrak{D}}
, because Demon gets the payoff
0
in every play.
Now, if
i\neq{\mathfrak{D}},{\mathfrak{L}}
, let us consider without loss of generality that
g
has the form
g=(h_{0},q_{0})(h_{0},q_{0},q_{1})\dots(h_{n},q_{n})
(if the last vertex is controlled by Demon, it can be removed).
Let:

(\pi_{0},q_{n})(\pi_{0},q_{n},q_{n+1})(\pi_{1},q_{n+1})\dots=\langle{\bar{\tau}}_{-i\upharpoonright g},\tau^{\prime}_{i\upharpoonright g}\rangle.

The play
\pi=\pi_{0}\pi_{1}\dots
is compatible with the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-i\upharpoonright h}
.
Therefore, since the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is a
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed SPE, we have the inequality
\mu_{i}(h_{<n}\pi)\leq\mu_{i}(h_{<n}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright h}\rangle)
, i.e., the inequality:

\mu^{\times}_{i}(g_{<2n+1}\langle{\bar{\tau}}_{-i\upharpoonright g},\tau^{\prime}_{i\upharpoonright g}\rangle)\leq\mu^{\times}_{i}(g_{<2n+1}\langle{\bar{\tau}}_{\upharpoonright g}\rangle).

Thus, the strategy profile
{\bar{\tau}}
is an SPE. Then, by hypothesis, we have
\mu^{\times}_{\mathfrak{L}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle>t
, and therefore
\mu_{\mathfrak{L}}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle>t
.
∎

As we will show, this result entails that the
\rho
-rational verification problem in the class
\mathcal{C}
is computationally equivalent to the following problem.

Problem

4

(
\rho
-universal threshold problem in the class
\mathcal{C}
)
.

Given a game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\in\mathcal{C}
, a player
i\in\Pi
, and a threshold
t\in\mathbb{Q}
, is every
\rho
-equilibrium
{\bar{\sigma}}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
such that
\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle>t
?

Corollary

2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a game class among parity games, mean-payoff games, discounted-sum games, and energy games.
Then, in the class
\mathcal{C}
, for a given rationality concept
\rho\in\{\mathsf{Nash},\mathsf{SubgamePerfect}\}
, the
\rho
-universal threshold problem, the
\rho
-rational verification problem, and the deterministic
\rho
-rational verification problem are reducible to each other in polynomial time.

Proof.

\blacktriangleright
The deterministic
\rho
-rational verification problem reduces to the
\rho
-rational verification problem.

This result is true because a deterministic memory structure is a memory structure.

\blacktriangleright
The
\rho
-universal threshold problem reduces to the deterministic
\rho
-rational verification problem.

Let the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, the player
i
and the threshold
t
form an instance of the
\rho
-universal threshold problem.
We define the game
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
as equal to the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, where Leader has been added to the player set, but controls no vertex.
We define
\mu_{\mathfrak{L}}=\mu_{i}
.
If
\mathcal{G}
belongs to the class
\mathcal{C}
, so does
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.
Let
\mathcal{M}
be the one-state deterministic memory structure on
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
that never outputs anything.
Then, a strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
is a
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed
\rho
-equilibrium, if and only if it is a
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed
\rho
-equilibrium with
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathsf{Ind}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
, if and only if the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-{\mathfrak{L}}}
is a
\rho
-equilibrium in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
.
As a consequence the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, the player
i
, and the threshold
t
form a positive instance of the
\rho
-universal threshold problem, if and only if the game
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, the memory structure
\mathcal{M}
, and the threshold
t
form a positive instance of the deterministic
\rho
-rational verification problem.
Moreover, the latter can be constructed from the former in polynomial time.

\blacktriangleright
The
\rho
-rational verification problem reduces to the
\rho
-universal threshold problem.

This result is a consequence of Theorem
20
, since the product game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{M}
can be constructed from
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
and
\mathcal{M}
in polynomial time, and since for each of the four game classes considered, if the game
\mathcal{G}
belongs to the class
\mathcal{C}
, so does the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{M}
.
∎

It is now time to note that this problem is the complement of a subproblem of the constrained existence problem of
\rho
-equilibria in the class
\mathcal{C}
, where the threshold vectors
\bar{x}
and
\bar{y}
are such that
x_{j}=-\infty
for all
j
, and
y_{j}=+\infty
for all
j
except one.
This strong connection between the two problems explains that, in the literature,
rational verification
is sometimes the name given to what we call here the constrained existence problem (with possibly different types of constraints)—for a recent example, see
[
DBLP:journals/amai/GutierrezNPW23
]
.

Therefore, the complexity of Nash and subgame-perfect rational verification in parity, mean-payoff, discounted-sum or energy games can immediately be infered from
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
6
,
11
,
12
,
15
,
17
,
16
and
18
.

Theorem

21
.

The Nash rational verification problem is:

•

\mathsf{coNP}
-complete in parity games;

•

\mathsf{coNP}
-complete in mean-payoff games;

•

coTDS
-hard and recursively enumerable in discounted-sum games;

•

undecidable and co-recursively enumerable in energy games.

The subgame-perfect rational verification problem is:

•

\mathsf{coNP}
-complete and fixed-parameter tractable (with the number of players and the number of colors as parameters) in parity games;

•

\mathsf{coNP}
-complete in mean-payoff games;

•

coTDS
-hard and recursively enumerable in discounted-sum games;

•

undecidable, and in particular not recursively enumerable, in energy games.

8.4  A potential limit: the temptation of chaos

It is now worth noting that the definition we gave of rational verification entails, in the case of mean-payoff games, results that may be considered as counter-intuitive.

Example

14
.

Consider the mean-payoff game depicted by Figure
26
, where Leader owns no vertex, and consider the only (vacuous) strategy available for Leader.
Does that strategy guarantee a payoff greater than
1
?
Intuitively, it does not, since Leader always receives the payoff
0
.
But still, that strategy, that game, and that threshold form a positive instance of the subgame-perfect rational verification problem, because no
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed SPE exists in that game (see the proof of
13
).

More generally, the definition we give of rational verification considers that a
good
strategy for Leader is a strategy such that for every response of the environment that is rational, the generated outcome observes some specification.
But a strategy is then good, in that sense, if
no
rational response of the environment exists: that is the phenomenon that we can call
temptation of chaos
.
While that case does never occur in all the other settings we have been studying, because rational responses are always guaranteed to exist (as we will see below), it must be considered specifically for subgame-perfect rational verification in mean-payoff games.

a
c
b
d
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{L}}}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{3}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{L}}}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{3}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{L}}}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{2}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{L}}}}{{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{1}}

Figure 26
:
The temptation of chaos: an illustration

8.5  Achaotic rational verification

8.5.1 Definitions

To avoid such phenomena, we generalize the approach used in a two-player context in
[
filiot_et_al:LIPIcs:2020:12534
]
, where
best responses
, when they are not guaranteed to exist, are replaced by
\varepsilon
-best responses
—i.e. best responses up to
\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon\geq 0
is as small as possible.

We therefore introduce an alternative definition of rational verification,
achaotic rational verification
: a good strategy for Leader is a strategy that guarantees the given threshold against every response that is
as rational as possible
.
A quantitative relaxation of our rationality concepts is therefore necessary: one is offered by the notions of
\varepsilon
-NEs and
\varepsilon
-SPEs, already defined in
chapter

4
.
For convenience, we write
\varepsilon\mathsf{SPE}_{\mathfrak{L}}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
for the set of
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed
\varepsilon
-SPEs in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
.
Later, we will also write
\varepsilon\mathsf{SPE}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
for the set of
\varepsilon
-SPEs in the same game.
Finally, given a strategy
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}
in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, we denote by
\varepsilon\mathsf{SPR}(\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}})
the set of
\varepsilon
-best responses to
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}
, i.e., the set of strategy profiles
{\bar{\sigma}}_{-{\mathfrak{L}}}
such that the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is a
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed
\varepsilon
-SPE.

Problem

5

(Achaotic (deterministic) subgame-perfect rational verification in the class
\mathcal{C}
)
.

Given a game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\in\mathcal{C}
, a threshold
t\in\mathbb{Q}
, and a memory structure (resp. a deterministic memory structure)
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathcal{G}
, do we have:

\infop_{\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathsf{Ind}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}(\mathcal{M})}~\supop_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon\geq 0\\
\mathsf{SPR}(\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}})\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}~\infop_{{\bar{\sigma}}_{-{\mathfrak{L}}}\in\varepsilon\mathsf{SPR}(\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}})}~\mu_{\mathfrak{L}}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle>t?

In contrast with classical rational verification, the supremum and the infimum above are taken on sets that are never empty: if one considers
\varepsilon
large enough (for example
\varepsilon=\maxop_{i\in\Pi}\maxop_{uv,u^{\prime}v^{\prime}\in E}|r_{i}(uv)-r_{i}(u^{\prime}v^{\prime})|
in a mean-payoff game), there always exists an
\varepsilon
-SPE in every game with a bounded set of payoff vectors, which is the case of all the game classes we considered so far.

The definition of
achaotic (deterministic) Nash rational verification
is analogous.

Example

15
.

Let us consider again the example depicted by Figure
26
.
In that game, there exist (
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed)
\varepsilon
-SPEs for every
\varepsilon\geq 1
(for example, the stationary strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
defined by
\sigma(a)=c
and
\sigma(b)=d
), but not for
\varepsilon<1
(for the same reasons as there is no SPEs).
Achaotic subgame-perfect rational verification amounts then to wondering whether all
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed
1
-SPEs
{\bar{\sigma}}
satisfy the inequality
\mu_{\mathfrak{L}}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle>t
, which is the case if and only if we have
t<0
.

In the general case, a proof analogous to that of
20
and
2
can show that for each
\rho\in\{\mathsf{Nash},\mathsf{SubgamePerfect}\}
and every game class
\mathcal{C}
among parity, mean-payoff, discounted-sum and energy games, the achaotic (deterministic or not)
\rho
-rational verification problem reduces in polynomial time to the following problem, and that a reverse reduction in polynomial time also exists.

Problem

6

(Achaotic
\rho
-universal threshold problem in the class
\mathcal{C}
)
.

Given a game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\in\mathcal{C}
, a player
i\in\Pi
, and a threshold
t\in\mathbb{Q}
, do we have:

\supop_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon\geq 0\\
\varepsilon\mathsf{SPE}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}~\infop_{{\bar{\sigma}}\in\varepsilon\mathsf{SPE}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}}~\mu_{\mathfrak{L}}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle>t?

8.5.2 Coincidence with classical rational verification

Among the problems we study here, this new definition is relevant in only one case: subgame-perfect rational verification in mean-payoff games.
In all other cases, the rational verification problems are equivalent to their achaotic versions, because Nash and subgame-perfect responses are guaranteed to exist.

Theorem

22
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of games, among the classes of parity, energy games and discounted-sum games.
Let
\rho\in\{\mathsf{Nash},\mathsf{SubgamePerfect}\}
.
Then, the positive instances of the achaotic
\rho
-universal threshold problem in
\mathcal{C}
are exactly the positive instances of the
\rho
-universal threshold problem in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

\blacktriangleright
Parity, energy, and discounted-sum games

The result is a consequence of the fact that in those three classes, SPEs are always guaranteed to exist—and therefore so are NEs.
Indeed, it has been shown in
[
GU08
]
that every game with Borel Boolean winning condition contains an SPE.
This covers parity, but also energy games.
Thus, if
\mathcal{C}
is the class of parity or of energy games, then the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{C}
contains an SPE.

If
\mathcal{C}
is the class of discounted-sum games, then the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, and therefore the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{M}
, is a game with payoff functions that are continuous for the canonical distance on infinite words.
By
[
DBLP:conf/csl/BrihayeBMR15
]
, every such game contains an SPE.

\blacktriangleright
Nash-universal threshold problem in mean-payoff games

In this second case, SPEs may not exist in mean-payoff games, but NEs still always do—see for example
[
DBLP:conf/lfcs/BrihayePS13
]
.
∎

8.5.3 The complexity of achaotic rational verification in mean-payoff games

We now focus on the complexity of the achaotic subgame-perfect-universal threshold problem in mean-payoff games, knowing that it will also give us the complexity of the achaotic subgame-perfect rational verification problem.

An optimal algorithm for this problem requires the following lemma: in each game, there exists a least
\varepsilon
such that
\varepsilon
-SPEs exist.
Moreover, that quantity can be written with a polynomially bounded number of bits.

Lemma

23
.

There exists a polynomial
P_{1}
such that in every mean-payoff game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, there exists
\varepsilon_{\minop}
with
\lVert\varepsilon_{\minop}\rVert\leq P_{1}(\lVert\mathcal{G}\rVert)
such that
\varepsilon_{\minop}
-SPEs exist in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, and
\varepsilon
-SPEs, for every
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{\minop}
, do not.

Proof.

This proof will rely on some new technical considerations on the negotiation function in mean-payoff games.

\blacktriangleright
A preliminary result

We will need to prove that the negotiation function is
continuous from below
.
Let us first recall a definition of that notion.

Definition

37

(Continuity from below)
.

A mapping
f:\mathbb{R}^{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{D}
is
continuous from below
if for every non-decreasing sequence
(\bar{x}_{n})
, we have
f(\supop_{n}\bar{x}_{n})=\supop_{n}f(\bar{x}_{n})
.

Sublemma

3
.

In mean-payoff games, the negotiation function is continuous from below

Proof.

Let
(\lambda_{n})_{n}
be a non-decreasing sequence of requirements on a mean-payoff game
\mathcal{G}
, and let
\lambda=\supop_{n}\lambda_{n}
. We want to prove the equality
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)=\supop_{n}\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{n})
.
Since the negotiation function is monotone, we already have
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)\geq\supop_{n}\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{n})
. Let us prove that
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)\leq\supop_{n}\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{n})
.
Let
\delta>0
: we want to find
n
such that
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{n})(v)\geq\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v)-\delta
for each
v\in V
.

We will use for that purpose, for a given player
i
and a given vertex
v\in V_{i}
, the concrete negotiation games:

\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v^{\mathsf{c}}}=\left(\{\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{C}\},V^{\mathsf{c}},(V^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{P}},V^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}),E^{\mathsf{c}},\mu^{\mathsf{c}}\right)_{\upharpoonright v^{\mathsf{c}}}

and:

\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda_{n}i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v^{\mathsf{c}}}=\left(\{\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{C}\},V^{\mathsf{c}},(V^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{P}},V^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathfrak{C}}),E^{\mathsf{c}},\mu^{n}\right)_{\upharpoonright v^{\mathsf{c}}}

for some requirement
\lambda_{n}
.
Let us note that both games have the same underlying graph, and that the only difference is between the payoff functions
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}
and
\mu^{n}
.

Let us define:

\gamma_{n}=\supop_{w\in V}(\lambda(w)-\lambda_{n}(w)).

Then, the sequence
(\gamma_{n})_{n}
is non-increasing and converges to
0
.
Let
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
be a stationary strategy for Challenger in the game
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright s_{0}}
; it can also be considered as a stationary strategy in the game
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda_{n}i}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright s_{0}}
.
Now, let
K
be a strongly connected component of the underlying graph of the game
\mathsf{conc}_{\lambda i}(\mathcal{G})[\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}]
, accessible from the vertex
v^{\mathsf{c}}_{0}
.
Against the strategy
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
, Prover may go to the strongly connected component
K
, and give to Challenger the payoff that he gets in any play in that strongly connected component.

If the strongly connected component
K
contains some deviation.

Then, the least payoff that Challenger can obtain in
K
is the same when the payoff function is
\mu^{\mathsf{c}}
and when it is
\mu^{n}
: it corresponds to the least payoff of the form
\mu_{i}(\dot{c}^{\omega})
where
c
is a simple cycle of
K
.

If the strongly connected component
K
contains no deviation.

Then, the vertices of
K
share a common memory
M
.
Let
R=\{\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Pi}~|~\forall i\in\Pi,\forall v\in M\cap V_{i},x_{i}\geq\lambda(v)\}
, and similarly, let
R_{n}=\{\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Pi}~|~\forall i\in\Pi,\forall v\in M\cap V_{i},x_{i}\geq\lambda_{n}(v)\}
.

If we have
(\mathsf{Conv}_{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(K)}\mu(\dot{c}^{\omega}))\cap R=\emptyset
, since the sets
\mathsf{Conv}_{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(K)}\mu(\dot{c}^{\omega})
and
R
are closed, then by taking the quantity
\gamma_{n}
small enough, we also have
(\mathsf{Conv}_{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(K)}\mu(\dot{c}^{\omega}))\cap R_{n}=\emptyset
.
In this case, whatever Prover does in the strongly connected component
K
, Challenger will always get the payoff
+\infty
.

If that is not the case, the polytope
(\mathsf{Conv}_{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(K)}\mu(\dot{c}^{\omega}))\cap R
is included in the polytope
(\mathsf{Conv}_{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(K)}\mu(\dot{c}^{\omega}))\cap R_{n}
, and the difference between the infima of those polytopes’ projections on the dimension
i
can be bounded by taking the biggest slope of those polytopes’ edges, which are in finite number.
Formally, we have:

\displaystyle\infop\left\{x_{i}~\left|~\bar{x}\in\left(\underset{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(K)}{\mathsf{Conv}}\mu(\dot{c}^{\omega})\right)\cap R_{n}\right.\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\infop\left\{x_{i}~\left|~\bar{x}\in\left(\underset{c\in\mathsf{SCyc}(K)}{\mathsf{Conv}}\mu(\dot{c}^{\omega})\right)\cap R\right.\right\}-\gamma_{n}\maxop_{c,d\in\mathsf{SCyc}(K)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\Pi,\\
\mu_{j}(\dot{c}^{\omega})>\mu_{j}(\dot{d}^{\omega})\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu_{i}(\dot{c}^{\omega})-\mu_{i}(\dot{d}^{\omega})}{\mu_{j}(\dot{c}^{\omega})-\mu_{j}(\dot{d}^{\omega})}

and if
\gamma_{n}
is small enough, the right-hand subtrahend can be made smaller than
\delta
.

Conclusion.

In both cases, we find that there exists
\gamma_{n}
small enough, i.e.
n
large enough, to ensure that the least payoff that Prover can give to Challenger in
K
varies by less than the quantity
\delta
when the requirement varies between
\lambda
and
\lambda_{n}
.

We can find such
n
for each strongly connected component
K
, and there exists a finite number of such components: we can therefore find some
n
that satisfies the same property for all
K
.
Similarly, we can find some
n
that also ensures it for all
K
and for all stationary strategies
\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}
.
Since by
14
stationary strategies are optimal in the concrete negotiation game, we infer that there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that:

\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{n})(v)\geq\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v)-\delta.

Finally, since there are finitely many states
v\in V
, we can conclude to the existence of
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that for each
v\in V
, we have:

\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{n})(v)\geq\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v)-\delta,

which concludes the proof.
∎

\blacktriangleright
Existence of
\varepsilon_{\minop}

Let us recall that, by
16
, for every
\varepsilon\geq 0
, the negotiation function has a least
\varepsilon
-fixed point, which we will write
\lambda_{\varepsilon}
.
Let us also recall that by
8
, a play in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
is an
\varepsilon
-SPE outcome if and only if it is
\lambda_{\varepsilon}
-consistent.

Let us define:

\varepsilon=\infop\{\delta\geq 0~|~\delta\mathsf{SPE}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\neq\emptyset\}.

We only need to show that
\varepsilon
is also such that
\varepsilon
-SPEs exist, or in other words, that
\lambda_{\varepsilon}(v_{0})\neq+\infty
.
Let us note that for every
\delta,\delta^{\prime}
with
\delta\leq\delta^{\prime}
, the requirement
\lambda_{\delta}
is itself a
\delta^{\prime}
-fixed point of the negotiation function, and therefore satisfies
\lambda_{\delta}\geq\lambda_{\delta^{\prime}}
.

Let us now define the requirement
\lambda:v\mapstochar\rightarrow\supop_{\delta>\varepsilon}\lambda_{\delta}(v)
.
For each
v
and every
\delta>\varepsilon
, we have:

\mathsf{nego}(\lambda_{\delta})(v)\leq\lambda_{\delta}(v)-\delta.

Using
3
, we obtain:

\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v)\leq\lambda(v)-\varepsilon,

i.e., the requirement
\lambda
is an
\varepsilon
-fixed point of the negotiation function, and therefore
\lambda=\lambda_{\varepsilon}
.
Since
\lambda(v_{0})=\supop_{\delta}\lambda_{\delta}(v_{0})\neq+\infty
, we obtain that
\varepsilon
-SPEs exist in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, and therefore that
\varepsilon_{\minop}=\varepsilon
exists.

\blacktriangleright
Size

Now, let us recall that we showed in the proof of
16
that one can define, for every
\lambda
, a finite union of polyhedra
X_{\lambda}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{V\times\Pi}
, where each of those polyhedra is defined by a set of inequations either of the form
x_{vi}\geq\lambda(w)
or of size bounded by a polynomial function of
\lVert\mathcal{G}\rVert
, and such that for each
i\in\Pi
and
v\in V_{i}
, we have
\mathsf{nego}(\lambda)(v)=\minop\left\{x_{vi}~\left|~\bar{\bar{x}}\in X_{\lambda}\right.\right\}
.

Let us now define the set:

Y=\left\{\left(\lambda,\bar{\bar{x}}\right)~\left|~\bar{\bar{x}}\in X_{\lambda}\right.\right\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{V}\times\mathbb{R}^{V\times\Pi}.

This set is itself a finite union of polyhedra defined by the same inequations than
X_{\lambda}
, or by inequations of bounded size (those of the form
x_{vi}\geq\lambda(w)
, where
\lambda(w)
is no longer a constant but a coordinate of
\left(\lambda,\bar{\bar{x}}\right)
).
Let us also define the mapping:

f:\left\{\begin{matrix}Y&\rightarrow&\mathbb{R}\\
\left(\lambda,\bar{\bar{x}}\right)&\mapstochar\rightarrow&\underset{i\in\Pi,v\in V_{i}}{\maxop}\left(x_{vi}-\lambda(v)\right)\end{matrix}\right..

Then, we have:

\varepsilon_{\minop}=\minop_{\left(\lambda,\bar{\bar{x}}\right)}f\left(\lambda,\bar{\bar{x}}\right),

and since
f
is a piecewise linear mapping on a finite union of polyhedra that has a minimum, that minimum is reached on a vertex
\left(\lambda,\bar{\bar{x}}\right)
of one of those polyhedra.
By
1
, such a vertex has coordinates bounded by a polynomial of the maximal size of the inequations defining the polyhedron to which it belongs, i.e., by a polynomial of
\lVert\mathcal{G}\rVert
.
Therefore, that is also the case of
f(\lambda,\bar{\bar{x}})=\varepsilon_{\minop}
.
∎

We are now equipped to prove the following theorem.

Theorem

23
.

In the class of mean-payoff games, the achaotic subgame-perfect-universal threshold problem, deterministic or not, is
\mathsf{P}^{\mathsf{NP}}
-complete.

Proof.

\blacktriangleright
Easiness

By
15
(taking
\bar{x}=(-\infty)_{i\in\Pi}
and
\bar{y}=(+\infty)_{i\in\Pi}
), there is an
\mathsf{NP}
algorithm that decides, given
\varepsilon
and
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, whether there exists an
\varepsilon
-SPE in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, i.e. whether
\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon_{\minop}
.
Using Lemma
23
, a dichotomous search can therefore compute
\varepsilon_{\minop}
by a polynomial number of calls to that algorithm.
Then, one last call to that same algorithm can decide whether there exists an
\varepsilon_{\minop}
-SPE
{\bar{\sigma}}
such that
\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle\leq t
.

\blacktriangleright
Hardness

?x_{1}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{A}}}}{{0}}~\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{B}}}}{{3}}~\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{W}}}}{{1}}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\neg x_{i}}}{{2m}}~\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C}}{{m}}~\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{A}}}}{{2}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x_{i}}}{{2m}}~\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C}}{{m}}~\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{A}}}}{{2-\frac{m}{2^{i+1}}}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\neg x_{i}}}{{2m}}~\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C}}{{m}}~\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{A}}}}{{2}}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\neg x_{n}}}{{2m}}~\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C}}{{m}}~\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{A}}}}{{2}}~\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{W}}}}{{1}}

Figure 27
:
The game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright a}

We proceed by reducing, to the complement of our problem, the following
\mathsf{P}^{\mathsf{NP}}
-complete problem.

Problem

7

(Lexicographic minimum problem)
.

Given a Boolean formula
\varphi
in conjunctive normal form over the ordered variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
, is the lexicographically first valuation
\nu_{\minop}
satisfying
\varphi
such that
\nu_{\minop}(x_{n})=1
? (and in particular, does such a valuation exist?)

Construction.

Let us write
\varphi=\bigwedge_{j=1}^{p}C_{j}
.
We construct a game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright a}
, with a player called
Witness
and written
{\mathfrak{W}}
, in which there exists an
\varepsilon_{\minop}
-SPE
{\bar{\sigma}}
such that
\mu_{\mathfrak{W}}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle\leq 0
if and only if
\varphi
is satisfiable and is such that
\nu_{\minop}(x_{n})=1
.
That game, depicted in Figure
27
, has
2n+p+4
players: the literal players
x_{1},\neg x_{1},\dots,x_{n},\neg x_{n}
; the clause players
C_{1},\dots,C_{p}
; the player
Solver
, written
\mathfrak{S}
; the player
Witness
, written
{\mathfrak{W}}
; the player
Adélaïde
, written
{\mathfrak{A}}
; and the player
Barthélémy
, written
{\mathfrak{B}}
.
It contains
3n+p+4
vertices:

–

the initial vertex
v_{0}=a
, controlled by Adélaïde;

–

two vertices
b
and
c
, controlled by Barthélémy;

–

for each variable
x_{i}
, a vertex
?x_{i}\in V_{\mathfrak{S}}
, a vertex
x_{i}\in V_{x_{i}}
, and a vertex
\neg x_{i}\in V_{\neg x_{i}}
;

–

for each clause
C_{j}
, a vertex
C_{j}\in V_{C_{j}}
;

–

a sink vertex
\triangledown
(drawn three times in Figure
27
for convenience).

Those vertices are connected by the following edges (unmentioned rewards are equal to
0
, and we write
m=2n+p
):

–

from the vertex
a
to the vertex
b
and from the vertex
b
to the vertex
a
, two edges that give Adélaïde the reward
0
, Barthélémy the reward
3
, and Witness the reward
1
;

–

from the vertex
a
to the vertex
?x_{1}
and from
b
to
c
, an edge;

–

from the vertex
c
to itself, an edge giving both Adélaïde and Barthélémy the reward
2
, and giving Witness the reward
1
;

–

from each vertex
?x_{i}
to the vertex
\neg x_{i}
and from each vertex
\neg x_{i}
to the vertex
?x_{i+1}
(or to the vertex
C_{1}
if
i=n
), an edge giving:

*

the reward
2m
to player
\neg x_{i}
,

*

the reward
m
to every player
C_{j}
such that the clause
C_{j}
contains the literal
\neg x_{i}
,

*

the reward
2
to Adélaïde;

*

and if
i=n
, the reward
1
to Witness;

–

from each vertex
?x_{i}
to the vertex
x_{i}
and from each vertex
x_{i}
to the vertex
?x_{i+1}
(or to the vertex
C_{1}
if
i=n
), an edge giving:

*

the reward
2m
to player
x_{i}
,

*

the reward
m
to every player
C_{j}
such that the clause
C_{j}
contains the literal
x_{i}
,

*

and the reward
2-\frac{m}{2^{i+1}}
to Adélaïde;

–

from each vertex
C_{j}
to the vertex
C_{j+1}
(or the vertex
?x_{1}
if
j=p
), an edge giving the reward
2
to Adélaïde;

–

from the sink vertex
\triangledown
to itself, an edge giving the reward
1
to Adélaïde, the reward
2
to each clause player, the reward
4
to each literal player, and the reward
1
to Witness.

We will show that in this game, when
\nu_{\minop}
exists, it defines a binary encoding for the quantity
\varepsilon_{\minop}
.
To do so, let us present a more general correspondence between
\varepsilon
-SPEs and valuations satisfying
\varphi
.

The strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\nu}
.

Let
\nu
be a valuation satisfying
\varphi
.
We define the stationary strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\nu}
as follows: from the vertex
a
, Adélaïde always goes to
{?x_{1}}
; from the vertex
b
, Barthélémy always goes to
c
; from each vertex
{?x_{i}}
, Solver always goes to
x_{i}
if
\nu(x_{i})=1
, and to
\neg x_{i}
otherwise; from their vertices, the clause players and the literal players that are satisfied by
\nu
do never go to the vertex
\triangledown
; and the literal players that are not satisfied by
\nu
do whenever they have the opportunity.

Sublemma

4
.

The strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\nu}
is an
\varepsilon
-SPE, where
\varepsilon=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\nu(x_{i})}{2^{i}}
, and is not a
\delta
-SPE for any
\delta<\varepsilon
.

Proof.

Solver’s payoff is constant, and Witness does not control any vertex, hence they have no profitable deviation.
In every subgame where they have actions to choose, all the clause players get at least the payoff
2
, since at least one of their literals is satisfied (and therefore the corresponding vertex is visited at each turn).
Similarly, from the vertex he controls, each literal player gets the payoff
4
, either because he is satisfied by
\nu
, or by going to the vertex
\triangledown
; they have therefore no profitable deviation.
As for Barthélémy, in every subgame where he has an action to choose, he gets the payoff
2
, and cannot get a better one, since Adélaïde always plans to go to
?x_{1}
.
Let us finally focus on Adélaïde: from the vertex
a
, the only one that she controls, she could get the payoff
2
by going to
b
.
By going to the vertex
?x_{1}
, she gets the payoff:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2n+p}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\left(2-(2n+p)\frac{\nu(x_{i})}{2^{i+1}}\right)+2p\right)

\displaystyle=2-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\nu(x_{i})}{2^{i}}=2-\varepsilon,

hence
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\nu}
is an
\varepsilon
-SPE, and is not a
\delta
-SPE for any
\delta<\varepsilon
.
∎

The valuation
\nu_{{\bar{\sigma}}}
.

Let
{\bar{\sigma}}
be a
1
-SPE such that the play
\pi=\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
traverses the vertex
?x_{1}
, but does never reach the vertex
\triangledown
.
We define the valuation
\nu_{{\bar{\sigma}}}
by
\nu_{{\bar{\sigma}}}(x_{i})=1
if and only if
\pi
traverses the vertex
x_{i}
.

Let us note that for every vertex
x_{i}
that is traversed by
\pi
, the player
x_{i}
can deviate and go to the vertex
\triangledown
, where he can get the payoff
4
.
Since
{\bar{\sigma}}
is a
1
-SPE, we have therefore
\mu_{x_{i}}(\pi)\geq 3
, and therefore
\mu_{\neg x_{i}}(\pi)\leq 1
.
As a consequence, the vertices
x_{i}
and
\neg x_{i}
cannot both be traversed; and if the vertex
\neg x_{i}
is traversed, then we have
\nu_{{\bar{\sigma}}}(x_{i})=0
.

Sublemma

5
.

The valuation
\nu_{{\bar{\sigma}}}
satisfies
\varphi
, and we have:

\mu_{\mathfrak{A}}(\pi)=2-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\nu_{{\bar{\sigma}}}(x_{i})}{2^{i}}.

Proof.

Let us consider a clause
C_{j}
of
\varphi
.
Since the vertex
C_{j}
is visited (infinitely often), there is a deviation available for player
C_{j}
that grants her the payoff
2
.
Since
{\bar{\sigma}}
is a
1
-SPE, we have
\mu_{C_{j}}(\pi)\geq 1
.
Therefore, there is at least one literal
\ell
of
C_{j}
such that the vertex
\ell
is visited by
\pi
, i.e. such that
\nu_{{\bar{\sigma}}}
satisfies
\ell
.
Consequently, the valuation
\nu_{{\bar{\sigma}}}
satisfies
C_{j}
, and therefore
\varphi
.

Moreover, since the play
\pi
does never traverse both
x_{i}
and
\neg x_{i}
for any
i
, it grants Adélaïde the payoff:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2n+p}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\left(2-(2n+p)\frac{\nu_{{\bar{\sigma}}}(x_{i})}{2^{i+1}}\right)+2p\right)

\displaystyle=2-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\nu_{{\bar{\sigma}}}(x_{i})}{2^{i}}.

∎

Consequence.

We can then prove the following proposition.

Proposition

1
.

if
\nu_{\minop}
is the least valuation satisfying
\varphi
(and in particular if such a valuation exists), then we have
\varepsilon_{\minop}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\nu_{\minop}(x_{i})}{2^{i}}
.

Proof.

Let
\delta=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\nu_{\minop}(x_{i})}{2^{i}}<1
.

By
4
, the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\nu_{\minop}}
is a
\delta
-SPE.
Let us prove that there is no
\varepsilon
-SPE with
\varepsilon<\delta
.
Let
\varepsilon<1
be such that there exists an
\varepsilon
-SPE
{\bar{\sigma}}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright a}
, and let us prove that
\varepsilon\geq\delta
.

If
{\bar{\sigma}}
is an
\varepsilon
-SPE with
\varepsilon<1
, then necessarily, there exist infinitely many integers
k
such that
\sigma_{\mathfrak{A}}((ab)^{k}a)={?x_{1}}
, because otherwise each play
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright(ab)^{k}a}\rangle
would either end in the vertex
c
(and then Barthélémy would have a deviation profitable by more than
1
by refusing to go to
c
), or would be equal to
(ab)^{\omega}
(and then Adélaïde would have a deviation profitable by more than
1
by going to
?x_{1}
).
Then, for each such
k\neq 0
, we have
\sigma_{\mathfrak{B}}((ab)^{k})=c
(if Barthélémy does not go to
c
, then he gets only the payoff
1
, while by going to
c
, he gets the payoff
2
).
Therefore, if we choose some
k
such that
\sigma_{\mathfrak{A}}((ab)^{k}a)={?x_{1}}
, we also have
\mu_{\mathfrak{A}}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright(ab)^{k}ab}\rangle=2
.
Since
{\bar{\sigma}}
is an
\varepsilon
-SPE with
\varepsilon<1
, we have therefore
\mu_{\mathfrak{A}}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright(ab)^{k}a}\rangle>1
, which means that the play
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright(ab)^{k}a}\rangle
does never reach the vertex
\triangledown
.
Then, the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright(ab)^{k}a}
is a
1
-SPE such that the play
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright(ab)^{k}a}\rangle
traverses the vertex
?x_{1}
, but does never reach the vertex
\triangledown
: the valuation
\nu_{{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright(ab)^{k}a}}
is defined, and by
5
, it satisfies the formula
\varphi
, and is such that:

\mu_{\mathfrak{A}}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright(ab)^{k}a}\rangle=2-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\nu_{{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright(ab)^{k}a}}(x_{i})}{2^{i}}.

By going to the vertex
b
instead of
?x_{1}
, Adélaïde has therefore a deviation that is profitable by
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\nu_{{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright(ab)^{k}a}}(x_{i})}{2^{i}}
.
Moreover, since the valuation
\nu_{{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright(ab)^{k}a}}
satisfies
\varphi
, it is lexicographically greater than or equal to
\nu_{\minop}
, hence the inequality:

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\nu_{{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright(ab)^{k}a}}(x_{i})}{2^{i}}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\nu_{\minop}(x_{i})}{2^{i}}=\delta,

and therefore
\varepsilon\geq\delta
, as desired.
∎

Conclusion.

We can now conclude that there exists an
\varepsilon_{\minop}
-SPE
{\bar{\sigma}}
in this game such that
\mu_{\mathfrak{W}}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle\leq 0
if and only if
\varphi
is satisfiable and is such that
\nu_{\minop}(x_{n})=1
.

–

If there exists such an
\varepsilon_{\minop}
-SPE, then necessarily the play
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
visits infinitely often the vertex
x_{n}
(every other play gives Witness a positive payoff).
Therefore, the valuation
\nu_{\minop}=\nu_{{\bar{\sigma}}}
is such that
\nu_{\minop}(x_{n})=1
.

–

Conversely, if
\nu_{\minop}(x_{n})=1
, then the
\varepsilon_{\minop}
-SPE
{\bar{\sigma}}_{\nu_{\minop}}
traverses the vertex
?x_{1}
and does never traverse the vertex
\neg x_{n}
, nor reach the vertex
\triangledown
; it grants therefore Witness the payoff
0
.  ∎

8.6  Rational synthesis

Let us close this chapter with some considerations on the natural continuation of rational verification, where the strategy
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}
is not given:
rational synthesis
, a concept due to Giuseppe Perelli, Orna Kupferman, and Moshe Vardi
[
DBLP:journals/amai/KupfermanPV16
]
.
Such a problem can be defined as follows, for a given game class
\mathcal{C}
and rationality concept
\rho
.

Problem

8

(
\rho
-rational synthesis problem in the class
\mathcal{C}
)
.

Given a game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}\in\mathcal{C}
and a threshold
t\in\mathbb{Q}
, does there exist a strategy
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}
such that every
{\mathfrak{L}}
-fixed
\rho
-equilibrium
{\bar{\tau}}
with
\tau_{\mathfrak{L}}=\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}
satisfies the inequality
\mu_{\mathfrak{L}}\langle{\bar{\tau}}\rangle>t
?

Another common presentation of this problem in the literature (used, for example, in
[
filiot_et_al:LIPIcs:2020:12534
]
) is in terms of the computation of the
Stackelberg value
.
Given a game
\mathcal{G}_{|v_{0}}
, the Stackelberg value can be defined as the supremum of all thresholds
t
for which the pair
(\mathcal{G}_{|v_{0}},t)
is a positive instance of the decision problem described above.

Variants of this problem can be obtained by restricting Leader’s strategies to finite-memory ones (which makes sense if Leader’s strategy is an abstraction for a program), or by considering its achaotic version.
Both variants might be relevant given the fact that if
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}
can use infinite memory, then the temptation of chaos can now be observed in all classes of quantitative games where payoff functions can have infinite range.
The game depicted by
Figure

28
, where violet vertices belong to Leader, illustrates that fact.
If Leader is allowed to use infinite memory, then she can design her strategy so that player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
has an incentive to eventually go to the vertex
b
, but
as late as possible
.
That will be the case for example with the strategy
\sigma_{\mathfrak{L}}
defined by:

\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{\upharpoonright a^{k}b}\rangle=\left(b^{k}c\right)^{\omega},

for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
and independently of
\sigma_{\circ}
.
Then, every strategy of player
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.46387pt}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\circ$}}}
admits a profitable deviation, by postponing the moment where she takes the edge
ab
.
By contrast, let us recall that we showed in the proof of
22
that in discounted-sum games, there is always a subgame-perfect, and
a fortiori
a Nash response to every finite-memory strategy.

a
b
c
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{1}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{2}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{0}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{0}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{2}}

Figure 28
:
The temptation of chaos in a discounted-sum game

This document does not bring any significant result about rational verification.
Let us simply mention that the case of parity games, Nash rational synthesis has been proved to be
\mathsf{EXPTIME}
-easy and
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-hard, and
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-easy,
\mathsf{NP}
-hard, and
\mathsf{coNP}
-hard when the number of players is fixed
[
DBLP:conf/icalp/ConduracheFGR16
]
.
As for subgame-perfect rational synthesis, it has recently been proved to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard and
\mathsf{coNP}
-hard,
2\mathsf{EXPTIME}
-easy in general, and
\mathsf{EXPTIME}
-easy when the number of players is fixed
[
DBLP:journals/corr/abs-2412-08547
]
.

It is also worth noting here that we have the following general result:

Lemma

24
.

For each
\rho\in\{\mathsf{SubgamePerfect},\mathsf{Nash}\}
and every game class
\mathcal{C}
among parity games, mean-payoff games, discounted-sum games and energy games, the
\rho
-universal threshold problem in the class
\mathcal{C}
reduces in polynomial time to the
\rho
-rational synthesis problem in
\mathcal{C}
.

The reduction consists simply in adding a player Leader who does not control any vertex.
Consequently, using
21
, Nash and subgame-perfect rational synthesis are at least as hard as the target discounted-sum problem in discounted-sum games, and undecidable in energy games.

Finally, to our knowledge, little is known about rational synthesis in mean-payoff games with arbitrary many players in the environment (the case where the environment is made of one player has been studied in
[
filiot_et_al:LIPIcs:2020:12534
]
).
When Leader is restricted to finite-memory strategies,
2
and
15
entail recursive enumerability.
Similarly, one could obtain an analogous result for achaotic rational synthesis by proving that finite-memory strategies are optimal for Leader, which seems to be a reasonable conjecture (while it is clearly not true for classical rational verification, as examplified by the game depicted by
Figure

28
).
But all those problems might still be undecidable.

Open Problem

4
.

Are (achaotic) Nash and subgame-perfect rational synthesis decidable in mean-payoff games?
Are they decidable when Leader is restricted to finite-memory strategies?

PART IV:

OTHER EQUILIBRIA

Chapter 9:  Strong secure equilibria and their applications

In the previous sections, our examples have primarily dealt with systems interacting with an unpredictable or partially predictable environment.
As we have argued, the most classical game-theoretic framework for modeling such situations is that of two-player zero-sum games.
However, when the assumption of full adversity is too restrictive, multiplayer settings can be more appropriate, incorporating relevant notions of collective rationality, typically captured by
equilibria
concepts such as Nash equilibria or subgame-perfect equilibria. In this context, the environment—potentially consisting of multiple agents—may be better represented as acting rationally according to its own objectives.

Here, we focus on another class of computer-science-related problems that can be modeled using multiplayer games:
protocol design
, where a set of agents interact according to a predefined protocol that must remain robust against deviations.
We argue that in such cases, a particularly relevant notion is that of
strong secure equilibria
.
That notion combines the well-established concepts of
strong equilibria
(resilient to deviations by coalitions) and
secure equilibria
(where no player can deviate in a way that harms another player without also harming themself). However, to the best of our knowledge, it has not been studied as a standalone concept before this work.

9.1  Motivating example and definition

The aim of designing a protocol is to establish a set of behavior rules that yield a specific outcome if thoroughly followed, and that are self-enforceable: every participating agent must adhere to these rules, lest they do not get anything out of the interactions.

Before moving on the more formal definitions and results of the paper, let us run through the obstacles and possible solutions to the design of a good fair exchange protocol.
Towards this, let us consider the simple setting of only two agents, Adélaïde and Barthélémy, that wish to exchange items online. (For instance, Adélaïde could want to purchase digital art from Barthélémy.)
A first naive approach to designing such a protocol could be “let both send their part to the other one”: see
Figure

29
for a depiction of the possible sequences of actions following this basic guideline.
If both of them do this, even asynchronously, then the exchange is performed.
Of course, there could be a malicious third party involved here, that could derail the execution of the protocol for any reason—an
external
attacker.
Another source of trouble could be the failure of the communication channels.
Here, we assume that the channels are
resilient
: they deliver every message perfectly, albeit maybe with an arbitrary but finite delay (every message is
eventually
delivered).

\begin{array}[]{rl}{\mathfrak{A}}:&\mathsf{item}_{1}\\
{\mathfrak{B}}:&\mathsf{item}_{2}\end{array}
\begin{array}[]{rl}{\mathfrak{A}}:&\mathsf{item}_{1},\mathsf{item}_{2}\\
{\mathfrak{B}}:&\emptyset\end{array}
\begin{array}[]{rl}{\mathfrak{A}}:&\emptyset\\
{\mathfrak{B}}:&\mathsf{item}_{1},\mathsf{item}_{2}\end{array}
\begin{array}[]{rl}{\mathfrak{A}}:&\mathsf{item}_{2}\\
{\mathfrak{B}}:&\mathsf{item}_{1}\end{array}
{\mathfrak{A}}
sends
\mathsf{item}_{1}
{\mathfrak{B}}
sends
\mathsf{item}_{2}
{\mathfrak{A}}
sends
\mathsf{item}_{1}
{\mathfrak{B}}
sends
\mathsf{item}_{2}

Figure 29
:
Exchange between Adélaïde and Barthélémy only.

However, another critical aspect to take into account is what assumptions we make about the motivations of the agents.
Indeed, one can argue that there is no reason for an agent to trust the other one.
In fact, both may even prefer an outcome where they receive something from the other as intended, but do not send their share.
On the other hand, the agents wish in priority to avoid being
wronged
, i.e. sending their share without receiving anything.
Depending on such assumptions, the solutions for designing a fair protocol may vary: clearly, the naive approach sketched above fails as soon as one agent is not fully trustworthy.

Let us briefly describe two approaches to ensure robustness against these possibly untrustworthy agents.

9.1.1 With a trusted third party

A first possible solution to ensure fairness while making no assumption on the trustworthiness of the participants is to involve a
trusted third party
(TTP) in the protocol.
This particular agent is assumed to be trustworthy, and to have no other interest than the completion of the exchange.
Furthermore, it is supposed to have sufficient leverage on the agents to enforce the completion of the exchange, if one of them tries to deflect from the intended course of actions.
The TTP is thus considered to be an agent with absolute
authority
.
One could consider performing the exchange only via the TTP, sending both agents’ parts to the TTP that would, upon full reception, redistribute them appropriately. This, however foolproof, is extremely costly, as the TTP is used in every instance of the protocol.
Usually, this is avoided by amending the naive protocol to add the rule “if an agent does not receive anything while they have sent their share, they contact and alert the TTP, who then makes sure the other agent complies”.
This is what is called an
optimistic
version of a protocol involving a TTP. It is assumed that the participating agents desire the intended outcome of the protocol, and thus that in most cases, the TTP will not need to intervene at all.
A depiction of a such a TTP moderated protocol exchange can be found in
Figure

30
.
This is also the approach of the Zhou-Gollmann protocol
[
ZhouGollmann97
]
that uses a TTP only in case of a (suspected or real) cheating attempt (and is encompassed by our framework).

{\mathfrak{B}}:&\mathsf{item}_{2}\end{array}
\begin{array}[]{rl}{\mathfrak{A}}:&\mathsf{item}_{1},\mathsf{item}_{2}\\
{\mathfrak{B}}:&\emptyset\end{array}

{\mathfrak{B}}:&\mathsf{item}_{1},\mathsf{item}_{2}\end{array}

:
Exchange between Adélaïde and Barthélémy with a trusted third party.

9.1.2 Beyond trust: without a trusted third party

The TTP approach is well-known and widely accepted as a necessity (as well-justified by the impossibility result of
[
pagnia1999impossibility
]
).
What can be done when this approach is not applicable?
Indeed, there are many cases where the trustworthiness of a third party is not clear enough to reasonably rely upon it.
For those cases, can we design protocols which rules are self-enforceable and that eliminate the need of a TTP?
In other terms, what if the incentives to complete the protocol properly were stronger than the ones to deviate from the intended behaviors?
An alternative, and the approach we choose in this work, is to rely, not on the trustworthiness of agents, but instead on their
rationality
.
While not amenable to every possible context where fair exchange is needed, it can provide a viable solution in relevant situations.
We will therefore consider that a protocol is safe if no alliance of agents can wrong another agent
without wronging a member of the alliance itself
.
The security of the protocol stems from the assumption that all agents, even though they might be malicious, would nonetheless behave rationally.

For instance, in peer-to-peer settings, no one can be absolutely trusted in theory, but all agents have an incentive to be honest, since keeping a good reputation enable them to perform further exchanges.
As another example, a ride-share application can be used as a trusted third party between a driver and a traveler without being trusted
per se
, but because both the driver and the traveler know that if the application had dishonest behaviors in some situations, then no one would trust it anymore, and the company that holds it would stop their profits.

In both these examples, important common features are their
multi-agent
nature and the
repeatability
of the exchange, albeit with different agents.
To illustrate further and start going towards a more abstract model, consider the following setting, depicted by Figure
31
.
Adélaïde, Barthélémy and Capucine are three agents who wish to exchange an infinity of items: each agent has infinitely many items to send to both other agents.
Again, as shown in
[
pagnia1999impossibility
]
, there is no protocol that could satisfy the usual definitions of fairness.
Indeed, there is an agent (say, for instance, Adélaïde) that can
scam
another agent (say Barthélémy), i.e. that can send only finitely many items to Barthélémy and receive at least one more item from him.
That is the case if the agents exchange their items directly, but also if they use the third agent (Capucine) as an intermediary, because Capucine is not a TTP and can therefore choose to help Adélaïde, forming a (malicious)
coalition
, to scam Barthélémy (or the reverse).

However, since the process is repeating for further exchanges, if Capucine does so, Barthélémy may react by stopping exchanges not only with Adélaïde, but also with Capucine.
Adélaïde would then be satisfied (she actually scammed Barthélémy), but Capucine would be worse off (she did not scam anyone herself, and can no longer exchange with Barthélémy).
So if Capucine behaves rationally, she has no incentive to help another agent scam the third.
In practice, therefore, such a protocol can be considered as reasonably
safe
: Adélaïde, Barthélémy, and Capucine all take, alternatively, the role of the TTP and enable an exchange of items between the two other agents; and if an agent deviates from the protocol, the other ones stop all exchanges with them.

{\mathfrak{B}}
{\mathfrak{C}}
