9.1.3 Strong secure equilibria

At this point, we hope the reader can be convinced that a relevant equilibrium notion for capturing protocols that ensure a reasonable level of safety is the following.

Definition

38

(Strong secure equilibrium)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
be a game.
Let
{\bar{\sigma}}
be a strategy profile in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
: a
harmful deviation
of the
coalition

C\subseteq\Pi
from the strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}
is a strategy profile
{\bar{\sigma}}^{\prime}_{C}\in\mathsf{Strat}_{C}\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
such that for every player
i\in C
, we have
\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-C},{\bar{\sigma}}^{\prime}_{C}\rangle\geq\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
, and for some player
i\not\in C
, we have
\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}_{-C},{\bar{\sigma}}^{\prime}_{C}\rangle<\mu_{i}\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle
.
A
strong secure equilibrium
in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, or
SSE
for short, is a strategy profile from which there is no harmful deviation.

Before moving to computational aspects, let us make two remarks to give an insight on how expressive a model based on SSEs is.
Indeed, one may believe that SSEs provide a very specific type of potential attackers—those that may participate in an attack as long as they are not wronged in the process.
But such a model can actually capture more classical types of agents, such as fully hostile ones, or on the contrary trusted ones.

•

A fully untrusted agent, on which no rationality assumptions can be made, can be modeled as an agent that is impervious to harm—i.e., one that always wins.

•

Conversely, in a protocol that includes an agent who is fully trusted and will never participate in an attack, such an agent can be modeled as a player who is wronged as soon as any other player is wronged.
As a result, if the protocol contains an attack, captured by the harmful deviation of a coalition, the trusted agent cannot be part of that coalition.

We provide a more detailed discussion in
[
DBLP:journals/corr/abs-2405-18958
]
regarding the protocol and attack models that can be captured by this notion, as well as its relevance to classical protocols such as the Zhou-Gollmann optimistic protocol
[
ZhouGollmann97
]
.
Since these security aspects diverge significantly from the game-theoretic focus of this document, we choose to concentrate here on algorithmic aspects.

9.1.4 Problem

Modeling secure protocols with strong secure equilibria is particularly relevant in Boolean games, where each player wins if and only if they are not wronged by other players.
In this setting, determining whether a given strategy profile (where every player wins) is an SSE reduces to checking whether there exists a coalition that can deviate and wrong some player without harming any of its own members.
Since such wronging conditions are typically represented by
\omega
-regular conditions (e.g., "at some point, Adélaïde sends the item
\mathsf{item}_{1}
, but Barthélémy never sends the item
\mathsf{item}_{2}
"), we restrict our study to parity games, as defined in
chapter

3
, and, more generally, to
\omega
-regular games
, a broader class defined as follows.

Definition

39

(Parity automaton)
.

A
parity automaton
over alphabet
X
is a tuple
\mathcal{A}=(X,Q,q_{0},\delta,\kappa)
where
Q
is a set of states, where
\delta:Q\times X\rightarrow Q
is a deterministic transition function, and where
\kappa:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
is a color mapping.
Given a word
w=w_{0}w_{1}\cdots\in X^{\omega}
, the
run
of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
is
\rho_{w}=q_{0}q_{1}\cdots
where for
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
q_{i+1}=\delta(q_{i},w_{i})
.
The set of infinite words accepted by
\mathcal{A}
is
\mathsf{L}(\mathcal{A})=\left\{w\in X^{\omega}\mid\minop\kappa(\mathsf{Inf}(\rho_{w}))\text{ is even}\right\}
.

Definition

40

(
\omega
-regular game)
.

An
\omega
-regular game
is a multi-player game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
such that there exists, for each player
i
, a parity automaton
\mathcal{A}_{i}
over alphabet
V
, such that for each play
\pi
, we have
\mu_{i}(\pi)=1
if and only if
\pi\in\mathsf{L}(\mathcal{A}_{i})
.

Moreover, since the primary objective in this framework is to design a protocol in which no player is wronged, we do not focus on the constrained existence problem of SSEs.
Instead, we study a variant of this problem, the
fixed-payoff SSE problem
, where we seek a strategy profile that not only produces a given payoff vector but also satisfies a specified
correctness condition
. This condition is of the same type as the players’ objectives: a color function on the vertices of the game in the case of a parity game, or a parity automaton in the case of an
\omega
-regular game.
In both cases, we denote by
\pi\vDash\varphi
the fact that the play
\pi
satisfies the condition
\varphi
.
It is worth noting that, when considering Nash equilibrium or subgame-perfect equilibrium, such a correctness condition can be encoded as the objective of a fictional player. However, this approach is not applicable here, as it may introduce harmful deviations against that player.

Problem

9

(Fixed-payoff SSE problem)
.

Given a game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, a payoff vector
\bar{x}\in\mathbb{Q}^{\Pi}
, and a condition
\varphi
, does there exist an SSE
{\bar{\sigma}}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
such that
\mu\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle=\bar{x}
and
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle\vDash\varphi
?

Algorithms for this problem can also be used to solve the constrained existence problem, at least in Boolean games (or more generally games with payoff functions ranging in a finite set), by checking each possible payoff vector
\bar{x}
between the two threshold vectors (with
\varphi=\top
).
Of course, in the general case, the number of such calls grows exponentially with the number of players. However, since the lowest complexity we will obtain in this chapter is
\mathsf{PSPACE}
, this additional cost will not be prohibitive, and our complexity results for the fixed-payoff SSE problem will immediately entail results for the constrained existence problem of SSEs.

The remainder of this chapter is therefore dedicated to establishing tight complexity bounds for the fixed-payoff SSE problem in parity games and
\omega
-regular games.
In particular, we will pay close attention to the complexities that arise when the number of players and the number of colors are small, as this is often the case in practical applications.

9.2  A tool: the deviator game

The main tool we use to solve our problem takes the form of a new game structure, in which one player,
Prover
, tries to prove that an SSE generating the desired payoff exists, while another one,
Challenger
, tries to prove that the strategy profile she constructs is actually not an SSE.
That game is the
deviator game
, very similar to a construction with the same name proposed in
[
DBLP:conf/fossacs/Brenguier16
]
, in a slightly different context.

Definition

41

(Deviator game)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
be a game, let
\bar{x}
be a payoff vector, and let
\varphi
a correctness condition.
The
deviator game
is the initialized game:

\mathsf{dev}_{\bar{x}\varphi}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}^{\mathsf{d}}}=\left(\{\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{C}\},V^{\mathsf{d}},E^{\mathsf{d}},\left(V^{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathfrak{P}},V^{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\right),\mu^{\mathsf{d}}\right)_{\upharpoonright v_{0}^{\mathsf{d}}},

where:

•

the player
\mathfrak{P}
is called
Prover
, and the player
\mathfrak{C}

Challenger
.

•

Prover controls the set
V^{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathfrak{P}}=V\times 2^{\Pi}
, and Challenger the set
V^{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathfrak{C}}=E\times 2^{\Pi}
.

•

The initial vertex is
v_{0}^{\mathsf{d}}=(v_{0},\emptyset)
.

•

The edge set
E^{\mathsf{d}}
is defined as follows: from the vertex
(u,D)
, Prover can go to every vertex of the form
(uv,D)\in E
(she
proposes
the edge
uv
).
From the vertex
(uv,D)
, Challenger can go to the vertex
(v,D)
(he
accepts
the edge
uv
), or to every vertex
(w,D\cup\{i\})
, with
w\neq v
and
uw\in E
, and where
i
is the player controlling
u
(he
deviates
from Prover’s proposal, and player
i
is added to the set of deviators).

•

Given a play
\chi
is this game, we write
\dot{\chi}
for the play in
\mathcal{G}
constructed by the actions of Prover and Challenger: if
\chi=(u_{0},D_{0})(u_{0}v_{0},D_{0})(u_{1},D_{1})(u_{1}v_{1},D_{1})\dots
, then we define
\dot{\chi}=u_{0}u_{1}\dots
.
We also define
\mathsf{D}(\chi)=\bigcup_{k}D_{k}
, the set of players who deviated along the play
\chi
.

•

Then, the Boolean payoff function
\mu^{\mathsf{d}}
is defined as follows. A play
\chi
is won by Challenger if and only if either:

–

we have
\mathsf{D}(\chi)=\emptyset
and
\mu(\dot{\chi})\neq\bar{x}
;

–

we have
\mathsf{D}(\chi)=\emptyset
and
\dot{\chi}\not\vDash\varphi
;

–

or for every player
i\in\mathsf{D}(\chi)
, we have
\mu_{i}(\dot{\chi})\geq x_{i}
, and there exists a player
j\in\Pi
such that
\mu_{j}(\dot{\chi})<x_{j}
.

Theorem

24
.

Prover has a winning strategy in the game
\mathsf{dev}_{\bar{x}\varphi}(\mathcal{G})_{\upharpoonright v_{0}^{\mathsf{d}}}
if and only if there exists an SSE
{\bar{\sigma}}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
with
\mu\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle=\bar{x}
and
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle\vDash\varphi
.

A proof of this theorem can easily be obtained by adapting the one presented in
[
DBLP:conf/fossacs/Brenguier16
]
.

9.3  Parity games

Let us now consider the case of parity games: then, similarly to what was done in
[
DBLP:conf/fossacs/Brenguier16
]
, a cautious way to solve the deviator game leads to an algorithm that uses only polynomial space.
For convenience, we now write
\mathsf{Parity}(\kappa)
for the set of plays satisfying the parity condition associated to each color function
\kappa
, and
\overline{\mathsf{Parity}}(\kappa)
for its complement.
Similarly, we write
\mathsf{B}(v)
for the set of plays that visit infinitely often the vertex
v
, and
\overline{\mathsf{B}}(v)
for its complement.
The condition
\varphi
is defined by the color mapping
\kappa_{\varphi}
.

Theorem

25
.

In parity games, the fixed-payoff SSE problem is
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-complete.
Hardness still holds in co-Büchi games and with correctness condition
\varphi=\top
.
With two players, the problem is at least as hard as solving a two-player zero-sum parity game.
If both the number of colors and the number of players are fixed, then the problem is fixed-parameter tractable.

Proof.

Given a parity game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, we write
n
for the number of players and
k
for the highest color that appears in the automata
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}
.

\blacktriangleright

\mathsf{PSPACE}
-easiness

By Theorem
24
, deciding the fixed-payoff SSE existence problem amounts to deciding which player, among Prover and Challenger, has a winning strategy in the corresponding deviator game.
That game has an exponential size, but has a specific shape that makes it possible to solve it region by region, without using exponential space.
Indeed, let us observe that along a play
\chi=(u_{0},D_{0})(u_{0}v_{0},D_{0})(u_{1},D_{1})(u_{1}v_{1},D_{1})\dots
, the sequence of sets
(D_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
is non-decreasing.

Let us therefore recursively define an algorithm that decides, given the game
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
, the vector
\bar{x}
and a vertex
(u_{0},D)
of the game
\mathsf{dev}_{\bar{x}\varphi}\mathcal{G}
, whether Prover has a winning strategy from that vertex.
Let
W=\{i\in\Pi~|~x_{i}=1\}
be the set of players that win according to the payoff vector
\bar{x}
.

Definition of the game
\mathcal{H}^{D}_{\upharpoonright(u_{0},D)}
.

First, construct the game
\mathcal{H}^{D}_{\upharpoonright(u_{0},D)}
as follows: construct the region of the game
\mathsf{dev}_{\bar{x}\varphi}\mathcal{G}
that is made of vertices of the form
(u,D)
or
(uv,D)
.
Define in that region the color mappings so that player
i
’s payoff, in that region, is always equal to their payoff in the corresponding play in
\mathcal{G}
: i.e., for all
i\in\Pi
, define
\kappa^{\prime}_{i}(uv,D)=\kappa^{\prime}_{i}(u,D)=\kappa_{i}(u)
(and similarly for the correctness condition, define
\kappa^{\prime}_{\varphi}(uv,D)=\kappa^{\prime}_{\varphi}(u,D)=\kappa_{i}(u)
).

For each edge
(uv,D)(w,D\cup\{i\})
that leaves that region, add to the constructed game the edge
(uv,D)(w,D\cup\{i\})
and the loop
(w,D\cup\{i\})(w,D\cup\{i\})
, and recursively decide whether Prover has a winning strategy from the vertex
(w,D\cup\{i\})
.
If she does, call the vertex
(w,D\cup\{i\})
a
Prover’s leaf
, and define the color mappings so that only players in
W
would win on that loop: for each
i^{\prime}\in W
, define
\kappa^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}(w,D\cup\{i\})=0
; for
i^{\prime}\notin W
, define
\kappa^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}(w,D\cup\{i\})=1
; and finally, define
\kappa^{\prime}_{\varphi}(w,D\cup\{i\})=0
.
If she does not, call the vertex
(w,D\cup\{i\})
a
Challenger’s leaf
, and define the color mappings so that only players in
D\cup\{i\}
would win on that loop: for each
i^{\prime}\in D\cup\{i\}
, define
\kappa^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}(w,D\cup\{i\})=0
; for
i\notin D\cup\{i\}
, define
\kappa^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}(w,D\cup\{i\})=1
; and finally, define
\kappa^{\prime}_{\varphi}(w,D\cup\{i\})=1
.

Now, define the payoff functions as follows.
If
D\neq\emptyset
, then a play
\pi
is won by Challenger if and only if we have:

\pi\in\left(\bigcup_{i\in W}\overline{\mathsf{Parity}}(\kappa^{\prime}_{i})\right)\cap\left(\bigcap_{i\in D\cap W}\mathsf{Parity}(\kappa^{\prime}_{i})\right)

(i.e., if the play
\pi
gives to at least one player
i
a payoff worse than
x_{i}
, and to every player who deviated at least the same payoff as in
\bar{x}
).
If
D=\emptyset
, then a play
\pi
is won by Challenger if and only if we have:

\pi\in\bigcup_{i\in W}\overline{\mathsf{Parity}}(\kappa^{\prime}_{i})\cup\bigcup_{i\notin W}\mathsf{Parity}(\kappa^{\prime}_{i})\cup\overline{\mathsf{Parity}}(\kappa_{\varphi}^{\prime})

(i.e., if the payoff vector generated by Prover, without any deviation, is not equal to
\bar{x}
, or if the correctness constraint is not met).
Note that in both cases, Challenger wins if he reaches a Challenger’s leaf, and loses if he reaches a Prover’s leaf.

Equivalence with the game
\mathsf{dev}_{\bar{x}\varphi}\mathcal{G}
.

Prover has a winning strategy in the game
\mathcal{H}^{D}_{\upharpoonright(u_{0},D)}
if and only if she has a winning strategy from the vertex
(u_{0},D)
in the game
\mathsf{dev}_{\bar{x}\varphi}\mathcal{G}
.
Indeed, if Prover has a winning strategy in
\mathcal{H}^{D}_{\upharpoonright(u_{0},D)}
, then she can follow that strategy in
\mathsf{dev}_{\bar{x}\varphi}\mathcal{G}
.
Then, she either stays in the region where the deviating coalition is
D
, or she reaches a vertex of the form
(w,D\cup\{i\})
from which she has a winning strategy, that she can then follow.
Conversely, if she has a winning strategy from
(u_{0},D)
in
\mathsf{dev}_{\bar{x}\varphi}\mathcal{G}
, she can follow it in
\mathcal{H}^{D}_{\upharpoonright(u_{0},D)}
: she will then either stay in the region where the deviating coalition is
D
, or reach a Prover’s leaf (since the strategy she is following afterwards in
\mathsf{dev}_{\bar{x}\varphi}\mathcal{G}
is still winning), which makes her win.

Resolution.

This game can be seen as an Emerson-Lei game
[
EmersonLei87
]
: Prover’s and Challenger’s winning conditions are Boolean combinations of Büchi conditions.
Indeed, we have the equality:

\mathsf{Parity}(\kappa^{\prime}_{i})=\bigcup_{2m<k}\left(\bigcup_{\kappa^{\prime}_{i}(v^{\mathsf{d}})=2m}\mathsf{B}(v^{\mathsf{d}})\cap\neg\bigcup_{\ell<2m}\bigcup_{\kappa^{\prime}_{i}(w^{\mathsf{d}})=\ell}\mathsf{B}(w^{\mathsf{d}})\right).

It was shown in
[
HunterDawar05
]
that such games can be solved using a space polynomial in the size of the game and of the objectives.
Here, both are themselves polynomial in the size of
\mathcal{G}
.
Thus, the last step of our recursive algorithm consists in solving
\mathcal{H}^{D}_{\upharpoonright(u_{0},D)}
.

Then, applying this recursive algorithm from the vertex
(v_{0},\emptyset)
decides our problem: there is an SSE
{\bar{\sigma}}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\upharpoonright v_{0}}
such that
\mu\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle=\bar{x}
and
\langle{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle\vDash\varphi
if and only if Prover has a winning strategy from that vertex.
Moreover, that algorithm uses polynomial space: each recursive call does, and the recursion stack has size at most
\mathsf{card}\Pi
, i.e. polynomial.
Therefore, the fixed-payoff SSE existence problem in parity games is
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-easy.

\blacktriangleright
Fixed-parameter tractability

In
[
BruyereHautemRaskin18
]
, it has been shown that an Emerson-Lei game
\mathcal{G}
with vertex space
S
and objective
\psi
can be solved in time:

O\left(2^{2^{|\psi|}}|\psi|+\left(2^{|\psi|2^{|\psi|}}\mathsf{card}V^{\mathsf{d}}\right)^{5}\right).

In our case, the size of the objective
\psi
depends only on
k
and
n
, while
\mathsf{card}V^{\mathsf{d}}=2^{n}\mathsf{card}V
: our problem is therefore fixed-parameter tractable with
k
and
n
as parameters.

\blacktriangleright

\mathsf{PSPACE}
-hardness (in co-Büchi games)

We reduce the quantified satisfiability problem (
QSat
) to the SSE fixed-payoff existence problem (with
\varphi=\top
) in co-Büchi games (or more precisely to its complement).
This will prove that our problem cannot be fixed-parameter tractable with
k
as a single parameter, unless we have
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{PSPACE}
.

Reduction.

Let:

\varphi=\exists x_{1}\forall x_{2}\cdots\exists x_{n-1}\forall x_{n}\bigwedge_{i=1}^{p}\bigvee_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\ell_{ij}

be an instance of
QSat
in conjunctive normal form where for all
i,j
, we define
\ell_{ij}
as a literal of the form
x_{k}
or
\neg x_{k}
for some
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
We write
\psi^{\prime}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{p}\bigvee_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\ell_{ij}
for the non-quantified part of the formula.

We construct a game with
2n+2
players: one for each literal player and two additional players
Solver
(written
\mathfrak{S}
) and
Opponent
(written
{\mathfrak{O}}
) as depicted in Figure
.
Round vertices belong to Solver, hexagonal vertices belong to Opponent, rectangular vertices belong to the literal indicated in the bottom right corner (if any: some vertices only have a single outgoing edge and therefore could belong to anyone without loss of generality).
Red clouds indicate the list of players for which a given vertex is in the co-Büchi set: infinitely many visits to these vertices means losing (payoff
0
) while only finitely many visits means winning (payoff
1
).

The game is composed of three parts: in the first part, the
setting
module, a valuation can be chosen by visiting the vertex
x_{i}^{\mathsf{s}}
(setting
x_{i}
to true) or the vertex
\neg x_{i}^{\mathsf{s}}
(setting
x_{i}
to false), the choice belonging to Solver if
x_{i}
is existentially quantified and to Opponent if it is universally quantified.
When the value of a variable is chosen, the corresponding literal player can choose to go to a sink vertex
\triangledown
(duplicated in the figure for clarity) or continue the game.

If all choose to continue, then the game moves into the second part, the
checking
module, where the formula is “checked”: each disjunctive clause
C_{i}
has a vertex owned by Solver who must choose a literal
\ell_{ij}
from the clause.
Vertex
\ell_{ij}
is in the co-Büchi set of player
\bar{\ell}_{ij}
, who loses if said vertex was visited infinitely often.
When this “checking” is done, the last part, called
punishing
module, visits the co-Büchi set of one literal per variable, the choice of which being left to Solver.

The game continues back to the checking module.
As the last vertex of these modules is in the co-Büchi set of Opponent, Opponent will lose if the game enters these modules.
Remark that there are no co-Büchi vertices for Solver who therefore always wins.

We will now show that the formula
\varphi
holds if and only if there is no SSE with payoff vector
(1)_{i\in\Pi}
.
The intuition is that a coalition made of Solver and all true literals can deviate to make Opponent and all false literals lose while not penalizing the players in the coalition.

If the formula
\psi
does not hold, then there exists an SSE generating the payoff vector
(1)_{i\in\Pi}
.

Let us assume
\varphi
does not hold.
Let
{\bar{\sigma}}
be the following profile of strategies: each literal goes to
\triangledown
whenever possible in the setting module, Solver plays any strategy, and Opponent plays the optimal strategy in response to Solver’s choice of valuation so that
\psi^{\prime}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is false.
The outcome of this profile is either path
?x_{1}\cdot x_{1}^{\mathsf{s}}\cdot\triangledown^{\omega}
or
?x_{1}\cdot\neg x_{1}^{\mathsf{s}}\cdot\triangledown^{\omega}
, both with payoff vector
(1)_{i\in\Pi}
.

Let us prove that it is an SSE.
Let
C
be a coalition of players and assume that there exists a harmful deviation for this coalition.
As Solver always wins, she can be assumed to be in the coalition.
And since no one loses in the setting module or
\triangledown
, any harmful deviation must reach the checking module.
Because that means that Opponent would then lose, Opponent cannot be in the coalition.
For each variable
x_{i}
, players
x_{i}
and
\neg x_{i}
cannot be both in the coalition as (at least) one of them loses through the infinite visits in the punishing module.
To actually reach the checking module, all literal players whose vertex was visited in the setting module must have deviated from
{\bar{\sigma}}
, and therefore are in the coalition.
As a result the coalition is made of Solver and exactly one literal per variable, defining a valuation.
Because Opponent played optimally, that valuation does not satisfy
\psi^{\prime}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, so there is a clause
C_{i}
where all literals
\ell_{i1},\dots,\ell_{im_{i}}
are false, meaning that the player corresponding to their negations are all in the coalition.
The choice by Solver of any of these literals therefore enforces a visit to the co-Büchi set of (at least) one player in the coalition.
As this choice is infinitely repeated, that makes this player lose and therefore it should not be part of the coalition, which is a contradiction.
So
{\bar{\sigma}}
is an SSE.

If the formula
\psi
holds, then there is no such SSE.

Let us assume that
\varphi
holds.
Let
{\bar{\sigma}}
be a strategy profile with payoff
(1)_{i\in\Pi}
.
As noted above, that payoff requires the outcome of
{\bar{\sigma}}
to end in the vertex
\triangledown
.
Consider the following strategy for Solver: in the setting module, choose the literal that ensures satisfaction of the formula
\psi^{\prime}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, based on the already known choices of Opponent.
This is possible since the formula
\varphi
holds.
Let
C
be the coalition made of Solver and all literal players corresponding to vertices
x_{i}^{\mathsf{s}}
or
\neg x_{i}^{\mathsf{s}}
visited in the setting module.
These players deviate by reaching the vertex
?x_{i+1}
(or
C_{1}
if
i=n
) instead of going to
\triangledown
.
Since the checking module is reached, Opponent (at least) will lose and be harmed by the deviation.
The valuation thus built ensures that
\psi^{\prime}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is satisfied, so in the checking module, for every clause there is a true literal.
The strategy of Solver consists in consistently choosing the vertex for these literals.
Note that this means visiting a co-Büchi set for a player that is not in the coalition.
In the punishing module, Solver visits vertices corresponding to players in the coalition (i.e. the exact same one that were visited in the setting module: if
x_{i}^{\mathsf{s}}
was visited, consistently choose
x_{i}^{\mathsf{p}}
, if
\neg x_{i}^{\mathsf{s}}
was visited, consistently choose
\neg x_{i}^{\mathsf{p}}
.
This ensures that the co-Büchi set of no player in the coalition
C
is ever visited, hence all players in this coalition still win, hence the deviation is harmful and
{\bar{\sigma}}
is not an SSE.

?x_{1}
x_{1}^{\mathsf{s}}
x_{1}
\neg x_{1}^{\mathsf{s}}
\neg x_{1}
?x_{2}
x_{2}^{\mathsf{s}}
x_{2}
\neg x_{2}^{\mathsf{s}}
\neg x_{2}
\cdots
x_{n}^{\mathsf{s}}
x_{n}
\neg x_{n}^{\mathsf{s}}
\neg x_{n}

C_{1}
\ell_{1,1}
\neg\ell_{1,1}
\ell_{1,m_{1}}
\neg\ell_{1,m_{1}}

.

.

.

C_{2}
\mathinner{\mkern 1.0mu\raise-6.0pt\vbox{\hbox{.}}\mkern 2.0mu\raise-3.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 1.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 1.0mu}
\mathinner{\mkern 1.0mu\raise 7.0pt\vbox{\hbox{.}}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 1.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 1.0mu}

.

.

.

\mathinner{\mkern 1.0mu\raise 7.0pt\vbox{\hbox{.}}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 1.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 1.0mu}
\mathinner{\mkern 1.0mu\raise-6.0pt\vbox{\hbox{.}}\mkern 2.0mu\raise-3.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 1.0pt\hbox{.}\mkern 1.0mu}

.

.

.

\cdots

x_{1}?
x_{1}^{\mathsf{p}}
\neg x_{1}
\neg x_{1}^{\mathsf{p}}
x_{1}
x_{2}?
x_{2}^{\mathsf{p}}
\neg x_{2}
\neg x_{2}^{\mathsf{p}}
x_{2}
\cdots
x_{n}^{\mathsf{p}}
